PART ONE

A FAMILY ILLNESS

CHAPTER ONE

THE DESERT

“Once upon a time there were three little sisters,”

the Dormouse began in a great hurry; “and their

names were Elsie, Lacie, and Tillie; and they

lived at the bottom of a well—”

“What did they live on?” said Alice, who always

took a great interest in questions of eating and drinking.

“They lived on treacle,” said the Dormouse, after

thinking a minute or two.

“They couldn’t have done that, you know,” Alice

gently remarked. “They’d have been ill.”

“So they were,” said the Dormouse; “very ill.”

—Lewis Carroll

The three of them were crossing a desert of white sand. They’d been crossing it for as long as they could remember. Today they listened to the wind as they traveled. The wind hadn’t let up all day. It carved ridges in the sand, like isobars on a barometric pressure map.

Two of the suns had set, but one remained in the western sky. It baked the heaps of boulders and the lichens that grew on them. Three sets of tracks stretched across the plain, wavering under a shimmering ocean of air. There were human tracks, tire tracks, and the prints of some huge beast.

The brontosaur’s head hung close to the white sand, swinging to the left and the right on her leathery gray neck. She was forty feet long and fifteen feet tall at the hips. She limped arthritically. Her joints were killing her.

The jeep drove along behind her on its four massive wheels on their independent axles. The glassy black panels of its photovoltaic collectors were angled out above its back to catch the last of the day’s light. Its drive chain felt sluggish. Its turbines whined, complaining of the heat.

The old Mexican woman walked behind the jeep. Her skin was the color of terra cotta, and her hair was as white as snow. She wore canvas shoes and drab green coveralls with many zippered pockets. Over her eyes, she wore tinted glasses with orange lenses, rims of copper wire, and side screens of copper mesh.

Her name was Eva, and she was more than half mad. Some days she walked in small circles for hours, twisting up her hair and talking to herself. When she did, Alex and Naomi would have to wait for her to snap out of it. Alex was the jeep. Naomi was the brontosaur.

They were all incredibly ancient. Their earliest memories dated back to the Twentieth Century. Alex claimed to be the eldest. On the face of things, you’d expect a brontosaur to be older than a jeep, but Naomi agreed with Alex and maintained that she, Naomi, was the youngest.

None of them seriously believed the stories they told themselves about themselves. They’d forgotten where they came from. They also didn’t know where they were. The desert crossing had been going on this way for centuries.

Triads, Alex thought to himself. I need more triads. Animal vegetable mineral, done that. One had her heart cut out. One got locked in a freezer. One gnawed off his own paw. Hah!

Naomi swung her muzzle toward the old woman. What is Father talking about? she asked.

Don’t ask me! Eva told her. How the fuck would I know? Ask him yourself, you fucking pinhead.

Yes, Mother, thought Naomi.

She’s in a snit, put in Alex.

She’s always in a snit, thought Naomi. She should be put to sleep.

Painlessly? asked Alex. Or after torture? Naomi chewed a ball of cud and considered the question.

The wind whispered over the ridges of the white sand. Alex drove around Naomi and took the lead. Eva went on walking behind the others. It gave her something to look at, other than sand patterns, sandstone outcrops, and lichens. The last of the suns was slipping down behind a mesa. Soon the air would cool off. Then the three of them would stop for the night.

The behavior of the three suns was erratic. One of them was liable to rise while another was setting, while the third hovered at high noon for days on end. At other times they would come close to synchronizing. There seemed to be little pattern in their movements. There seemed to be only confusion.

Eva stumbled over a rock and looked down. Every stone had its own spattering of lichen—burnt orange or pale aqua.

The brontosaur marched on in the failing light, tears leaking slowly from her eyes, suffering the stiffness of her joints. Years of exposure to sun and wind had weathered her sleek hide into rutted tree bark that was stretched like a rickety tent over her spine and ribs. She swallowed her cud and winced from a pain in her hips, pulling her whiskery muzzle back from crooked brown teeth and shrunken purple gums.

Inside the jeep, Alex freeze-framed his optical input of Naomi’s grimace and compared it to a reference still from twenty years earlier. Yes, those gums were definitely receding. The ancient beast had never looked worse. She was a ruined specimen. And with Alex’s luck, he’d wake up tomorrow inside her head. What a life!

In Alex’s view, they must all have gone mad. For whatever reasons, they were now insane. From the heat, or from the cold, or from the isolation or the crowding or the storms. Probably from the storms. What the storms did would drive anyone mad.

The sand whispered past beneath his tire treads. The wind sucked at his solar panels, which were folded flat to his back now, like glossy black beetle wings. His turbines droned like a jar of cicadas. As he bounced across some limestone, his tires scraped their wheel wells. His shock absorbers were thoroughly shot.

Alex had a theory that he’d been born as a man, a fragile construction of bone and jelly. In the laminated circuitry of his mind, he would come across disconnected snatches of the man’s life, like scissored clips from some grainy old newsreel. Alex wondered what had become of that man.

Working from his freeze-frame of Naomi, he traced the contours of her head into his file of mechanical drawings, white on a field of blue. He simplified her saurian anatomy into volumes of solid geometry—direct view, side view, overhead. Alex enjoyed building scale models in his brain. It was one of his hobbies. But it was hard for him to concentrate with Eva babbling to herself.

I can’t stand any more of this, she told herself. It never ends. It won’t leave us alone. He must be mad. No he can’t be mad. He’s dead. Machines don’t go mad. And Naomi isn’t mad, she’s just a retard. So I must be the mad one. It must be my fault. Oh, Naomi! If only we could be rid of him at last. You and I could be so happy. But there’s nothing I can do. He said he would kill me, the bastard. He promised me, Naomi. He promised. Just a lot of big talk.

Eva lengthened her stride, overtook the brontosaur, and walked close behind the jeep, feeling the sand in her shoes. Naomi cocked one eye at her. Eva was shouting at Alex in her head.

Shift it to third, Shithead! You’re fucking up the transmission! Shift into third!

Go get fucked, thought Alex. Don’t mind me. I’ll wait until you’re done. Please. Climb on your pet lizard, squirm around, make noise, whatever it takes. In essence, Eva, in brief—go fuck yourself.

She’s a better fuck than you’ll ever be, thought Eva.

The jeep braked its wheels abruptly. Eva, walking behind it, stumbled into its rear bumper. It revved its rotors, shifted into reverse, and charged at her. She scrambled sideways before it could pin her under a tire. Alex laughed and rolled off in a cloud of white dust.

Naomi could feel her mother’s anger constricting the veins inside her own skull. Please, Mother. Please, Father. Please don’t quarrel. A storm is on its way. Let’s just be quiet and keep moving.

Naomi was correct. A storm was moving toward them across the desert. A storm had caught up with them every day around sundown for several days running. The storms were always painful. When Alex and Eva were fighting, they were especially painful.

None of the three knew where the storms came from. All they had were competing theories. They didn’t know who sent the storms, nor how the storms could do what they did. They knew that they were crossing a desert and had been for centuries. What they would find on the far side of the desert, they couldn’t say.

They could have covered more distance in a day if the woman would have consented to riding on the jeep or on the brontosaur. But she chose to walk, because riding all day put her in a stupor, and then she didn’t sleep well and didn’t dream. When she didn’t dream, her dreamlessness would inevitably attract a storm. And the storms kept getting worse. Just knowing that they were waiting out there made Eva want to die. The storms were torture devices. But where was the torturer hiding?

Who made their deaths impossible? Who reversed every lethal decision? Who vetoed their suicides? Eva had died more times than she could count on this desert crossing. There was the time she’d thrown herself off a mountain, the time when Alex broke her neck, the time when Naomi had rolled over and suffocated her. None of it had made any difference. The next storm would hit, and in the aftermath Eva would wake up in perfect repair. Who made all these repairs? Who pulled the strings that yanked her from her brain? Who forced her to play musical chairs for a body to live in? Eva wondered.

The first of the stars were out. The wind was picking up.

Naomi lifted her neck and saw a gully up ahead, sunk into the rock, out of the wind. It looked like a good place to spend the night. Naomi would curl up and chew the rocks out of her feet. Then she would piss into Alex’s distilling unit and feed Eva some cud. Then they would all try to sleep. Perhaps the storm would pass them by today.

Alex muttered to himself. His speaker volume was at zero, but the other two still heard him. Just like her to tell me how to drive. I’ll teach her the proper respect. Filthy little bitch! Putrid little bitch fucker! Disgusting little cunt-sucking bitch fucker!

Is he jacking off in there? asked Naomi.

He’s probably trying to, answered Eva. Just ignore him.

Father isn’t right in the head, is he, Mother?

No, he’s not, Dear. That’s it exactly.

Do we have to keep going? asked Naomi.

Tomorrow, answered Eva. Tomorrow we’ll start again.

Will we ever get across?

I don’t know.

Will you tell me when we get there?

Yes, Naomi. I’ll tell you.

Down in the gully, the wind made a hollow sound. Eva sat down and disentangled the hooks of her sunglasses from her hair. She held the glasses in her hands and stared at them. The sunlight struck a red glow from the copper wire. She folded the glasses and tucked them in a pocket. She stuck her hands into the armpits of her coveralls and felt the damp and the heat of her body. She made her hands into fists.

If I were a dinosaur like Naomi, she told herself, I would crush Alex like a small metal bug.

But Eva had been a brontosaur, only last week. Before the last storm or the storm before that. She had been a brontosaur. She should have crushed him while she had the chance.

Before the last storm or the storm before that. Or were they all the same storm, one storm that followed them around? What sort of country were they moving across a week ago? Eva tried to remember. Fields of cracked mud. Miles of dusty mud with jagged fissures like the crooked honeycombs of a million melted beehives.

Today she was inside the woman, where she’d begun. And Naomi, she realized, was also in her natural form. They were all in their rightful places today. Eva hadn’t noticed it before.

Down in the gully, there was a floor of loose sand between two walls of rock. The wind gusted past, over their heads. Eva hung her head and gazed at the sand between her thighs. She rubbed her neck. No one spoke.

Naomi raised her head into the wind and grazed at the lichens. Her long rough tongue scraped them right out of the crevices.

Eva walked around the gully peeling dead lichens from stones with her pocket knife. She collected a heap of brown scraps and dug a trench in the sand for a fire.

Alex parked on the plateau, near the brink of the gully. The stars were bright. There wasn’t a cloud in the sky.

Suddenly they all knew that a storm was closing in.

“Oh shit!” said Alex.

They did what they always did. They put their heads together. It was the only way they knew to make the storms less harrowing.

Eva climbed from the gully and ran to the jeep. She lay across its hood and pressed one cheek to the windshield. Her coveralls felt clammy in the cold air, but the steel beneath her was warm and dry. She held onto an air scoop. Her hands were shaking. She felt Naomi’s head nestling down beside her, Naomi’s humid breath flowing around her. The brontosaur’s great gray body curled around the jeep. Its tail looped them and settled to the sand, as silent as a cloudbank. Naomi’s bulk muffled the wind.

Alex started hissing at Eva. Why don’t you cry? Aren’t you afraid? You are! You’re falling apart! Do you think we care?

Oh shut up! groaned Naomi. Can’t you both just shut up?

I hope that the storm drags you off to the moon and puts you down in a rock, the jeep droned on. Then you can cry all you want, and no one will have to listen to you.

Naomi closed her eyes. Nictating membranes slid across glossy green orbs as big as melons. Gatherings of wrinkled leather enfolded them. The three travelers waited for the storm to pounce. The closer together they huddled, the less would be the pain. Otherwise they’d have run off in three directions screaming long ago. It was the storms that held them together.

The wind swept past, neither louder nor softer than before. They were invaded by a premonition of crowding. Naomi’s eyes flew open, bulging and wild. She twisted her head and saw thunderclouds in the sky, glimpsed figures moving inside their bruised underbellies. The radiant figures floated on white wings. Electrical halos played around their heads.

“Angels!” Naomi cried out. “I see angels in the clouds!”

“Calm down,” shouted Eva.

The storm sucked them up in a howling black funnel.

Their souls were three spiny starfish that scratched and scraped at the sutures of a single skull. They wept, they wailed, they tore their hair, they begged for rescue, they bled, they ate their hearts out, they lost their minds. They ran out of tears, they ran out of breath, they ran out of blood. But the tumor in Naomi’s head kept bleeding anyhow, and she couldn’t bite it, she couldn’t pull it out with her teeth.

They tumbled and spun—claws, arms, wheels, wires, leather, flesh, Eva, Alex, Naomi—uprooted from themselves in some inconceivable direction and then allowed to fall. Down they fell, without jaws, without axles, without hands, clutching at the air.

Naomi cracked open one eye. The eye was on the left side of her head, at the same level as her nostrils. She was back in the brontosaur.

Eva opened her eyes—first one eye, then two, then four. Her batteries felt weak. She shut off her headlights.

Alex woke up in the old Mexican woman, gasping and trembling. His head felt like a smashed gourd. He covered his face with his hands. “I can’t take much more of this,” he told the others.

But he didn’t have much choice.

All of the stars were out. They weren’t the stars of Earth. They were too many and too bright. The jeep’s metal shell contracted in the chill of the evening, breaking the stillness with an occasional ping or ponk.

Naomi stretched herself, arching her back like a cat. She walked to the mouth of the gully, reciting sutras under her breath.

Alex climbed down from the hood of the jeep. He knelt on the white rock, and his long white hair blew across his face. He began to weep.

Eva charged her magnetos and spun her rotors. It was a nice night for a drive, but her batteries were too low. There was nothing to do but sleep.

Sleep, thought Alex. What a disgusting habit.

I’m still awake, thought Naomi.

Go to sleep, Eva told her.

I can’t, thought Naomi. Father is mean and nasty and won’t tell a story.

Tell her a story, thought Eva. Help us get to sleep.

I don’t know any.

Make something up, thought Naomi. Tell us about the factory.

I never worked at a factory.

Of course you did, though Naomi. You dream about it all the time. Please don’t be so mean. Tell us a story.

So he did. And as he did, Naomi dropped off. After she was asleep, she couldn’t be sure, as she listened to him, whether Alex was telling about something that had happened to him, or something he’d dreamed, or whether he was actually dreaming and talking in his sleep, or whether she, Naomi, was dreaming the whole thing.

CHAPTER TWO

TRADE-INS

And when he came to observe his feet,

Formerly garnished with toes so neat,

His face at once became forlorn,

On perceiving that all his toes were gone.

—Edward Lear

A story? You want a story? I’m crawling with stories. They slide in and out of me like pinworms. I’m like some long-winded war veteran with a story for each of his missing limbs. I’m like a pill bug on its back, bristling with amputations and waving my long lost legs while silicon chips coagulate in my thick black blood. My brain is clotted with stories.

Once I had hands. Two flesh-and-blood human hands. Shall I tell you how I got rid of them?

I’ll tell you about the night when I got rid of my left hand. I was a man in my twenties named Alex. I probably had a surname as well. People do. I was living in Chicago and working at a factory that made model kits out of styrene.

I worked in the warehouse, the graveyard shift, midnight till eight in the morning. A third of each paycheck went for withholding tax, another third to the union, and the rest you could take home for rent.

I worked pulling cardboard boxes off of steel shelves that were three stories tall, and loading the boxes onto trucks. I’d walk to work on warm summer nights. I’d eat a breakfast at an all-night diner. Coffee and eggs, toast and jelly. The Twentieth Century. Who could forget it?

I was working at the warehouse one night, stapling boxes on the staple press. I took flat cardboard forms, one by one, yanked them open, folded in the bottom flaps, and stapled the flaps together on the press. When you stepped on the floor pedal, a pneumatic armature like a mallet with fangs would slam down on a pedestal and leave behind one bright copper staple.

Already one of my hands was steel and plastic. I’d got rid of my flesh-and-blood right hand about a year earlier. The accident had happened right there at that factory. Then I'd been transferred from Production to Warehousing. I’d had a Mec-Hand One Ninety mounted on my stump. It was crude but sturdy. Then I’d traded in the Mec-Hand for an Ortho-Hand Four Seventy, which was what I was wearing that summer.

The problem with my hands that summer was that I wasn’t balanced. My right hand weighed more than the left. I could feel myself becoming lopsided like a fiddler crab. I needed more surgery.

The warehouse smelled of dust and forklift exhaust and hard rubber wheels. I stood at the staple press, asleep on my feet, holding onto cardboard boxes while the press squirted stale air in my face. Was I really that man, all soft and pink? He hardly seems possible.

Vera walked up to the staple press. Vera was a Mexican woman that I worked with. She always wore khaki dungarees and a red bandanna, and she always had a blaring radio hung on her belt.

“Earth to Alex,” she said. “Earth to Alex. Do you copy?”

“Would you please turn down that radio?” I asked her.

“No. What are you doing?”

“Stapling boxes. What are you doing?”

“Annoying you.”

“Vera, what can I do for you?”

“Raise your right hand,” she told me. Like an idiot I held up my hand. Vera pulled an order manifest out of her shirt pocket—white copy, yellow copy, and pink copy, all neatly folded into a wad. She stuck the paper into my hand.

“The foreman,” she told me, “says to pull all that shit before the shift ends. Urgently needed last week.”

“Did the foreman pick me for this assignment, or did you?”

“You were picked for your astounding expertise. You’re the only man alive who could pull this shipment. Now excuse me. I have a heavy date with a forklift.” Vera walked away with her red bandanna and her Mexican polkas and left me with my order manifest.

I threw one last stapled box on the pile and went to look for the first item of the order. Catalog number Q-14738. Quantity: One. Product description: Super-Shark Strato-Jet With Glow Decals. The snag here was that five digit catalog number. I had no idea where to look for it on the shelves. Anyone else would’ve skipped it and come back to it. But not me.

I wandered down aisles that were as long as city blocks. Some of the aisles were blocked with logjams of pallets. Sometimes I had to climb over tall stacks of shipping boxes.

Q-14738, Q-14738, Super-Shark Strato-Jet. Where were the little fuckers? Solar-Propelled Kite-Ship With Shuttle Cruiser. Con-Shelf Scuba-Sled, Model T Ford, Reptiles of the World. . . Boxes. Walls of boxes.

I inched sideways between a stacked skid and one of the steel horizontals of the shelf racks. I was reading the inventory tags taped to the bottom shelf. Barely room to move, and barely light enough to see the pencil scrawls on the tags. Q-14738, Q-14738. Piss on it. It had to be a misprint.

My neck was stiff as a board. I rolled my head around, trying to work out the kinks. I listened to the air conditioner.

If an earthquake should happen at this moment, it occurred to me, I would be buried under ten tons of model kits. I used to think about sudden earthquakes. It was a game I played in my head. Sometimes I’d let my mind wander to other places, other times.

At this impasse, for example, my mind wandered back to the day when I was fitted for my Ortho-Hand.

I rode a bus downtown to my prosthetics outlet. My Mec-Hand was due for some routine maintenance. I walked into the carpeted showroom. Glass doors shut behind me. The showroom was hushed and elegant. Row upon row of stylish human parts rested on Lucite display shelves, cushioned in blocks of Styrofoam. Here a dainty Asian foot. There a rugged African knee. And inside glass cabinets, a wide assortment of fingers, toes, and genitalia. I seemed to be alone.

But soon enough the service staff had me laid out on a black vinyl couch in a screened cubicle. They gathered around in their white smocks and rubber aprons and went over my Mec-Hand with lenses, calipers, and diagnostic electrodes.

“So tell me,” said one of the technos, an old guy with bifocals, “how’s this little baby holding up? Any problems?”

“Well,” I said, “there’s a little dent where I shut a drawer on it.”

He bent the Mec-Hand pinky back and forth. “That’s nothing. We’ll run some tests, open her up for lubrication, have you out of here in ten minutes.”

Sooner or later I’d have to tell them my real reason for being there.

“I want to trade this one in for an Ortho,” I told them. All the technos stopped what they were doing and stared at me.

“You realize,” said the old guy, “that a trade-in would involve another course of elective surgery. More surgery, more physical therapy, more calibrations. . . You’ve already been through that wringer.”

“So I guess I must know what I’m getting into,” I pointed out.

“Sure you do. But the corporate insurance won’t cover you for this.”

“I can afford it. I think the Ortho would be more useful to my career. I read a brochure about them.” The old guy seemed embarrassed.

“You do mount Orthos, don’t you?” I asked him. “I saw some in the showroom.”

In the end, they dealt with me in the way that most people deal with a pushy nut case. They let me have my way. When a nut case gets an idea into his head, no one can talk him out of it. They carved the Mec-Hand off my stump that very day.

“This may hurt,” said the old guy.

The Mec-Hand made cringing movements when the technos detached it. Its hydraulic tubes squealed as they were cut, and leaked noxious fluids on the workbench. I was glad to see the last of it.

“We’ll find a good home for it,” said the old guy, snapping off his gloves.

I scratched my wrist. It always itched when its wiring was exposed.

Who can refuse a man in search of self-improvement?

Where was I?

Q-14738. The inventory tag was staring me in the face, dangling from the bottom shelf across the aisle. Son of a bitch.

The Q-14738s were right above me, according to the tag. But I needed a clear approach for the rolling staircase. I needed for a forklift to clear the aisle. I crab-walked out of the narrow channel that I was in. I hollered for Vera. Vera was hot shit in a forklift. She could handle that thing like it was a part of her, weasel it into the tightest spaces, spear some crooked top-heavy pallet, then rocket into reverse and never drop a box.

She and her lift rumbled into the warehouse with a skid of 2019s hot off the molds.

“Hey, Vera. I need some help.”

“Relax,” she yelled back. “First I got to put this away.” Which she did.

I explained my difficulty, retired to the mailing table, and made myself a cup of cold coffee. By the time I got back to my aisle, it was emptied.

I turned over some boxes and determined that there was no skid of Q-14738s on the floor of the first shelf. I fetched a stepladder and checked the second shelf. No such luck. I dragged the rolling staircase to the spot, walked up it, and read the stencil labels on the third tier. Not a Q-14738 in sight. I had to go higher.

I wasn’t looking forward to this project. I didn’t like high places. I had a strong aversion to heights. I’d rented a third floor apartment, but I had cardboard over all the windows. I’ve been rebuilt from the ground up, several times, by experts, but at no time have I reacted well to heights. But I had my manifest. My course was clear. I yelled for Vera. She rolled up the aisle on her yellow lift truck and parked in front of my shoes, blowing propane fumes in my face.

“I need a lift,” I told her.

“You look terrible,” she said. “You should take a break.”

“I have to look at the top three shelves,” I told her.

“No problem. Hop on.”

I looked around for a free skid. “Let me find a skid to put on the fork.”

“What a sissy,” said Vera. “I won’t let you fall.”

There was one free skid, but a jack was stuck into it, and the boards were all warped to hell. The more I wrestled with the jack handle, the more stuck it got. I gave up and stepped onto the prongs of the lift truck’s fork.

“Going up,” said Vera sweetly. The hoist motor whirred, the hoist chain clanked, and the concrete floor fell away beneath me. The lower three shelves sank past. The vibration of the fork moved up through my feet and my knees. The boxes on the fourth tier weren’t Q-14738s. Nor were the boxes on the fifth.

“Slower,” I called down.

Vera slowed down the hoist. “I can’t hear you,” she shouted. The fork stopped with a jerk. I threw out my arms to get my balance.

“Don’t look down,” Vera sang up.

The roof of the warehouse was a yard above my head. At my elbow, fluorescent tubes hummed in dusty tin hoods. On the topmost shelf, there was one half-filled skid of 3025s. No joy.

Sharing the skid of 3025s was one oddly shaped box. I couldn’t see the odd box’s label. It might be full of Q-14738s. I’d just have to take a look.

I’ve always been methodical. Methodical to a fault, that’s me in a nutshell. Methodical beyond all the limits of sanity.

The slats looked strong enough to hold my weight. I braced one hand on a steel pillar, ducked my head, and stepped off the fork onto the skid. It was cozy in the shelves, like a cave in the side of a cliff. I heard a box of 3025s crash on the concrete three stories below. I must have kicked it over.

“Are you trying to kill me?” shouted Vera.

“I’m thinking about it very seriously,” I shouted down.

“Yeah, that’s your problem, Al. You’re too damned serious.”

I lay down on my side and rotated the mystery box, looking for a stencil. The box wasn’t stenciled on any of its sides. I jammed aluminum-reinforced fingers under a top flap and ripped it off its staples. Then I turned the box around and tore up the opposite flap. Fuck the box! I had to look at the cartons in the box. Down at floor level, Vera was talking to me.

“Alex,” she said, “can I ask you about something personal?”

“No,” I yelled down at her. “Absolutely not. I’m very busy.”

I snatched a model kit out of the box and glared at the lid graphic. The name and number were there at the corner. Lunar Pogo Craft #3031. This just wasn’t my day.

“—when or if ever you’re going to ask me out,” Vera was saying. “On a date, you know? I’m just asking because it’s obvious that you like me. I mean, if you didn’t, you wouldn’t get so irritated with me, right?”

“No,” I told her. “I’m too old for you. I don’t go on dates. I’m too busy.”

I had no strength to argue with her. I rolled onto my back and pulled my cigarettes out of my shirt pocket.

“Hey, Al?” said Vera. “You still up there? I’m going to knock off for the day. Like right now. Okay?”

“Would it be okay if I drop a box on your head?”

“Hey,” said Vera. “I don’t have to sit here and listen to threats.” With this, she fired up her lift motor and reversed up the aisle.

When the rumble of her wheels died away, I got a cigarette lit and settled back with my head against the box of 3031s. I wondered how I was going to get down from there.

Strange how the mind drifts away from its immediate problems. Stranded up there, I found myself recollecting the night when I’d lost my original right hand, the one I was born with. I remembered everything about that night. It was a classic industrial accident. Man meets machine. Crunch.

I was working the graveyard shift at the Production Department, in an old part of the factory with grimy brick floors and cranking skylights. My job was mixing up barrels of polystyrene granules and feeding them into the hoppers of the injection molders.

Rubber conveyer belts carried endless processions of delicate plastic twigs. Leafing out from the twigs were model parts. There were aircraft fuselage sections and exhaust manifolds. There were landing gear and observation bubbles and halves of pistons with tiny pins to fit in tiny sockets. The other wage slaves sat on stools, packing twigs and instruction sheets into cartons.

But the machine that did in my hand wasn’t one of those big injection molders. That particular machine was a compression molder that stood in a dim gallery of the factory with windows on Wrightwood Avenue. The whole thing was maybe four feet on a side, but tall. Each molding cycle lasted over two minutes, and the press hissed and shook when it finally ejected a part down its chute. My job was scooping flaky yellow Bakelite into its hopper.

So it was two in the morning, and I was topping up the hopper on this particular machine. I glanced at the collection bin to see whether the parts looked okay. The parts were heads for the pilots of Mega-Manbot Water Striders. Crash helmet, goggles, resolute chin. Hundreds of goggles looked back at me from the bin. Hundreds of resolute cleft chins.

I checked the molder’s gauge panel. The needle for the mold thermometer was in the green. Too low. I looked closely at the mold. The upper and lower halves weren’t locking. Those compression molders were a real nuisance.

Rattling loudly, the press lifted the mold’s top half. Then I could see the problem. Three pilot heads pancaked one on top of another, mashed flat. The ejection hoses hissed at them in vain. But for a minute or so, until the mold closed again, I had access.

I pulled my jackknife from my back pocket and poked at the jammed pilot heads. I got the near side of the pancake pried up easily. But to loosen the far side, I had to reverse my grip on the knife, brace my left hand on the molder’s casing, and lean a little further forward.

I should have noticed the mold closing. I must not have been getting enough sleep.

The first thing I noticed was the smell. Like frying sausages. Then I saw the closed mold on the end of my arm, where my hand was supposed to be.

I thought to myself, when did that happen? Only then did I hear the sound of the mold’s closing. A dull thud, a cracking of small bones, and a rush of escaping steam. I heard all that after it happened.

The worst part was that before I could pass out, I had to wait for the mold to open again. If I passed out with the mold still closed, I would have to fall off my wrist, that is, I would have to rip my arm off the edge of my hand, a thing I wished to prevent, and did not wish to see. To avoid seeing it, I would have had to close my eyes, a thing which I greatly feared to do, because of my certainty that if I did close my eyes, my hand would start to hurt, a thing I did not wish to feel. So I stood, and I waited.

My throat tasted of burnt plastic. In the collection bin, hundreds of miniature yellow faces looked through hundreds of goggles in hundreds of directions. I wondered who would keep the hoppers filled when I was riding off in an ambulance.

After the mold opened, I lay on my back on the red bricks and stared at the roof. I did not want to look at my arm. Eventually someone noticed me and set off the fire alarm. A mangling isn’t exactly a fire, but I could understand the thinking. All the sprinkler heads under the roof began to spray a cold mist, which settled on the bricks like a fine rain. The clamor of the factory blurred into a single sound, like the fading of some gigantic gong. I passed out.

Sometimes I wonder what became of that hand. The foreman must have scraped it out of the molder. I wonder whether he bothered to wrap it in a napkin and send it along to the emergency room. I wonder whether a nurse dropped it discretely into a trash bin. I wonder whether a garbage truck hauled it off to some landfill with the tongue depressors, the snotty tissues, and the foil trays of meat loaf and peas. I’ll never know.

But lying on that pallet in that warehouse, I felt sure, as I stubbed out my second cigarette, that I was well rid of that old right hand. That hand couldn’t be trusted. That hand had exhibited a self-destructive streak a yard wide. There’d been something wrong with that hand.

Be that as it may, I was going to have to climb down from there sooner or later. I looked forward to the bus ride back to my room. I would ride the Pulaski bus north at eight in the morning, dead to the world and daydreaming of coral snakes and isopods, bone marrow and xylophones, traffic lights, whistles, the planet Neptune, absolute zero. . .

A scuffling sound distracted me from my musings. The back of my head was up against that box of 3031s, and something was moving around inside it.

I sat up, turned myself around, and knelt there, watching the box. A hand crept out and crouched on the one of flaps. It was an Ortho-Hand, just like mine, except that it was a left hand instead of a right.

It jumped down off the box and landed on the pallet. Ignoring me completely, it migrated to a corner of the skid. There were other things moving in the box. Soon they began to crawl out. Mechanical hands, dozens of them, each one cleaner and shinier and more futuristic than the one before.

One by one they abandoned the skid. In single file they made their way down the shelves. I saw them gathering in a swarm down on the floor. It dawned on me that they were waiting for me.

So I followed them down. I slid from tier to tier down a pillar. It wasn’t difficult. The hands moved up the aisle in tight formation, like a carpet of metal fingers. I walked behind them out of the warehouse. It was nearly quitting time.

I didn’t have to follow them. I could have punched my time card and walked out into the morning wind. I could have gone and sat on a bench under rustling trees in Humboldt Park and watched the broken bottles glitter in the grass. But I chose to follow the swarm of hands. I didn’t want to make them angry.

We crossed a threshold into the bright light of the Production Department. Black women with nimble fingers and empty eyes and stooped Hispanic men in undershirts were folding instruction sheets and packing twigs of model parts into cartons. No one saw the hands. No one noticed me. They were too busy keeping up with the machines.

The hands lined up like an honor guard on either side of the aisle between two injection molders. I walked down the aisle and turned to the machine on my left. As I watched through a pane of safety glass, the mold opened, coughed out a twig of miniature auto parts, and briskly closed again.

Two of the mechanical hands joined themselves at their wrists and climbed the side of the molder, flip-flopping like some strange insect. They pushed aside the safety glass. Now I had a clear shot. I knew just what I had to do.

The mold opened. I put my left hand in its way. This time I remembered to shut my eyes before the mold closed.

I had a plan, you see. I wanted to improve myself.

Flesh wasn’t good enough for me.

Look at the moon, Alex. A crescent moon has come up. A real fairy tale moon.

Do you want to hear this story, or don’t you?

I thought it was over.

So it is.

Naomi liked it. She went right to sleep.

So she has.

Tell me one of yours.

CHAPTER THREE

THE CHOSEN DONOR

Oyanoconic in nanacaoctli

Ya noyol in choca.

I have drunk the wine of mushrooms,

and my heart weeps.

—Nahuatl song

The star field turned slowly over the desert. The air was cold now. The walls of rock that enclosed the gully were invisible in the darkness, but the white sand seemed to glow. It glowed around the wheels of the jeep and around the old woman, asleep on her side. The brontosaur was curled into a great black mound beside them.

Eva wasn’t sleeping well. She felt uncomfortable in the circuitry of the jeep. She’d been listening to Alex dream about his various hands.

She drifted back through her life and thought of the day, so long past, when she had almost lost her heart. It happened all the way back in the twentieth century. She’d been young and beautiful then. Her hair had been black and lustrous.

Now she was a ridiculous relic, no good to anyone, with crooked teeth, split nails, and broken shoelaces. And with her family around her, always her family, her monstrous prison-house family. Her crazy husband who’d built himself out of auto parts. Her feeble-minded daughter who weighed a ton. And herself, too old to be human anymore. But once she’d been young and beautiful. Once she’d been the center of the world. So she dreamed of the raven-haired girl she’d been.

Long ago in the twentieth century, there was a young woman who lived only for her task. Hers was a proud nation, and it had set her a lofty task. The task lay ahead of her and guided her every step.

She lived in the Yucatan, within the walls of the Donors’ Convent at Chetumal. She could run like the wind. Her mind was filled with a shimmering fog of sacred legends and convent gossip. She was a blissful idiot. But ah, she had her task.

The walled compound of the convent was nested at the center of the Imperial Botanical Gardens. Inside the walls, rows of bread nut trees shaded the arboretum. Every morning a man ten feet tall with the head of a turtle drew a cart to the gate of the gardens. The cart held a tour group of fidgeting little boys with the heads of shrimp. They were led by a stern stooped man with the head of a parrot fish. Novices with the heads of angelfish spied on the boys through cracks in the walls of the convent.

Every night twenty novices slept on slabs of sandstone in a dark dormitory. The skin of their faces was striped coral pink and coral blue. Eva was one of them.

In the spring of Eva’s sixteenth year, the day of the sacred Cardiac Ceremony approached. The novices talked of nothing else. They wondered which of them would be the Chosen Donor. On the night preceding the great day, they lay on their slabs as usual, but none of them slept. Eva lay and listened to their breathing. Lobster birds called harshly in the arboretum. A prioress with the head of a squid entered the dormitory and drifted through the shadows. Her feet traversed a pool of moonlight that fell from a casement onto turquoise floor tiles. She stopped at Eva’s bedside and whispered an incantation. She was pronouncing Eva dead. And Eva rejoiced at having been chosen.

According to custom, the dead girl arose before dawn, put on her muslin tunic and her sandals, and went out to walk among the bread nut trees. As she went outdoors, her tutor fluttered down from its niche in the ceiling, perched on her shoulder, and folded its scaly wings. It was an altered remora, a sucker fish that helped her with her lessons.

Eva walked the paths of the arboretum—cobbled paths between beds of flowers and herbs. She paused beside a stand of prickly pear with waxy yellow blooms that attracted the pollen minnows. The minnows flitted from bloom to bloom, clicking pleasantly. He tutor sang her a serenity mantra. A restless wind shook the paloverde trees.

“Look, Tutor, the trees are dancing.”

“It’s the wind,” said the remora.

“They never stop moving,” said Eva.

“They’ll stop when the wind dies down.”

A dragon fish crossed the path, walking on its spines. It blinked at the rising sun. The wind whirled past, and it spread its tail fins—membranes of electric green with ocelli of silver and blue. Eva’s tunic rustled with a sound like corn husks. The branches of the trees were never still. Soon the spring rains would come. Eva wouldn’t see them. Eva was a ghost now.

She touched the bone at the center of her chest. She ran her finger down her scar—a pale numb ridge that ceremonial knives had cut into three times. Beginning from this seam she would part before the laser scalpel like a peach, like a stream of clear water. She would dissolve into light, and only her heart would remain—a gift for the Whale Emperor, who had always owned it.

Her tutor whispered to her. “Go inside now. It’s time.”

“Not yet.”

“You must. The abbess is waiting.”

Eva made for a side entrance of the convent. “Where are you going?” cried her tutor. “That’s not the right door!”

Eva went into the kitchens. The only sister working there was the senior cook, a manatee woman that Eva loved dearly, fleshy and whiskered, with satiny blue-black skin and kind brown eyes. The cook stood at her chopping block, cutting yams. A year earlier, when Eva had a fever, the cook had brought her a bowl of blackberries in goat’s milk. The cook cut yams and hummed to herself, a peaceful sound.

An angelfish novice leaned into the kitchen and saw Eva there. “Here she is!” the novice called out. All at once the kitchen was swarming with novices—squealing, laughing, shouting questions, crowding around Eva, touching her with their delicate blue hands. They all looked exactly like her, but only Eva was the Chosen Donor.

She couldn’t breathe. They were too close, too loud, too ecstatic with envy. They were trying to crowd inside her. She tore herself free of their hungry caresses. She pushed her way through them into a corridor. She ran toward the entry hall to meet the abbess, her raven-dark hair swinging wild.

She collided with a tall frail sister in a robe of green and gold. It was the abbess! Eva backed away, horrified at her blunder but unable to find her voice for an apology.

Slender pearl-gray hands smoothed the brocade of the squid mother’s robe. Her eyes blinked wetly and regarded the breathless girl who knelt at her feet. “Rise,” said the holy mother, gesturing with a tentacle. “Why do you run in the hall?”

“I don’t know, Your Grace,” said Eva.

“Today, child, you must let nothing disturb you. You must let no distraction sway you. Take my hand, child. Walk with me.”

Hardly believing, Eva took the squid mother’s hand. They walked the empty corridors undisturbed, as though enchanted out of time. They climbed a spiral staircase and emerged through the floor of the chapel. Four more zahori of the squid totem awaited them in the purple light.

The sisters took Eva’s tunic and sandals from her. She stood naked on the cold marble, while her tutor clung to her shoulder with its sharp black fins, watching everything from its small red eyes. The sisters bathed her with sponges and stroked her skin with scented oils. They brought forth living jewels that glowed like fireflies—sapphire indigo and diamond white. They applied the jewels to her arms and shoulders. The jewels took root in her flesh and shone with a steady blue light. The sisters brushed her hair and dressed her in the quexquemitl cloak and the Chosen Donor’s headdress.

Then they handed her a silver chalice that held wine and ololiuhqui the seed of the morning glory. As she sipped the wine, she felt the flower essence spreading through her like a vapor. Her bones went crystalline. She felt real at last, as though all that had gone before had been a disguise. Finally she was free to walk her path and have done.

The elders moved around Eva, perfecting her costume. She might have melted into the floor, but the dust motes floated in the shafts of light, so Eva floated also. Everything amazed her—the stained glass, the slender gray hands that adjusted her feathered cloak, the small red eyes of her tutor. . .

Outside the chapel doors, harsh men’s voices began to shout. “Make way! Make way!” The blowfish priests had come to claim her. The sisters of the convent raised their own voices in a wail of ritual lament. The doors flew open.

A crowd of squat round priests waddled into the chapel, pushed aside the squid sisters, and surrounded Eva. They wore crimson lavalavas, and necklaces of volcanic glass were draped on their spiny bellies. Their scales were the colors of maize kernels—red, orange, and yellow. They had little fins for arms and just enough head for their wide toothy mouths. One of them bellowed up at Eva. “Come with us!”

The priests marched Eva out of the chapel into the sunlight. They marched her through the convent gates into the botanical gardens. She crossed the azure lawns, amazed by the orchards and the fields of black corn and blue corn. A dragon fish scolded the world from a banyan tree. A beautiful palanquin rested on the grass between two turtle men whose shells would bear its poles. The sunshade of the palanquin was covered with orchids—saffron yellow and velvet black. The turtle men wore liveries of embroidered silk. They turned their horny snouts and watched Eva climb into her seat. Then they lifted the poles.

Worm-headed gardeners swung wide the gates of the gardens. The blowfish priests followed the palanquin in a throng, as the turtle men bore it across a bridge of amber glass. The bridge stood on pylons above a mile-wide channel of glittering green water. The wind tugged at the plumes of Eva’s cloak. On the far side of the water she heard the murmur of thousands of voices. At first she thought the sound must be Xochipilli the Prince of Flowers singing in her blood, or the whistling of Nti Ki Xo the landslide mushroom. Then she remembered that thousands of people were gathered at the Plaza of the Granaries to see her pass.

An escort of twenty shark soldiers stood in ranks on a widening of the amber bridge. They joined the palanquin and the priests. The bridge road made a gradual arc across a canyon between immense conical granaries. Cordons of shouting soldiers held back torrents of shouting people. Nervous clusters of translucent shrimp children sucked frozen custards on sticks, while parrot fish scholars in black robes pointed their staffs at Eva. “Wave to the people,” said her tutor.

Spider-crab rickshaw men cheered for her. They’d brought lamprey women from the nurseries, who coiled in the rickshaws under parasols, their slick white bodies swollen with eggs. Eva waved to the lamprey women, and some of them waved back. The granaries were hung with bright pennants that fluttered and snapped, and every window bulged with octopus men. A marching band of sea star girls fell into formation behind the soldiers. The girls shrilled their ocarinas and pounded their drums.

“Life is sweet,” said Eva.

“If you say so,” said her tutor.

The road dipped down a ramp into a bright tunnel. The tunnel wound through the foamcrete foundations of the city. The soldiers and the marching band halted, fell silent, and turned back.

The tunnel led Eva and the priests to a moving beltway, wide as a river, that swept them across miles of arched gray underground. All around Eva, above and below, she saw bunkers manned by piranhas of the warrior caste, and the hoops of the colossal particle cannons that protected her nation from missile attack. She could see through the foamcrete. Below the foamcrete and the stone she saw magma flowing. She was a geothermal mine shaft and a solar observatory. She saw the whole continent.

“We’re living on a raft,” she told her tutor.

“Keep breathing,” said the remora. “You’re forgetting to breathe.”

The beltway spat them all up onto a stark flatland punctuated by security towers. The three towers of the medical center loomed ahead, their outer skeletons modeled after the helical latticework of a sea sponge. When the center’s foundations were poured, a thousand infants had been interred as a consecration. Within it the imperial surgeons waited, and the Whale Emperor himself.

Eva’s bearers and the obese armless priests waddled onward toward the medical center’s entrance. As they drew near, fireworks were launched from the roofs—a rain, a flood of fireworks, blinding in broad daylight, streaking the sky with cobalt blue and chlorate green. Eva stepped down from her palanquin and danced with delight. Whirring sparks fell from the jewels in her arms. She spun and laughed, and the sparks glowed and died on the flagstones.

The blowfish priests settled her down and moved her along toward the towers of glass. Some of them groaned a burial chant, for after she entered the center, Eva would be legally buried as well as ceremonially dead. The priests were escorting her to Tlalocan the Other World. But the medical center wasn’t Tlalocan. Why were they all babbling about Tlalocan?

Hundreds of Sick Ones appeared on the balconies of their wards—sunfish men, cowrie eunuchs, children of the acorn worm. . . They looked drained and sleepless. They’d been giving a lot of blood for the emperor. Giving their blood was a solemn privilege for them and gave their wretched lives meaning.

The holy emperor used up a lot of blood. After a single circuit of his veins, it was piped away to bless the hydroponics farms. He also needed blood for his bathing pool, which was as wide as a lake. He didn’t really need a bathing pool that big. He was only a fat old man with four hearts. But he was the emperor.

“Why has she stopped?”

“What is she staring at?”

“Keep chanting!”

The emperor needed four hearts to keep him alive. After four years inside him, each heart had to be replaced with a fresh one. That was what Eva was for.

Far above, an acorn worm boy climbed onto a balcony railing. The other Sick Ones shouted encouragement until he jumped, and cheered him all the way down. He hit the flagstones and bounced once. Three more Sick Ones followed, then a dense shower of them. Suicides were only permitted on the Day of Donorship, but today they were a sacrament. The blowfish priests chanted their approval.

The growing heap of smashed invalids was an ugly sight. Eva hurried into medical center’s lobby. The priests gave her over to the keeping of four nurses. The nurses were salmon women with soothing voices, gentle hands, and flat orange eyes.

They took away Eva’s cloak and headdress. They arranged her body on a bier of white glass. They wheeled the bier to the bank of a canal. A gilded gondola floated on the viscous violet fluid of the canal. The nurses carried the bier onto the gondola. The little golden boat surged forward along an underwater groove. It took them to a docking at the base of a shaft. A silver gate slid up from the glimmering fluid and sealed them off. The fluid level rose and lifted the gondola steadily up the shaft.

The nurses drew Eva to her feet. They guided her through a tubular corridor that curved like a snake. Eva’s knees were weak, but the nurses held her in place. They prevented her from drifting from the path. They passed a stickleback radiologist in a heavy leather apron. He looked up from his clay tablets and bowed to them.

“Thank you,” said Eva. “Thank you.”

“Not at all,” said one of the nurses. “It’s an honor for us.”

As they walked, the nurses used scissors to clip the raven tresses from Eva’s head. Locks of hair littered their trail in melancholy constellations. They plucked the jewels from Eva’s skin. They ran krill tools over her scalp, until it was all buzzed clean.

Beyond an archway was the surgical theater—a circular chamber under a dome of mirror glass. At the center of the chamber was a dais of basalt. On the dais stood an obsidian slab. Off in an alcove two surgeons stood naked under sterilizing lamps. Their heads were the heads of manta rays. All this was as it should be.

But it was dawning on Eva that they must be insane. These decent friendly helpful people—these calmly smiling professionals who wouldn’t dream of doing Eva harm—they were all quite mad. They were preparing to cut her heart out. Her heart cringed in her chest. She held herself stiff to keep from screaming.

She had lived among these people all her life. They had taught her to walk. They had taught her to think. Now they would cut her apart and throw away the pieces they didn’t need. But something had gone wrong inside her. She didn’t want to die.

“It won’t be long now,” said the remora on her shoulder.

“Be quiet,” snapped Eva. “You don’t know anything.”

A salmon-faced nurse tilted the obsidian slab vertical and stood Eva with her naked back against its chill. The slab tilted again to take her weight. Medical sensors thrummed within the black stone. Rotating platinum needles pricked her skin. Her flesh grew heavy as lead. A nurse drew a leather strap tight across her waist and buckled it. Eva watched herself in the mirror dome above her. The theater dimmed. A cone of hot red light isolated the sacrificial slab. The manta surgeons gathered around in sequined white gowns and latex gloves. Their internal voices clamored in Eva’s ears.

“My dear child! Relax. You mustn’t fret. We have great confidence in you.”

“Tiresome little shit sucker. Just my luck if that pea-wit druggist fucked up the dosages.”

“Quetzl. I’m surrounded by morons.”

“You must open your heart to the Laser of Faith, Daughter. You must learn to give freely of yourself.”

The voices droned on, trapping Eva in uncertainty. She began to weep, saying, “Help me, Mother! I am lost! I have fallen from grace!”

“Damned incompetents! She’s overdosed! I’ll fry those squid bitches’ eyes in butter for them. Sabotage my surgery.”

Eva’s heart was a small blind creature that lived in her chest, contentedly breathing blood. It inhaled scarlet serpents and blew them out through four branching trumpets in her lungs. It was in no danger. The surgeons would care for it. They simply wanted to put it somewhere else.

The cone of red light narrowed. The laser lamp hovered in the shadows. Cleave before the cutting light, Eva recited to herself. Fear it not, but part before its radiance, as summer clouds before the sun.

After the operation Eva would be maintained by a heart/lung conch for one hour. She would appear on a balcony and address an adoring throng. She would wear a thorny white wedding crown and a cape of white lace and corn flowers. If she found she couldn’t speak, a recording would be substituted. Then they would tattoo the death glyph onto her cheek, take her to the catacombs, and disconnect her. She would stand beside the ones who’d gone before her. All this was as it should be.

Blue water welled up from grids in the floor and flooded the theater. The nurses strapped the surgeons into their oxygen masks. The brine began to lap at Eva’s heels. One of the nurses put a mouthpiece between her teeth.

“Bite down. Blow into it. Take a deep breath. That’s fine.”

The water swallowed Eva’s legs. The instrument table went under. Eva closed her eyes.

Her remora bit scores of tiny tin teeth into her neck. What’s wrong with you? it seemed to be screaming. Haven’t I taught you anything? Will you lie there and let them carve you open?

Leave me alone! Eva thought at the hateful black fish.

But it would be so easy. If you push the secret level, the hollow panel will spring open. You can run down the secret passage. You’ll run like the wind and escape them all. It only needs one little push.

But I want to die, Eva told it. This is my wish come true! Why are you trying to ruin it?

“She’s struggling.”

“She’s trying to talk.”

“Tighten the head strap!”

“More sedation!”

Eva calmed herself and was soon breathing easily through her air tube. She opened her eyes in the submerged red light. Bound and gagged on her tilted slab of obsidian. With platinum needles turning in her flesh. While earnest surgeons with faces of scale and cartilage waddled about her in weighted belts and rubber masks. Murmuring to one another through microphones. Streams of bubbles rising from their respirators. Closing in for the kill.

Eva’s hands raised themselves, as the chief surgeon held the dagger before her—the ceremonial dagger carved of ironwood, sharp as glass. Acting on their own instructions, Eva’s hands grasped the dagger’s hilt, sharing it with the hands of the surgeon. Four hands lowered the dagger point to touch the milk-white scar at her breastbone. Eva would make the first incision. The surgeon was only here to help her.

Unfamiliar voices spun in her head, blurred and contentious, like chattering shoals of shrimp, spinning their arguments into frequencies inaudible, leaving Eva behind. Ray faces and salmon faces peered down at her through faceplates. Their eyes were small blackened dead things. Less like eyes than wounds from a hot poker.

Eva began the incision. A crimson cloud spread from the dagger point. The chief surgeon tightened his grip on the hilt. But his hands were weak. Eva took a deep breath.

She spat out her mouthpiece and yanked the dagger from the surgeon’s hands. She drove the blade into the side of his neck, gouged at his spine, and pulled the dagger free again. This one wouldn’t be cutting out any hearts today.

She unbuckled her waist strap and twisted herself into a ball. A nurse reached for her ankles. Eva kicked the hands away. Then she swung her heel into the toothless white snout of an assistant surgeon—snap. His face bled a rusty orange murk.

Another nurse lumbered up behind her. Eva kicked off from the surgery slab and shot up through the sterile red water toward the dome. She swam to the mirror glass, pressed her palms against it, set her eyes close to it. She saw people behind the glass in rows of theater seats, all staring at her.

Those people had air to breathe, but Eva had none. She hammered at the glass with her fist. Then she realized that the dagger was between her teeth. She took the hilt in her fist and stabbed at the mirror. She stabbed again and again, until the glass cracked. Water rushed through the cracks, sucking at Eva. Spectators rose from their seats and crowded the aisles. But still Eva had no air.

She wedged the strong wooden blade into the focus of the cracks. She shoved the hilt sideways. The dome burst open like a cranium tearing all its sutures, blown to hell. Brine flooded the theater. Eva fell into the current and through it. She landed on a rough green carpet on her hands and knees. She could breathe again.

“What are you doing?” screeched her tutor.

She tore the remora from her shoulder and crushed its head against the floor.

She must have been some sort of mutant freak. She wanted to stay alive. And so she did. Until she grew old and ridiculous.

Now it’s Naomi’s turn.

Naomi! Wake up and tell us one of your dreams.

CHAPTER FOUR

DESERTION

A man of words and not of deeds

Is like a garden full of weeds.

And when the weeds begin to grow,

It’s like a garden full of snow.

—Mother Goose

The stars are out. Everyone on this stupid desert is asleep. Papa is asleep. Mama is asleep. They’re dreaming. But I’m awake. I’m always awake.

This is a dream too, this desert. It’s the dream that Dr. Mazer puts in our heads. I know because he told me so.

I don’t have dreams. But sometimes I send a ghost out of my head, and she watches things for me—things that are really happening or things that happened long ago.

Just now, while Mama was telling her dream, I sent myself back to the night when I ate Papa. I ate him in a bowling alley in British Columbia in 1990. But I accidentally when back too far, all the way to 1970. So I had to hover around and watch my life go by for twenty years. It didn’t take long. Nothing ever happens to me. In my head I’m still a little girl. That means I’m a retard. That’s what they told me at boot camp.

I was fifteen when I joined the Canadian army. It was the same as the orphanage. Bad weather. Scratchy blankets. Moldy barracks. You were supposed to do what you were told. If you didn’t, you had a Bad Attitude.

I got stationed at this radar installation in the middle of some frozen Yukon nowhere. The outpost was operated entirely by unhappy young women. I lived there, numb and fuzzy, for a year and a half, doing nothing but mop concrete floors. I was lucky. My girlfriend had no work to do. She lived in her bunk, reading magazines.

Then my duty officer promoted me to dish washer’s assistant. I got to stand in a steamy kitchen ten hours a day, scraping cafeteria trays and staring through two layers of glass at the twilight over the permafrost.

We lived in half buried Quonset huts connected by accordion tubes. The sky was always white, and the sun on the frost hurt your eyes. If you went walking, the ice squeaked under your boots, and the air hurt your lungs. None of us ever got leave, and there was nowhere to go. Also I never saw any radar towers. Eventually I made a Fine Distinction. I realized that I wasn’t serving at an army base. I was living in a detention camp.

I got an appointment with my guidance counselor, Esther. I wanted to apply for a transfer. Esther suggested the cryostatic reserves. The frozen soldiers. She gave me a contract to read. A consent form. I sat on a cold chair in an empty cafeteria and tried to read it.

The Cryostatic Corps offered a deal that seemed too good to be true. They put you under for twenty years. In case of martial law or a ground war, they woke you up. If they never needed you, they brought you around after twenty years, and you were free. Veteran’s pension plan. Loans for education. I was sold. I poured some salt out of a shaker and drew pictures on the table.

Cryostasis was very popular at the time, all around the world. All the major players were doing it. It was the latest wrinkle on the arms race. The relative troop strengths of the various frozen armies were always in the news. The reporters were bored with nuclear arsenals, tonnages of nerve gas, and germ weapons with unpronounceable Latin names.

Esther and I drove a convoy truck from the radar station to the troop repository. That’s where they did the freezing. We drove dead straight roads across mossy tundra and mud flats. The repository was a parking lot, a couple of hangars, and a windowless administration building. I got out of the truck and saluted Esther. She drove away.

I pushed through a door marked ORIENTATION. A receptionist showed me to a briefing room. A dozen or so uniformed men and women sat at desks and filled out forms. An hour later all the canister volunteers had arrived, and the pre-drop briefing could begin.

A technical sergeant strode up to a blackboard, introduced himself, and pulled down a projection screen. He launched into a lecture on the freezing procedures, punctuated by a series of slides. All very technical. I sat in the dark, entranced by electron micrographs of pyramidal neurons—dendrites branching like stark black twigs after an ice storm, axons that hung from gangliae like crooked icicles.

The fan in the slide projector whirred. The technical sergeant droned. I got interested in the neck of the boy sitting in front of me. I hadn’t seen a boy for months. Something about how deterioration of synaptic ions was inhibited by the induction of high cycle current through superconductive cytoplasm at temperatures approaching seventeen Kelvin, next slide please, and something something something, lights please, click, any questions?

An orderly called us, one by one, into the prep clinic. In the prep clinic they laid me out on a leather couch and shaved my head, my armpits, and my crotch. They had little steel basins full of lather. A little rubber gasket and two copper hoops danced in the bubbling water of a sterilizer.

The gasket and hoops were going to be fitted into the top of my skull. Trepanation, it’s called. Cave people used to do it for their dead, to give the soul a path of escape. The prep medics had a different reason for doing it. Cerebrospinal fluid expands when it freezes. It has to have somewhere to go, or it crushes the brain. So the medics had to drill little holes in our heads and fill them with flexible membranes. Down in the canisters, under the liquid nitrogen, the membranes would swell.

A medic shot my scalp full of anesthetic. Some other medics poked around up there for a while. Whatever they were doing, I didn’t want to know about it.

When they were done with me, they sent me into a locker room. I took off my uniform and folded it into a rectangular wire basket with a tin number tag. I even had to give up my glasses, and I could hardly see without them. A staff sergeant sent my group into the steam bath. The steam bath was full of hammocks, hammocks of white nylon twine hung in frameworks of aluminum tube. The sergeant told us to lie down in the hammocks and relax.

One of the volunteers said, “Why do we gotta get in hammocks to take a bath?” He talked funny. I think he was a retard like me.

The sergeant said, “Because that’s the procedure.” We all got in hammocks, and the sergeant left.

There was a faint hissing. The bath filled with dense white steam. I stared at the ceiling, feeling warm and sleepy. Then I noticed that I couldn’t turn my head. A voice in my head said one word. Curare.

Then there were hands moving on my skin, dabbing on surgical cement, pasting on little brass disks that trailed red wires, attaching disks to my thumbs, my toes, my ribs... My head lolled to one side, and I was staring into the eyes of the woman in the next hammock over. She stared blankly back at me. I couldn’t close my eyes. We were all too naked.

When my wires were attached, the hands unhooked my hammock and spilled me into a steel cart. They rolled the cart to a hangar. The roof of the hangar was green Plexiglas. The gray cement floor was crowded with chrome canisters, nine feet tall—a forest of canisters. The hands parked my cart beside my assigned can. They compared the number on the can with the tag on my ankle. They took readings from gauges and made notes on a clipboard. They took hold of the big black three-prong plug where all my wires led. Then they climbed the C-shaped steel rungs of the can’s ladder, up to the open hatch. They reached the plug over the rim and twisted it into the can’s inner wall. They climbed back down again.

When are they going to put you to sleep? asked a voice in my head. They said that they would put you to sleep!

A mobile crane, an operator, and a handler made their way among the can clusters. They stopped beside my cart, hooked my hammock to the crane, and hoisted me aloft. I hung from my feet, slowly revolving while the handler wrapped me in a sticky white gauze that he rolled off of spools.

“This one’s ready,” he said.

The voice in my head was screaming. She’s not! She’s not ready! But they didn’t hear it. The crane hoisted me higher, then lowered me into a dim cavity. A blindfolded paralyzed Houdini, attempting an impossible escape.

I could just barely see through the gauze. Spook house mirror skin stretching across chrome. Pink cocooned in white, sinking into the dark. The handler put his head into the circle of light above my feet. He pushed in a stray loop of wire. He slammed the lid.

In utter blackness a suction pump stole away my last breath. The roof of my mouth ached with the pressure of the pent-up molten nitrogen that was waiting to fill the can. I prayed for an end to the waiting. I prayed for the vacuum to fill.

Then the blind shrieking blood-red bolt of lightning shot down a thousand wires and nerves and lit for one instant a tightening cloud of brittle blue droplets. Snap! And it was all over for me.

Cheated of shivering and cramps. Cheated of pleas and tears. Nothing outside of me, just my brain winding up my spinal cord like a rubber band in a toy airplane. Struggling to squirm out of the rotten frozen core of that dark, that dark, that dark so cold, you could cut it with a knife, with a knife so sharp, it would cut off your thumb, and your thumb so numb, it wouldn’t cut butter, and the butter so small that it sings in the eye of an icicle and slips through a needle, through a needle so thin, it would slide off your spoon, and the spoon so hot, it burns a hole in your tongue, and your tongue so wet that it sticks to a frosty parking meter, and there you are, there you are, there you are, stuck. On a city street in the dirty sleet, stuck by your tongue.

And it’s no consolation that you’re one of a series—just one little girl in a row of little girls in blue jackets, all standing on the sidewalk, all spit-glued to parking meters because they were so foolish. Because they were foolish and also because someone, someone they never see, keeps coming around and putting quarters in the meters. He does that to keep the little red violation flags from popping up. And to keep the current running through the meters. So the tongues stay stuck—all those raw purple tongues of all those little girls in blue jackets. For twenty years.

Oh children misled! said the voice in my head. Beware! Beware! For thou art dead. Beware entrapment by refrigerator. Beware the lid that slams on suffocation. Beware the stinging freezer mites that swarm in the ice cubes that float in the desolate gutters of Freezer Burn—where no one can call you an ambulance.

This was the place of itching buzzing battery acid, where balloons broke like eggshells, and eggshells bent like balloons.

Some days I pretended to be a little Dutch girl. She’s fallen through thin ice at a skating pond. She’s crawling on a ceiling of ice, but she can’t find the place where it broke. No one can help her. The bubbles of her shouts rise wobbling through green water and roll around the slick white ceiling. Behind the ice is a mild blue sky. But her fists can’t break through. A final bubble clings to her sky blue lips. No kindly wood chopper happens by to chop her free.

I was wide awake. The technical sergeant promised we’d blank out, remember nothing. What a stinking lie! My thinking went faster and faster, the longer I was under. But perhaps I was the only one awake. Perhaps I was a fluke. None of the other corpsicles were complaining. Not a whimper. The hangar was peaceful. Freon pumps hummed between their teeth. Snow fell all winter on the Fiberglas roof. In the spring it melted. Once a month a technician in green coveralls came around and checked our hoses for leaks. The years passed.

It came to me that I’d have to escape. I considered the matter in the privacy of my bone marrow. All those other stiffs were welcome to sleep their lives away like pale larvae in narrow cells of beeswax. But not me. I was busy reaching down my throat and gulping down my vomit, swallowing the sweet puke again and again, until it turned to royal jelly, and I changed.

My helpers changed me. I had swarms of helpers—a mysterious gang that looked like human hormones but moved in poisonous circles. I gave them their instructions in dark corners of my rigid electrified tissues. They nodded and shuffled off into the polar wastes. Out there, where there weren’t any witnesses, they did things that spat on every law of zoology. They made up new laws as they went, subverted my chromoplasm, bent it to my will.

That seems unlikely to you, Papa? Oh Papa, you’re too ignorant to make a start on. How do you think anything gets done on this planet?

The first stage of pupation was to melt. I dissolved myself into half curdled hepatic lipids and confused pituitary lecithin. I melted to sour pupa milk, which sweetened as it matured. Continents of my codons sank like stones into a heaving cellular ocean of life noise. To be lost forever, one would assume. Denatured into tattered soiled punch cards on the floor of the Deoxyribo-Nucleic Archives, unretrievable. Forgotten references to failed escape tactics. Blurred beyond all hope of reconstruction, you’d assume. You’d assume, but you’d be wrong. Because there’s always another copy.

There’s always another copy, safe and snug, somewhere in the Life Library, waiting for a call slip. Behaviors and structures that have been cooling their heels in the stacks since the Mesozoic. I saw them. I swam in them. I was a call slip. I had slipped through the ice, and I was calling as loud as I could. Dog-paddling in endless possibility. I could wake up tomorrow a pinworm or a cuttlefish. Or a row of pretty dead blackbirds hung by their claws from a high-tension wire. Coming unstuck.

My eyes taught themselves to see in the sparkling black syrup. My fingers taught themselves to move again. I grabbed my hammock. Slowly I dragged myself right-side-up inside my thermos jar.

No one can see in the dark. No one can move in liquid nitrogen. But I could. I wasn’t a pupa anymore.

There was a latch wheel on the underside of the hatch. I hung from my hammock and yanked at the wheel with a blue fist. The seal cracked. White vapor boiled up into the light. I pushed the hatch open and clambered over the rim.

Then I fell, but my hammock trailed behind me and snagged. I slid from it gently to the gray cement floor. When I was dry, I sat up and pried the brass disks off my skin. I felt stubble on my scalp. My hair was growing back.

Then I was standing outside on a parking lot and wearing green coveralls. There were pine trees all around, under a starry sky. My breath made ragged clouds that drifted off into the night. Where did the pine trees come from? They must have moved me.

There weren’t any sirens or searchlights or guards shouting. Just me, standing in a puddle of rain water. And a couple of army jeeps inside a storm fence topped with razor wire. And beyond the fence—the trees, the stars, and an asphalt road. Was everyone on earth frozen? No. I was deserting my post. I had to get away from there, as fast as possible. I needed shoes.

Then I was driving a muddy green jeep in circles around the parking lot. Fog in the headlight beams. Tires swishing through the puddles, round and round. I flew like a blackbird through explosions of bright water, soaked to the skin and laughing like a mad thing.

There was a gate chained shut. The headlights dove straight at it, and it popped from the fence. The jeep bounced over it and carried me into the forest.

The road spilled down hillsides between dark mounds furred with fir trees and caterpillared with berry brambles. Above the treetops a river of stars poured past. Cattails and milkweed stalks waved to us from a drainage ditch. They beckoned us down the embankment. But the jeep kept its tires to the spine of the road.

The road was a black snake with birch trees for ribs. The jeep was in its belly. My hair lashed at my eyes. It was stretching out of my head like butterscotch syrup. My teeth were chattering, and wind harps and thumb pianos were playing in the flaps of my ears. Red reflectors on posts marched along the road shoulder—glowing sparks, falling past.

I made up songs in time with the rattling of my jaw. I was out of the army. If my hair kept growing like this, there’d be a mountain of it by morning. I could make a warm nest in it and hide there. The MPs would never find me. I would live in uncharted caves within an impregnable mountain range of dirty blonde hair.

The jeep sliced a whistling tunnel through the stillness and cricket song. The turbines made up songs out of methanol and hummed them to my bones. The wind made up frostbite songs and pricked them like quills into the wings of my ears. Burr weed and thistle. Oak and elm. The mist turned to drizzle. It stung my face. Rivulets climbed the windshield. It made me queasy to watch them skid around. A furry gray moth brushed a stroboscopic streak across the headlights. It smashed into the windshield and turned to a yellowy mess, splat! A pretty good trick, I thought.

Up ahead, beyond a rise, the forest began to glow. A pair of headlights came over the hump, then dipped out of sight again. I remembered about lanes and the rules of the road. I urged the jeep to the right. It was all coming back to me.

The headlights came hurtling down a slope at me, connected to a freight truck as wide as the road and twice as tall. Untold tons of incandescence and flattening tire tread bore down on me. The jeep plowed through a wall of ice water. I stiffened my arms and fought the steering wheel. My lungs filled with diesel stink, and water ran down my collar. Human contact.

The rain began to beat down hard. It strafed the road and stuttered on the hood of the jeep. The jeep rounded a bend, and suddenly we were passing neat suburban houses. White metal lawn furniture and bicycles on white gravel driveways. A church and a fire station. An auto shop with a yard full of wrecks.

Several questions troubled me. Would the MPs think that I’d escaped in the jeep, or that I’d been stolen? Would the local police have my description? Did I fit my description? How could I see so well without my glasses? And how long could I drive before I fell asleep? I hadn’t eaten or slept for twenty years.

The jeep cruised slowly past a wide stuccoed building. On the roof, an orange neon sign said BOWL-O-RAMA. Beside the door, a green neon sign said BEER & PIZZA. The jeep swung onto the parking lot and killed its engine.

I climbed out stiffly. The rain pounded down. I could hear music playing. But I had no shoes. I had no money. What if people spoke a different language now? I looked at the smashed moth on the windshield. One of its legs moved. Maybe the rain moved it. Vapor leaked from my mouth. I reached for the yellow stain and scraped it onto my index finger. My finger wiped it onto my tongue. I could taste that it was still alive, so I swallowed it down. It turned to a warm red ember in my stomach. I began to feel brave.

I crossed the parking lot and pushed through the glass door. I stood on a prickly green carpet in a low echoing barn. Sappy music buzzed from speakers in the ceiling. The green carpet ran the length of the building, with racks of rental bowling balls and ramps leading down to the alleys. Twenty alleys of varnished wood lay off to my right, with ten scoring desks facing them, and five semicircles of benches. Against the back wall were snack machines, a microwave oven, some video games, and a cashier’s booth.

The cashier was a balding man with a Hawaiian shirt and bloodshot eyes. He sat on a stool reading a paperback he’d propped against his cash register. Beyond him, a fat little boy with skinny legs was standing on a chair and playing a video game. Two people were bowling—a man in a baseball cap and a woman in a red dress. I pretended an interest in the snack machines. Corn chips and cheese puffs in cellophane. Dead stuff.
