“Hey,” the cashier said loudly. “You wanna bowl?” I walked up to his booth, dripping on the carpet. I hoped he wouldn’t notice my bare feet.

“Sh-sh-sh-sure,” I told him. My teeth were chattering.

He reached into a cubby behind him and slapped a pair of shoes on the counter. He ripped a scoring sheet from a yellow pad and took a stubby pencil from a box. “Enjoy your game,” he said.

I stood at a rack of bowling balls, pretending to choose one. I spied on the two bowlers. They were sitting on their bench and talking. The woman was beautiful, with glossy black hair down to her waist. She wore a lot of Mexican jewelry—silver and turquoise.

The man was eating a slice of pizza, drinking beer from a can, and gazing at the woman’s chest. He wore a faded blue shirt and some strange kind of gloves. No, they weren’t gloves, they were artificial hands. He took another slice from the pizza box beside him. He offered it to the woman. She shook her head. A thread of mozzarella connected the slice to the box.

I sat down on the carpet and put on the shoes. Tying the laces took forever. My fingers were like play dough. I stood up and picked out a ball—a black ball marbled with red. I carried it down a ramp and passed the bench where the man and woman were sitting. My shoes squeaked on a rubber mat.

I set my ball in the trough of my ball return. I sat down at my scoring desk and clipped my sheet to the Formica. Now what? I was too exhausted to care. It felt so good just to sit in one place. I folded my arms on the desk top and lay down my head. I half shut my eyes and watched clusters of blue lights play in the curtain of my long wet hair. I spied on the man and the woman some more.

The man with no hands stared into his beer can, deep in thought. The woman leaned forward to whisper in his ear, cupping one palm beside her mouth. He turned his head and planted a kiss on her cheek. She stood up and walked to the ball return. She pressed a button and dried her hands in the air jet. Her fingernails were coral pink. Alex watched her with a crooked smile. She hefted her ball to her chest. It was transparent with silver glitter stirred into it. She confronted alley ten, very concentrated. Her feet were bare. At the alley’s far end, three pins stood in a pool of light. Two on the left, one on the right.

“No way,” Alex heckled. “Never in a million years.”

“Shut up and watch,” Eva told him. She walked toward the foul line, dipping her body gracefully. The ball rolled straight into a gutter, wham! She turned to Alex, smiling. Alex laughed.

The sparkling silver ball rumbled the full length of the gutter and disappeared behind a rubber flap. The pin setter lowered a metal arm and swept the pins from the target stage. It set ten neatly ranked replacements in their place, holding the ten white necks in ten pincers. The pincers let go and withdrew from sight. One pin tottered for a moment. Ten brave little soldiers awaited the next bombardment.

Alex sucked some froth from the rim of his beer can, set the can carefully on the floor, and walked, not too steadily, to his ball return.

“You’re smashed,” Eva told him. “You can’t win. You don’t have a prayer.”

He grappled a black ball out of the ball return and squared off against alley ten, glaring at the pins and swaying a little. He leaned forward, charged the alley, stopped short, and hurled the ball. It bounced twice and thundered menacingly at the pins. Nine of them exploded into darkness.

I knew that I had to get up and move around. If I fell asleep at my desk, the cashier would throw me out, and I’d perish of cold and hunger and loneliness. So I walked to my ball return, punched the hot air button, and warmed my hands. When the air jet turned off, I turned it on again. My hands looked blue. I picked up my ball and turned to alley twelve. There stood my own little squadron of fat white pins with skinny red-striped necks. The bravest pin stood in front, all alone. The next two bravest stood behind him.

A video game was squonking and belching off behind me. Armadas of spaceships biting the interstellar dust. I glanced to my left. Alex was chewing on a pizza. Eva was opening a beer.

I shouldn’t have been there. I should’ve hidden in the forest and gone to sleep among the pine needles. My clothes were dripping. I was a wanted woman. I had no money and a rubber gasket in my head. But since I was standing at the foul line, I summoned the strength to send my ball wobbling down the alley. It actually found its way to the pins and clipped one off the back row, where the cowards hide. The surviving nine pins were pinched by the pin setter and airlifted. The emptied target stage was dutifully raked. The pins returned to their stations. My ball rolled into the ball return. I had to sit down.

I drew an N on my score sheet in the column where you write the names. Then I held onto the desk and shut my eyes, because the bowling alley was tipping. My pencil rolled off the desk. I should’ve been asleep in the woods. A corpsicle can’t freeze to death. A corpsicle can’t die from exposure.

No, but she might die of thawing.

“This pizza,” said Eva, “is colder than this beer.”

“Solids lose heat faster than liquids,” asserted Alex. He had his nose in her hair, and was trying to unzip her dress.

“Cut it out,” she told him, pushing him away. “You’re a mess. Get up and bowl.”

“I’m a mess?” said Alex. “You think I’m a mess, you should check out the Thing From Another World in the next lane.”

“Shut up,” she told him.

“She’s shivering over there.”

“Shut up! She can hear you.”

“No she can’t. She’s dead to the world.”

“I w-w-wish I w-w-were dead,” I announced, and opened my eyes long enough to lie down on the nearest bench and curl up on my side.

“I know the feeling,” said Alex.

Someone touched my arm. Eva was sitting beside me. She looked like a Mexican angel in too much makeup. She helped me sit up.

“Can you eat something?” she asked me. I shook my head. She put a slice of pizza in my hand. I touched it with my tongue, but it was just cardboard and ketchup, just dead stuff. The pizza was dead, and I was dying, and some whores look like Mexican angels, and some men have no hands.

“What’s your name?” she asked me.

“N-n-Naomi.”

“Do you live around here?”

“N-n-no.”

“Do you like to bowl?”

I shook my head.

“Neither do I. But I’m working. I’m on a date.”

“W-w-what’s your name?” I asked her.

“Eva.”

“What happened to your friend?”

“He’s not my friend. He went to take a leak.”

“I mean what happened to his hands?”

“Who knows? Don’t worry about him.” Her face was dark and smooth and luminous. Her hair had blue fires inside it.

“I like your face,” I told her.

Eva brushed some of her hair over her shoulder. “I’m glad,” she said. “It was expensive.”

“Are your hands blue?”

“No, but your lips are blue.”

“They sh-sh-should be,” I told her. “I just got out of a deep freeze.”

“You look like it. You want a warm beer?” I shook my head again. My hair was down past my shoulders now. Eva twisted her silver necklace around one finger. I wondered what she was thinking.

She put her hand on my shoulder. “Look, Naomi, it’s none of my business, but you’re soaked to the skin, and you’re acting pretty spacey. Maybe you ought to go home.”

“I don’t live here,” I told her. “I’m a soldier. I need to sleep, but I don’t have any money. I ran away. I haven’t slept for twenty years. I need to sleep!”

“Don’t raise your voice.” She scanned the area. Alex was on his way back to us. “Naomi, listen closely. You and I are leaving here. We’ll go in my car. But first I have to talk to someone. Sit right here, understand? I’ll be back.” Alex sat down heavily beside me. “And keep your distance from the smashed octopus.”

“I’d like to know what makes her so superior,” he said. “Hello. My name is Alex. I’d shake your hand, but it would only gross you out. Do you want to wipe your nose? Here, I brought you some tissue. Where’d she go anyway? What’s your name?”

“Naomi,” I told him.

“That’s nice.”

“No it isn’t.”

“Yet it is.”

“What are those things on your arms?”

“They’re just things.”

“They’re weird.”

“I know. But I like them.” He laid his right arm across the back of the bench. His arm touched my back. His arm was warm. I felt sleepy. I pushed the hair out of my face and rubbed my eyes with the heels of my hands.

“My hair keeps growing,” I told him. He nodded wisely.

“As though there were no tomorrow. Even after you’re dead. Can I get you some hot soup or something? Aren’t you cold?”

I touched my fingers to his upper lip. His lip was rough. His arm was warm behind my back.

“Please,” I said. “Please. Shut up. I have to sleep.” I was gazing into his sad brown eyes. As if I’d cast a spell, he closed his eyes and began to snore.

I closed my own eyes to see whether I could sleep. I saw the skating pond where the little Dutch girl drowned. There were ice maidens in silver sequins doing leaps and spins above the girl’s head. Dancing on her grave. I couldn’t watch.

While you snored, I waited and shivered. The MPs were going to find me and stuff me back in a can. Unless I died first. I couldn’t get warm. My head was cracking open. The sadness in my chest was too much to hold. I put my arms around you.

Then I heard music. Lyres and mbiras and steel drums and balalaikas, gorgeous and far away. I whispered in your ear.

“Alex,” I said. “Dance with me.”

You slumped off the bench with your chin on my neck. I maneuvered you onto your feet. I held you and swayed to the music. I took off your cap and felt the hair on the back of your head. I pressed my forehead against yours. My fair flyaway hair bristled into the field that was building between us. I closed my eyes and put my tongue in your mouth. I wanted to punch the barb of my tongue right through your hard palate and sting you in the brain. I wanted you to love me.

Steam was rising from my coveralls. Thorny yellow wires were pushing out of me, piercing and twisting to be free, crowding my clothes with their twitching. They poked through my coveralls. I opened my eyes a crack, and I could see them through my lashes. My nerves were growing out of me like blackberry shoots in a time-lapse botany film. And when a nerve had worked a sufficient length of itself free of me, it would thread itself bloodlessly into you, Alex. Taking you over, tying you to me, whispering down your collar and up your sleeves, binding you in a thicket of nerves. The tighter I held you, the denser the bramble grew.

And the denser it got, the more you melted into me. It was hard to hold you up as we swayed to the music. You weighed a ton. And I weighed a ton. You and I were becoming confused. The more confused we got, the less we resembled human beings. I didn’t care. I was on fire. I was breathing through four nostrils. I was going to live.

I was aware that the cashier had fled into the night, gagging in horror, and that the little boy was cowering under his video game, peeing his shorts. But all I cared about was the way your hard-on felt, sliding across my hip.

I wanted to tell you things, but my tongue couldn’t find your ear. Then I found your left eustachian tube, slipped my tongue through, and coiled it to the core of your cochlea. Then you could really hear me. Then I felt I had your attention. Deep in that dark pink seashell. Nerve to nerve.

Alex?

Who?

I’m eating you alive.

What?

I’m eating you alive. Do you mind?

A plastic hand fell to the floor.

Where am I?

You’re dreaming.

The other hand dropped off and rolled under the bench.

Well. . . If I’m dreaming, I guess it can’t hurt. Go ahead. Eat me alive.

Your pants were down around your ankles, and my tongue was in your ear. You were thinking that your wet dream had somehow derailed itself. You considered opening your eyes, but you’d already seen so many freak accidents. You didn’t need the gory details.

A wind was blowing around my nerves. It made them dance like Queen Anne’s lace, sweetly intricate and bright with dew. Then it howled and swept us away. A buzzing glacier buried us. We clung together inside the ice, breathing through one another’s mouths. We melted together, and we survived.

Hold me, Alex. Hold on tight. Come inside me, and I’ll tell you a secret.

Thus I lured you into my awful immortality. Thus were you crucified on the tumbleweed of a young woman’s loneliness. Thus I became your prison. Poor Alex.

I’ll tell you a secret. I knew exactly what I was doing. I planned the whole thing. I sucked you in. I knew it was wrong, but what else could I do? I was frozen. I was dying. I was lonely.

Forgive me, Papa. Forgive me my ancient sin. Forgive me for being cold. Forgive me for being hungry.

Forgive me for needing you.

CHAPTER FIVE

STORM CENTER

The ideas of mankind are of five kinds,

say the Teachings. First there is right

knowledge. Second there is wrong

knowledge. Third is fancy, fourth is

sleep, and the fifth is memory.

—Patanjali

One of the suns rose from behind the sandstone mesa to the south of them. It cast rust-red shadows, miles long, across the hard-packed sand. The sky was a washed-out indigo, like a soap bubble about to burst.

Ironwood bushes and sagebrush with tiny yellow flowers grew here. The light was melting the frost off them. An arroyo of bare sand snaked through the desert. Paloverde trees stood along it. Everywhere were crumbling outcrops of greenish shale that were spattered with lichens—bright orange and pale blue.

The brontosaur slept on the sand of the arroyo, inert as a great gray hummock of earth. Its legs were folded under it like a camel’s. Its neck was curled around itself. Its head lay on the sand like a yard-long shovel blade, all leather and bone. The right side of its jaw nested against its shoulder, the left side against the spine of the old woman. Its upper lip moved. The bristled nostrils on top of its head huffed out a warm breath that moved through the old woman’s long white hair.

The woman slept on her side, with her head resting on her sneakers, which rested in turn on a mound of sand. She had tucked her left hand between her cheek and her shoulder. Her dark glasses were folded in a pocket of her frayed green coveralls—orange lenses, copper frames, side screens of wire mesh.

Alex was asleep inside the woman. He cleared his throat and shifted his hips. He groaned and pulled one knee toward his chest. He slowly unbent his left arm, reached behind him, and felt the side of Naomi’s head. The morning was cold. It would be hours before the desert warmed up. Alex kept his eyes shut.

Eva was already up. She had the jeep parked next to a tangled stand of prickly pear. Chains of flat green lobes had struggled up through masses of wilted brown wreckage. All her solar panels were lifted on their struts, trapezoids of black silica angled toward the sun, catching the first light. A second sun rose—a brilliant star caught in a notch between two mountains. Eva watched the suns come up.

Three voices murmured like wind in the spaces between them.

A bowling alley? thought Alex. Back up. How did you get from the deep freeze to the bowling alley?

I was a cannibal spirit, thought Naomi. I must have got lost from a tribe of cannibals. A tribe of cannibal Eskimos from the arctic wastes.

I must have slept through that part.

Naomi rolled onto her back and stretched her legs. She wished that she had wrists to flex, but all she had today were ankles. She kept her eyes closed and listened to Eva talking to herself, up by the cactus.

She once told me that I had beautiful hands. We lay on our backs on the sand at sunset. My hands would dance against the sky, weaving webs. And my daughter would watch.

I still think you have beautiful hands, Naomi told her.

Shut up, thought Eva. No one is talking to you.

Naomi inched one eye open and blinked back her nictitating membrane. What was she this morning? A woman? A parakeet? An electric blanket? She rolled onto her side and lifted her stiff neck.

Alex propped himself up on one elbow and sighed. He stared at the dry creek bed in front of him. Grains of sand fell from his hair. He stood up, climbed the bank of the arroyo, pushed his coveralls down around his ankles, squatted, and emptied his bladder.

Naomi lumbered up out of the hollow, dipped her head, and ripped up a clump of sagebrush in her teeth. She stood and chewed it—roots, sand, and all.

I don’t remember these being here, she thought to herself. They must have grown in the night.

Eva adjusted the tilt of her photovoltaics. A servomotor purred for a moment. A hinge creaked. Eva pretended she was a giant black beetle warming its wings in the sun.

“Which way were we going?” asked Naomi, craning her neck to the east and speaking to no one in particular.

“West,” said Eva. “West until we find a road.”

“Sounds like a plan to me,” said Naomi. The brontosaur moved off across the desert, putting the suns behind her. She trampled the brush and the cholla cactus but walked around the paloverde trees. Eva spun her magnetos and felt her transmission fluid pressurize. She backed away from the prickly pear hedge, retracted her photopanels, and turned to follow Naomi.

Alex sat on a boulder for a while, rubbing the knots out of his neck and shoulders. He sat in the middle of an unreal desert, stranded in the body of his wife and wishing that he had some coffee and a cigarette. He looked at his hands. Long ago when he’d been flesh, he had made love to this woman, just once, and fallen in love with her forever.

My wish is granted, he thought to himself. Total possession of the object of my desire. From the inside out. I’ve died and gone to heaven. I wish I were dead. This could go on forever. It’s all in our heads.

I haven’t got a head today, Eva told him. And if you don’t can the early-morning self-pity, I’m going to barf my coolant.

White sand and gravel scraped pleasantly past Naomi’s belly. Eva’s tires rolled over the white sand and gravel between the prints of Naomi’s feet.

A jeep has no head, Alex had to agree. But a dinosaur does. I could believe in a dinosaur, but I don’t believe in Naomi. That brontosaur is a transparent delusion. She’s a refugee from a cheap Japanese monster movie. Look at the seams in that latex. Look at those baggy knees. And those mountains. There’s chicken wire showing through the papier maché.

Naomi and Eva crossed onto prairie land where oat grass grew thick on the rocky ground. After the foot pads and jeep tires had passed over, the flattened stalks would unfold themselves, crackling in the morning sunshine.

Alex began to scream. A half a mile away, Eva and Naomi groaned.

Do something, Mother. Go back and run him over.

Just ignore him. He’ll catch up.

He’s disgusting. I wish he’d have a heart attack.

Not with my heart, thought Eva.

It’s miniaturization, thought Alex. We’ve all been miniaturized for the special effects for a Lost Dinosaur Isle movie. It’s the climate that’s killing me. I’ve got maggots hatching in my clockwork.

Do you know what I like about Alex? thought Eva.

No. What? thought Naomi.

You don’t know either? That’s too bad. I’ve completely forgotten. There must be something I like about him.

Alex found his shoes, laced them on, and started across the sand, following the distant hum of the jeep’s motors.

Eva steered around the paloverde trees and bumped over the rocks. She stopped and rotated her turret optic. “There’s a road,” she said aloud.

Naomi lifted her head. Eva was right. Telephone poles and everything.

The brontosaur and the jeep climbed a gravel embankment onto a strip of asphalt that stretched to the horizon, running north and south. Naomi lay down on her side on the road. They waited for Alex to catch up. Eventually he lurched up the embankment with a green pop bottle in one hand.

“Civilization,” he croaked.

They moved south along the road. After a half hour or so, they came to a road sign. ARIZONA ROUTE 80. A while later they came to a sign that said: RODEO, NEW MEXICO, 3 MILES. Naomi stopped to rest and stood in the sun panting, with her tongue hanging out. She looked over her shoulder and squinted at the glare. She scraped one side of her head against her shoulder, rubbing the sty on her lower eyelid.

“I have a question,” she announced. “Why are there three different suns?”

“Alex,” said Eva. “Naomi has a question.”

“Three suns,” said Naomi. “How is that possible?”

“It’s not,” Alex told her. “Astrophysically they have no leg to stand on. Several possibilities arise. A. We’re all dead, and this is some cheesy afterlife purgatory. B. One of us is having a long involved dream or vision. C. The suns are a mirage induced by time distortion.”

“Time distortion?”

“Sure. If the three of us are in separate time frames, then we might conjure up three apparent suns in three positions. This would suggest a pocket universe.”

“We’re not in anyone’s pocket,” said Naomi. “We’re at the bottom of a well, eating treacle.”

“Well, I’m glad that’s cleared up,” said Eva.

“I’d like to bite someone’s head off,” said Alex.

“Not mine,” said Eva. “I haven’t got one.”

The three of them proceeded south, toward the buildings of a town.

“Where is the search party?” asked Naomi. “When are we going to be rescued?”

“They’ll rescue us when they want us,” said Alex. “They know where to find us.”

“What do they want from us?” Naomi whispered.

We’re a weapon, Alex told her. We’re a secret biological weapon that’s been hidden away. The government has stored us on a quarantine asteroid. We’re a classified virus that leaked from the plague industry and scared all the lab rats shitless. We had to be covered up. Because we’re too much too soon. We’re the telepathy factor and the cure for cancer. We’re a threat to modern disaster science. We’re immortal, Naomi. We’ve gone beyond death. We’re immortal, and the disease in communicable. You caught it in your tank of ice, and you made it communicable. Immortality is the ultimate disaster, Naomi. It never ends.

“Why did it have to be us?” asked Naomi.

Fate, Alex told her. Like the three Chinese brothers. One of them could swallow the ocean. One of them could hold his breath for three days. They all looked exactly alike. And do you know why? Because they were a monster. Not three monsters knotted together, no. One monster torn apart. But it’s no good coming to me for answers. There’s nothing left of me, Naomi. Just machine language and ghost rages. All things considered, I wish I were dead. But I’ve probably already been granted that wish, along with all the others. Iron turns red when it corrodes, and copper turns green. Meat turns to maggots, and thoughts turn to speech. Things are creeping up on me. In the first reel of “Frankenstein”, Fritz drops the jar. He drops the jar with the good brain, so he has to take the criminal brain. Everything turns to shit right from the start.

They came to a population sign at the Rodeo town limit. Naomi could see rows of houses. They passed a cattle ranch—barns with aluminum roofs, a mobile home, hay bales, irrigation ditches. Broken glass glinted on the shoulders of the highway. The cinder block buildings at the center of town stood flat and white, like overexposures, like shining ashes that the wind might blow down at any moment. A bar, a post office, and a grocery.

Ages ago, Naomi told herself, the desert lay under the ocean. The ants have taken over from the crabs, that’s all that’s happened. But the cactus remember the coral reefs.

The three of them walked into town. They’d encountered towns before. The towns were never inhabited. Once they’d found a derelict amusement park. Once it was a detention camp. Once it was the ruins of Aztec ziggurats. They’d crossed the mud and barb wire of trench warfare, and they’d crossed Nigerian millet fields overlooked by domed granaries. They’d seen slag heaps and meteor craters. But never a river. And never any rain.

In Rodeo there were Chevys parked in driveways and a flatbed hay truck with a horse trailer in front of the post office. There were cars with no wheels run into ditches. Naomi stopped in front of a boarded-up grocery store with gas pumps.

“It certainly looks like Rodeo,” observed Eva.

“You’ve been here?” said Alex. He sat down on the front steps of the grocery and shook out his shoes.

“I was hitchhiking up from Douglas. A horny salesman in a Volvo gave me a ride.”

Naomi began to recite sutras. The angas of the yogic marga are eight in number. The first of these being yama, that is, abstention. The second being niyama or observances. The third being asana, that is, postures.

Alex climbed the steps to a wooden porch and wiped at a dusty window. He cleared a patch of glass and exposed a sign in red marker pen. LETTUCE $2.95 EA.

“They sold lettuce here for two ninety-five a head,” he said. “That’s highway robbery.”

Naomi draped her neck over a gas pump. “I could use some lettuce,” she said.

Alex rattled the door of the grocery, walked across the porch to a wooden bench, dragged the bench to the door, and used it to break out the glass. He stood pulling triangles of glass from the door and muttering.

They built this place out of our heads.

I haven’t got a head, Eva pointed out.

Proves my point, Alex went on. They used it to build this place. Why are your nails so filthy?

Why don’t you clean them? You’ve got hands.

Where did you put the jackknife?

Look in your pockets, Alex. And pick the stones out of Naomi’s feet when you get done.

Fourth is pranayama, the breath. Fifth is pratyahara, sense control.

Eva, I think you have head lice. Couldn’t you take sponge baths?

I could if I was a sponge.

Naomi snickered. Sixth is dharana, concentration. Seventh is dhyana, meditation. Eighth is samadhi, contemplation. The first anga, abstention, yields five consequences. The first of these—

Naomi, Alex broke in. Must you recite that stupid Hindu garbage over and over again?

“Leave her alone,” said the jeep.

Alex turned to it. “I should break your legs,” he said. “You’re so damned selfish.”

He opened the door of the grocery. He couldn’t shut the others out of his head, but he could put some walls around him. He walked through the store, picking things up, turning things over. Dust had built a lush fur on the floor, the shelves, the cans and bottles. Auto tools hung on wire loops against the pocked plaster of a wall. Socket wrenches, monkey wrenches, pipe wrenches a yard long. Cardboard price tags on pieces of string. Artifacts of a lost civilization. A necropolis robbed of its mummies. Someone had stolen all the corpses.

Alex raised the aluminum lid of a vegetable cooler. At the bottom of a plastic bin, tomatoes and heads of lettuce had melted to puddles and dried to hard black stains. Alex let the lid drop, went to a rack of gum and candy bars, and stuffed the pockets of the coveralls. He found some paper bags and began to fill them. Cans of beans, tuna, corn, turbine oil, toilet paper. . . As much as possible he kept to products in the form of cylinders. He carried the bags to the porch, pulled out two quarts of oil, and walked up to Eva. She flipped her hood open.

All three suns were up now. It seemed to be the chalk-white sky itself that glared down so heavily. The telephone poles cast three sets of shadows on the asphalt and dry weeds. The day was going to be hot.

The brontosaur lay on her side on the gravel of the parking lot, panting like a patient hound. Eva sunbathed and gazed at the distant mountains, humming to herself. Alex sat on the porch of the store, eating beer nuts in the shade and muttering to himself.

Three sisters at the bottom of a well. Lived on treacle. Can’t have. Would’ve been sick. Were sick. Very sick. Axon, dendrite, and soma. Flame, breath, and rust. I think too much. I think too much, Naomi sees too much, and Eva feels too much. We’re living beyond all human levels of stress. We must be in hell.

Naomi retreated internally from the reasoned babble that was leaking into her head, and from Eva’s humming. Naomi knew of several avenues of escape from her family’s illness. Escape routes did exist.

Her favorite hiding place was the brontosaur’s hindbrain. To go there Naomi imagined herself as a little girl with yellow hair, who stood in a limestone tunnel with her fleecy thoughts wrapped around her as a shawl. She walked up the tunnel, which was limestone worn by water into the inner shapes of spinal vertebrae. The tunnel leveled out and opened into a spacious cave at the core of the brontosaur’s pelvis. At the center of the cave was a fat white armchair. The little girl sank into the chair and closed her eyes.

Light poured in through the bones. The armchair was a cluster of ancient gangliae, reptilian and wise. The voices of her parents were muffled by the thick walls of bone. Naomi held her eyes closed, feeling contained and yet set free, released into vast empty spaces.

Tik tak toe, three in a row, Naomi recited to herself. It’s a game for two, so I never get a turn. They play it on my skin. It isn’t fair. They’re always pretending. He’s the Steadfast Tin Soldier, she’s the one-legged ballerina, and I’m the fish that ate them. But really we’re all just a burnt lump of tin. It’s a sad old story. The princess loved the soldier, but the soldier loved the witch. And the princess lived alone in a tower of salt, alone with the angels, whose eyes are big as teacups, big as platters, big as cartwheels. I’ve forgotten the ending. I’ve lost the means to think. I should be floating down a river collecting twigs and crayfish in my hair. I should be dissolving in thick black mud. But a river is nowhere to be found.

Cradled in bright bone, snug in the dense gray seed pod of her hindbrain, Naomi silently shrank into a small square sheet of rice paper, which lay on the low lacquered desk of an old Chinese gentleman. The man picked up the paper in his long thin fingers and folded an origami bird. Naomi’s eyes looked over his shoulder, then wandered. Outside the paper walls of the old gentleman’s house, in a garden of moss and stones, a musician was playing a kyoto that had been fashioned from the shell of a turtle.

Naomi looked at the shell and saw the turtle coasting on an oceanic current, grazing rafts of kelp, living her life at sea. On the day of her death, she went ashore to lay her eggs. She clambered up white beach sand, over crab skeletons and fish husks to a dune where she buried her eggs. A beachcomber clubbed her dead and took her home to his wife, who chopped up her meat for a stew and sold her shell to a maker of kyotos.

Two of the turtle eggs hatched, and one of the hatchlings got down to the surf. But this hatchling wasn’t a sea turtle. Instead it was a throwback, a plesiosaur. The turtle isn’t a descendant of the plesiosaur, but the plesiosaur didn’t know that. And that plesiosaur was Naomi’s grandmother.

Naomi snapped out of her trance. The parking lot began to rotate beneath her. Abruptly someone sawed off the lid of her skull and poured scorpions and molten lead into her brain basin. Or so it seemed.

Eva! Naomi cried. Eva! Where are you? I don’t feel right.

The jeep was already beside her. It’s the brain tumor again. This is just what we need.

The brontosaur vomited foamy bluish lumps onto the gravel. Her belly heaved some more, but nothing came up. The flesh of her muzzle drew back from ruined purple gums and pitted brown teeth. Eva stood and watched, wishing that she could swab Naomi’s eyes with wet towels or stroke her brow. There wasn’t much a jeep could do to comfort a brontosaur.

Compassion, thought Alex. It’s a sickness. Encephalitis is a mercy by comparison. I have all their bad habits.

Alex still had Eva’s dipstick in his hand. He walked up beside her, replaced the stick, and slammed her hood shut. Then he surveyed the area, picked a side street, and walked up it, away from the highway toward the foothills. He walked two blocks east and turned right. Cracked and tilting sidewalks crossed plots of dirt that had once been lawns. Alex kicked down the handle of a rusted-out lawn mower.

Triads without a grid. Fragmentary case histories falling into ever smaller fragments. Exhausted assembly language. Tik tak toe, three in a row, a tack in a toe, and the tick makes it go. I lose, you lose, we lose. Snakes and ladders, tunnels and chutes, wormholes and sinkholes. Christ on a stick with raspberry frosting. How could I let this happen? I was a doomed steer on the blissful road to slaughter I had it made. Now I’m a permanent condition. Now I want to bite off the head of a jeep because she doesn’t love me. I give up. I surrender. Where do I go to surrender? No proper map, no orders, no fuel, no landmarks, no radio traffic, no steering, no brakes. . . Get it over with. Finish us off.

Eva turned a corner and spotted Alex. He was sitting on the front porch of a peeling pink bungalow. The roof of the porch had fallen in. Rusted window screens hung in flaps. Eva pulled into the driveway.

Naomi’s got a fever, she told him.

No doubt. Naomi’s got a fever, and I’m insane. What’s your problem?

I’m not happy, Alex. This desert won’t hold still.

Maybe it’s trying to get away.

I’d like to get away.

The old woman and the jeep fell silent. The wind moved a curtain in the pink house.

Alex shrugged. I’ve seen worse places. At least there aren’t any bugs.

Listen. Naomi is retching her guts up. Would you unstrap one of these water jugs and screw the cap off?

Sure, thought Alex. He stood up and did as Eva asked.

Alex, tell me something. What is it that eats at your silicon brain? What makes you so fucking mean?

Alex chewed at his thumbnail and blinked back tears. “You don’t like me,” he said.

Eva could see the shame and disgust gathering around his head like a dark cloud. He shivered and hid his face in his hands. It wasn’t his face. They weren’t his hands.

Something has to change, he whispered in his misery. I should destroy us all.

You’ve done that repeatedly, Mighty Warrior. I’ve killed you too, three or four times as you may recall. Euthanasia doesn’t appear to be one of our options.

Alex wasn’t listening. I’ll kill her. I’ll kill her, I’ll kill her killer, and then I’ll kill her child, while she begs for my mercy. Then I’ll forgive me and make love to her. Then I’ll kill her again. She’ll be sorry when she’s dead. And her daughter deserves to die with her. Because her daughter loves me just as badly as I love her. And that’s a cruel and unusual punishment.

She loves you like a father, Alex. When you die, she’ll carry your metal shell on her back. She’ll sleep nights on top of you, smitten with grief. She’ll hump your tires. She loves you, Alex. There’s no escape. I know these loyal bitches.

Alex went on muttering under his breath. He bent down and picked up a loose brick.

We can’t be the only ones locked up here, Alex.

Why not?

There must be other cells with other prisoners.

What if there are?

Escape, Alex. Sooner or later someone will escape.

How would that help us?

Someone could start a prison break.

Alex closed one eye and sighted down an edge of the brick. I wouldn’t count on it. Some prisons are escape-proof.

You have no imagination, thought Eva.

The jeep reversed down the driveway. Alex hurled his brick, but it fell short. The jeep bounced into the street and rumbled away, picking up speed.

You can run away, Alex told her. But a storm is coming. And after it hits, I’m going to give you a crown of thorns you’ll never forget.

Eva headed back toward the center of town and took a short cut down a littered back alley. Derelict swing sets stood behind fences of ocotillo stalks. She bashed aside a trash can to relieve her bad temper.

He’s evil, she complained. If only Naomi and I could cut him loose, remove him like a tumor, hold him in a pair of surgical tweezers over a stainless steel bowl, and drop him in, plunk.

All the while Naomi recited rapidly. For every action there are five lines of causation, the first being the site of it, the second being the doer of it, the third being the tools for it—

The wind dragged a thready haze of cirrus clouds across the sky. Alex trudged up a crooked path into the foothills. He passed yucca bushes and barrel cactus. The town was behind him, down below. He sat down on a stone and fell into a numb rage. Words cranked through his head like an endless bicycle chain.

If only there were somewhere outside of us, some way out. If only Naomi could die of her tumor. Eva would dehydrate, and I would rust in some peaceful gully, mission accomplished. But no. This place has a mind of its own. This place, this trap, this bedlam, this bardo state, this gnarled knot of nothing.

Sudden as the pounce of a cat, loud as a hammer on a steel barrel, Naomi’s voice rang out inside of Alex. Let’s get out of here! Let’s leave!

How? Show me how.

You have to pray, Naomi told him.

Pray? Get serious. Pray to what?

To anything. To whatever’s outside of this place. It doesn’t matter. Pray to the angels.

You’re delirious.

Pray to the angels, Alex! They came from the sun in the days before people. They’re hovering around us. Can’t you feel them? Warrior angels with swords of radiance. Their faces are so kind. They want to help us, Alex. Most of them do anyway.

Naomi, Alex thought tolerantly, you are so full of shit.

The jeep skidded to a stop on the gravel beside the sick brontosaur. Naomi gasped for breath. A bloody spillage was seeping from her cloaca.

Hello, Mother, Naomi thought faintly. Am I dying? Or am I just pretending? Will you bury my head in the sand when I’m dead, Mother? Will you dig a hole with your hands?

Naomi paused to listen. She heard a voice like an old Mexican woman at the bottom of a deep well. Naomi? Naomi, I brought you some water. Can you lift your head? Can you get your teeth around the neck of this jug?

The brontosaur groaned and thrashed its head and tail from side to side. Its muzzle clipped Eva's front bumper and smashed one of her headlights. Eva jerked herself back to a safer distance and slowly circled the parking lot.

She's really out of it now. I wonder whether ten bottles of aspirin would do her any good. I could mash them in some water and force-feed her the paste. No, I can't do any of that. I'm inside a jeep. Eva rammed the porch of the store to vent her irritation. A porch railing fell on her hood.

Naomi was howling like a dying sea cow. Hold me, Mother! Hold me! Don't leave me! Don't let me die!

The child's demands were unreasonable. There was nothing Eva could do. She backed onto the highway, turned her wheels, and drove south. She pushed her speed up to fifty miles per hour. The desert flew past in a blur. All three suns were roughly overhead.

Alex sat on his rock and held a cholla thorn in his right hand. He pressed the point of the thorn into the flesh of his left thumb. A drop of blood formed at the point of the thorn.

Alex jerked up his head and scanned the sky. He thought he'd seen a lightning flash from the corner of his eye. But the sky was clear, and no thunder came.

Because it wasn't lightning.

The hills and the little fake town grew dark, as though clouds were moving across the sun. The suns were dimming, like three bulbs on the same circuit.

Out on the highway, Eva sensed another seizure coming on. She had no idea how far she'd driven from Rodeo. She turned around and raced back toward town. But the storm was ready to break. If it dragged her into the sky while she was driving, the jeep would crash. So she braked, shut down her turbines, and stood in the middle of the road. The spiteful muttering from Alex got louder in her circuitry. Louder than gunfire, louder than bombs, it blotted out the world. The sky opened under her like the mouth of a dark dank well.

The jeep stood empty on the asphalt. A gust of wind moved across the desert. Eva was gone.

Naomi lay on the gravel. She couldn't move. Her head was squeezing her brain like a vice. Help me, Papa! I need water! I need medicine! I need lightning and chaos and healing. I need a silk ladder that I can climb through the roof of the world. I'm so tired of this smoke and filth.

Naomi fell into a bottomless black pit.

Hiding from me, thought Alex, thinking of Eva. Always hiding from me and laughing at me. I should plant her in the ground and see what grows.

Alex dropped sideways into nothing. Rodeo, New Mexico, was uninhabited again. The pit was full.

The old woman lay on her back beside a foot trail, her mouth open, her eyes on the sky, her face as blank as stone. Paloverde branches rattled in the wind. A lock of white hair blew across her eyes. She might have fallen to earth from heaven—an elderly Hispanic angel, lying stunned amid the sagebrush.

The brontosaur lay on its side by the gas pumps. Its breathing was shallow but steady. One eye stared unseeing at the Chiricahua Mountains to the west. The other eye regarded the Peloncillos to the east.

The jeep stood in the middle of Route 80. One more dead car in town.

The suns began to wander aimlessly in the sky. A fourth sun rose, then a fifth and a sixth—cold as moons and scarcely brighter than stars. Twenty suns or more cluttered the sky. They swarmed above the town like gnats. The sky darkened to a rusty black. The suns formed into clusters and swam in schools like minnows. Some of the clusters spun around a common center, like leaves in a whirlpool. Myriads of suns rose from behind the mountains, colorless and false, like planetarium projections.

Above the suns, above the sky, the sets and properties for the recent dreams of the three travelers stood idle. Up there a long-demolished plastics factory still clanked and hissed and dribbled gray-green smoke across an empty sky. Up there buzzards circled over freshly butchered meat at the apex of a Mayan ziggurat. Up there a bowling alley nested among the evergreen boughs.

But above all that, somewhere else entirely, a young man with long blond hair was walking through a glade of birch trees, across a crust of snow. He wore a white dress uniform which corresponded to the navy of no particular nation. The uniform included a visored cap, epaulets, and white silk gloves. The young man carried a leather briefcase. His slick black shoes crunched the dead leaves beneath the snow. He was tall and slim, and he was walking fast.

As the birch trees thinned out, snow began to fall, as fine as sifted flour. The man crossed an open field of dead oats. Cloud shadows sailed across the frosted stubble. The man made his way to a narrow shaft of white light. The snow hissed like radio static. The young man shook back his hair and squinted into the wind.

He spotted another shaft of light and made his way toward it. He walked through it and on to the next and the next. The birch trees were lost in the darkness behind him. The man in the naval uniform crossed a tundra of green moss that squelched beneath his weight. He crossed the green felt of an immense pool table, his heels ringing woodenly. He never slowed his pace. He entered a pavilion of marble pillars and walked out the other side.

He stood on a long green carpet, about five yards across. The carpet extended off into the distance. Ensign Wing resumed walking. He was going to visit Dr. Mazer, who was an angel, like himself. He wasn’t looking forward to the visit.

White noise. White ants on white sand slides. Snow blindness. A shift of attention.

In the sky over Rodeo, only three suns remained. They were converging over the town. As they fused, all the triplicate shadows of the telephone poles merged into singular shadows. Then they gradually split apart again.

Eva gave a start. She blinked in the glare. All the machinery had melted off of her. Her spine was arched uncomfortably across a stone. She rolled onto her hands and knees and felt the rubble beneath her palms. She was home again. Where was Naomi?

Eva walked into town. She called Naomi's name. The jeep came up the highway toward her, calling out in a digital version of Naomi's voice. Naomi sounded frightened. Eva tried to think. If Naomi was in the jeep, then Alex. . .

Oh no.

A serpentine shape rose from behind a house. It swayed like a cobra, with the suns behind it. Its eyes were rimmed with red, and it displayed its crooked teeth in a lopsided grin.

“All around the cobbler’s bench,” sang Alex, “the monkey chased the weasel.”

The brontosaur reared up and tried to crawl over the roof of the house. But his forefeet went through the tar paper. He yanked his feet free and bounded around the block, snapping his jaws. Eva was already running. Alex turned and landed a vicious kick on the side of the jeep, slamming it sideways like a toy. One of its fenders flew off. Alex threw back his head and roared at the sky.

Eva ran behind a tool shed and watched Alex charge off after Naomi. The jeep was too big to hide itself. Naomi would have to outrun him. Alex chased her out of town but lost her on the highway. He turned back and prowled the streets of Rodeo.

Eva peeked around the shed. Alex’s head poked over a rooftop. He was looking for her, drooling and spitting. Ducking out of sight, she knocked over a shovel. The monster turned its head toward the sound and chuckled. Eva began to run, hammering the hard earth with the rubber soles of her shoes. But she couldn’t catch her breath, and Naomi’s voice was shouting in her head.

I’m coming, Mother! I’ll help you!

No! Keep away from him! Run!

White noise. The crackle of static. A shift of attention.

The green carpet extended across a limitless wooden floor. On the wooden horizon there loomed a gigantic gray castle in the shape of a castle from a chess set.

Ensign Wing walked the green carpet until it was joined by a beige partition, eight feet tall. The partition extended, perfectly straight, to the horizon line where the limitless floor met a sky as white as paper. Wing walked beside the partition, on and on, until a series of gray steel doors began to interrupt it at regular intervals. In each door was a window of frosted glass.

A second partition joined the first, on the opposite side of the carpet, forming a hallway without a ceiling. Wing passed a marble urn full of white sand. He heard the chirp of a sparrow and looked up. Snowy tree branches interlaced above him. He sighed and resumed walking. He came to a door with printing on the frosted glass: DR. MAZER.

Wing faced the door with his head bowed and his free hand resting on one hip. He shook his head and walked a few more doors down the hall. He sat down on the green carpet with his back to the wall. He reached into a pocket of his uniform and took out a butane lighter and a fat joint of ganja. He sat and smoked, watching the rustling of the leaves. Then he looked up, and there was a ceiling of white fiberboard, with fluorescent tubes in pebbled fixtures.

Wing returned to the door marked MAZER and rapped at the glass. No answer. He tried the knob. He pulled a ring of keys from a trouser pocket, unlocked the door, and went in.

The office was dark except for the light that spilled in from the hall. Wing’s restless green eyes probed the darkness from beneath the visor of his cap. There was a wide oaken desk. There were bookshelves and a filing cabinet. At the edges of a Turkish carpet were ferns potted in large Chinese vases of blue porcelain. Hung on the walls were parchment maps mounted in chromium frames. A coffee machine stood on a low lacquered table and steamed. Three video monitors faced the desk from an alcove of white plaster set into one wall. Wing’s hand found a light switch and snapped it. Bright ceiling lights came on.

Sitting behind his desk, Dr. Mazer winced and put a hand over his eyes.

“Oh,” said Ensign Wing. “Sorry. Thought you were gone.”

“I’m always here,” said Mazer. “I was napping.”

“May I sit down?”

Mazer attempted a smile and gestured Wing to a chair. Mazer wore the appearance of a short thickset man in a white lab coat, with a bald head and thick eyeglasses. His nose was hawkish. His lips were thin and severe. Wing sat down.

“Good of you to come,” said Mazer. “You haven’t paid a call in quite some time.”

“No, I haven’t.”

“I realize of course that visiting an insane asylum is not your idea of a good time.” Their eyes met. Neither angel looked aside.

“Actually,” said Wing, “I’m here to make inquiries on behalf of Reverend Weaver.”

“How disappointing,” said Mazer. He touched a remote control and turned on the video screens.

The Ensign swiveled his chair and observed the screens, which all showed the same image. A prehistoric monster appeared to be demolishing a small twentieth-century town. A driverless jeep was ramming itself repeatedly into one of the dinosaur’s hind legs.

“I like the mountains,” said Wing. “Very realistic.”

“Care for some coffee?” asked Mazer.

“Thanks. I’ll pour. A cup for you?”

“Please.”

Wing went to the coffee machine, poured two cups, and carried them on saucers to the desk. Then he took a clipboard from his briefcase and studied the blank front of a file folder. He set the clipboard on Mazer’s desk and looked again at the monitor screens.

The brontosaur gnashed its teeth like a rabid camel. It frothed at the mouth, and a strand of bloody mucus swung from one nostril. Its eyes had gone white as milk.

“I assume,” said Mazer, “that the Reverend is concerned about the three lost souls on those screens. I only have three patients left in the place.”

“That’s correct, Sir.”

“I’m surprised at how many among us have opinions about my little clinic. I’m surprised that they discuss me behind my back but don’t see fit to visit the facility in person.”

“Perhaps they don’t feel welcome,” Wing suggested.

“They aren’t welcome if we’re talking about the Reverend,” said Mazer.

Wing sipped his coffee. This wasn’t going to be easy. “You may not believe this,” he said, “but Weaver is still a great admirer of yours.”

Mazer pouted. “But Weaver is critical of my treatment plan in this case.”

“Listen, Father, I’m only trying to open a channel of communication.”

“I’ll talk to you about the case. But Weaver can butt out.”

“Fine. Let’s leave Weaver out of the discussion, shall we? I’d like to talk about those three.” Wing aimed a thumb at the screens. “How exactly do you hope to achieve their rehabilitation through your. . . method?”

“My storm system. You want a progress report? No problem. I have no secrets here.”

Wing had heard that one before. His eyes went cold as ice.

Up on the screens, the brontosaur had his jaws around the jeep’s front bumper and had tipped the vehicle onto its rear wheels. The saurian kicked at its underside with his forefeet. The monitors faintly hummed.

“They certainly do like to fight,” said Wing.

“Yes. It’s a problem.”

An old woman in coveralls ran on-screen and pitched a sharp rock at the beast’s head. “Is that Eva?” asked Wing.

“I’m not sure. I haven’t been listening to them. One of the three.”

The brontosaur dropped the jeep and rushed at the old woman, who ran out of the image.

“So, Wing,” said Dr. Mazer, “what is your considered opinion of these three unfortunates? I say considered since you’ve been tapping those monitors for weeks now.”

Wing smiled modestly. “Offhand I’d say that they’re worse than ever. I think they’ve been deteriorating for the last several centuries.”

“Well, they are dead. It’s reasonable that they should deteriorate.”

“Hey, this is no joke, Dad. You’re fucking with human souls here.”

“I’m well aware of that.”

“And you’ve had them in treatment here for more than ten centuries.”

“And look at them,” said Mazer.

“Yes. Look at them.”

“Unhappily married. Hopelessly stagnant. Constantly snapping at one another’s throats. A manic father. A schizoid mother. A daughter moving deeper into autism.”

“And you continue to torture them with your storms,” said Wing.

“Torture is a strong word.”

“But it’s an appropriate word for torture. If this is therapy, it isn’t working.”

“You people seem to forget that Weaver sent me those three in the first place,” said Mazer.

“That was a long time ago,” said Wing. The two angels watched each other closely. “Why don’t you give them back to Weaver? You’ve already lost your professional reputation. I mean, everyone in the organization thinks you’re nuts.”

“What do you think?”

“I think you should cut your losses and put the three of them out of their misery. Blow out their brains and let the Wheels of Incarnation sort them out. I’d do it in a minute.”

“I’m sure they’d be gratified,” granted Mazer.

The two angels sat and drank their coffee. “I just wish that Weaver would leave me out of this,” Wing grumbled.

Mazer took off his spectacles, folded them, and slid them into the breast pocket of his coat. “You and I never did get along too well,” he said. He rose from his leather chair and paced the office. “But believe me, I know these three better than anyone. I know what program of therapy is correct for them. And I have no doubt of the eventual success of my system. For these three, the indirect approach is the only approach possible. I know it, and they know it too. They sense that I’m going to cure them.”

“They don’t even know you exist,” Wing objected.

“Oh, they don’t admit it consciously, but they know.” Mazer stood behind Wing’s chair and laid a hand on the younger angel’s shoulder. A note of pleading crept into his voice. “Listen, Wing, I know how this looks. No progress. My last three patients all messed up and stuck together. All of that is true. You don’t know the half. But don’t you see? Don’t you see, Wing? It had to be like this. I had to drive them to this state. There’s no other way forward. They need to be miserable. They need to be sunk in self-disgust. How else can they be motivated to climb out of their pit?”

Wing was stunned. His mind was racing. He finally got the picture. He hadn’t seen it coming, but he saw it all now. The other angels were right. Mazer had gone mad. He was over the edge, as far gone as his patients.

Weaver has to deal with him, he told himself. The situation here is impossible.

Mazer was sitting behind his desk again, pounding it for emphasis. “I’m telling you they’re going to pull through! Then we’ll see what Weaver has to say! Then we’ll see who’s crazy! Weaver and all the rest will be crawling to me on their knees and swearing that they never doubted me!” Mazer noticed that he’d upset his coffee cup.

A brown puddle had reached the Ensign’s clipboard and was soaking into his papers. Neither angel made a move to save them. Mazer’s face was flushed and sweaty. Wing lifted his clipboard, let it drip for a time, and shoved it into his briefcase. He stood up and edged toward the door.

“I’m really not qualified to judge,” he said. “I’m just gathering data. I’ll file a report, and that’s probably the last you’ll hear about it.” Wing shut the door behind him.

When Mazer had calmed down, he walked to the light switch and returned the office to darkness. The darkness helped him to think. He rubbed his jaw in the blue glow of the monitors.

The brontosaur had got the old woman’s head in its jaws. She hung from his mouth, dangling over the town. One of her arms hung brokenly. As the monster’s teeth clamped tighter, blood began to stream down the woman’s face and neck. The images had no color. The blood looked black.

Mazer looked away.

Up the hallway, beyond the pool table and the pavilion, Ensign Wing strode across an expanse of snowy moss. His briefcase was jammed under his left arm. He held a stick in his right hand and lashed at the drifting snowflakes with it. After a while he stopped and leaned against a birch tree. He reached into his jacket and found another joint. He searched his pockets for his lighter.

Falling snow. Hissing static. A wall of chattering video phosphors. The Brownian movement of black ants on black sand.

A shift of attention.

PART TWO

RELAPSE

CHAPTER SIX

THE FALLEN WOMAN

And when your back begins to smart,

It’s like a penknife in your heart.

And when your heart begins to bleed,

You’re dead and dead and dead indeed.

—Mother Goose

Eva was sleeping in a ditch beside route eighty, not far from Rodeo, New Mexico. Crickets chirped in the tall grass all around her. She slept soundly. She’d had a hard day.

She’d argued with Alex in the afternoon, and he’d crushed her skull in his jaws. After he’d killed her, he’d run off chasing Naomi. Luckily Naomi was in the jeep and was hard to catch. Eventually Alex had calmed down. Then he and Naomi had returned to Rodeo to look at Eva’s corpse.

After the suns set, Alex had curled up between two houses and fallen asleep. Naomi was parked beside him for the night, like a faithful dog. The day had left them all feeling drained and edgy.

Eva kept hoping that the moon would rise. Never any moon here. She lay in a ditch, deliciously exhausted and dead to the world.

Dreaming of better days.

I was wrapped in a sarong of green silk. I was standing ankle deep in the playful froth of the Caribbean. The white sand was littered with seaweed and alive with tiny crabs. In the warm water of the tide pools, barnacles breathed easily, raking plankton with their feathery radial arms.

No. No crabs. No seaweed. Tell the truth.

Xcalak. The Yucatan Peninsula. The twentieth century.

The fallen woman stood on a huge gelcrete wharf at the Xcalak dockyards. She’d walked to the seaward end of the it. Freighters towered up from the sludge to either side of her. The sun was setting somewhere behind the cloud cover. She leaned on a guard rail and looked out across the gulf.

The gulf was solid with orange scum, curdled with oil and awash in white suds. Rafts of filth. Gelatinous. Dead. Toxic. The corpse of Mother Ocean. The only movement was the flaring of the fires that burned on Her dead skin. Greasy brown smoke drifted laterally. The smell of Her rotting was everywhere in Xcalak.

The crust of the scum was so thick, you could walk on it. But eventually it would eat through your boots. And if you came to a soft spot, you slipped through. Like falling into a putrescent whale.

The huge steel ships had plows on their prows, and long steel oars for pushing them through the scum. The oars lay folded against their sides like behemoth insect legs. The murmur of the city seemed distant between their bulkheads. Cargo cranes stood on their decks, stark against the dying light. On the superstructure of a freighter, a starfish sailor sat in a sling and scoured metal.

And darkness was upon the face of the waters. And the Spirit moved in the darkness. But not in Xcalak. In Xcalak the Spirit was out of reach, hiding somewhere behind traumatized purplish-yellow clouds. Never any rain. Dry ditches baking in the heat. Smells of urine and garbage. Never any wind. The Chacs had deserted it.

Oh send us the Chacs again, wise Ehecatl! Send them back to us, we beg you, oh one-eyed father of the winds.

No one was listening. The fallen woman leaned on a railing, wearing her red dress. A black drawstring bag hung from her shoulder. She watched the fires at sea. The deck hand with the face like a sea star scoured metal.

She was seventeen years old. She’d been working as a whore for almost a year, in four different cities. She’d learned two professions among her people—priestessing and whoring. Whoring was honest work. It served a need, she supposed.

The streetwalking trade had taught her many new skills. She’d learned to sit in a train station and read a newspaper for hours. That was a skill. She could also recite her plight. Certain men required that a fallen woman tell them stories. They wanted to hear the sordid details of her recent degradations. They seemed to enjoy that, and the stories didn’t have to be true.

To look at her, you’d never imagine she’d been born an angelfish. She was wearing an assumed face. Illegal cosmetic surgery had provided her with the appearance of an anemone woman. Coral pink tentacles had been grafted onto her scalp. If the sailor in his sling were to turn and look down at her, he’d know she was a whore by the tentacles. You could spot her kind a block away.

Tired of standing, the fallen woman sat down on a mooring post. She arranged the loose ends of her sash across her lap. The white silk made rainbows, like an abalone shell.

She had never seen a rainbow or an abalone shell. All these words came later.

She sat and gazed across the surface of the ocean, a rusty orange scum. The clouds were a drapery of fumes that coiled over the sea like permanganate-purple intestines.

She thought about the orbital stations that her nation had constructed—silver milkweed pods whistling through the roasting cold sub-lunar vacuum. The winking of navigation lights in hard silence. The neat clean survival tests that confronted the conch men of the crews. An angelfish priestess in anemone drag sat on a post and wished that she were a conch man.

Yes, that’s how it was.

The fallen woman walked back toward the city, past canvas tarpaulins and iron cleats. Past towers of logs that were made of sponges, past bales of cotton that were grown from a white mold. The wharf passed over the harbor wall and over the foamcrete terraces that mounted to the level of the streets. She moved inland along the sidewalks, between warehouses of sooty brick. Above the roofs, scarlet rays shot through ragged clouds. The shadows of empty carts lay long on the cobblestones. There wasn’t a soul in sight. A maze of stone.

She passed a trench where the city’s plumbing was exposed. A chain-link fence enclosed the excavation. Earth movers crouched on their tires in the twilight, humped and sullen, among spools of gleaming cable. She came to a corner and stepped off the curb to cross.

A man’s voice addressed her, very close. “Miss?”

She turned to face him, her heart already pounding.

“I didn’t see you,” she said. Which was hard to believe. He was squatting in plain sight—armored like a tank and half as big as one. A crab man. A cop. She must have thought he was a bulldozer. He reared up onto his back pairs of legs and clicked on the floodlights in his belly. His glossy black shell was inlaid with Toltec designs of jade and quartz.

“Where are you going, Miss?”

“I was out on the dock. I went to look at the ships.”

“Where are you going?” he repeated.

“I’m going home now.”

“Where do you live?” She named a place. His puffy face was socketed into his thorax. He scratched his cheek with one of his claws.

“You shouldn’t be down here alone,” he told her. “It’s not safe.”

“I’ll be more careful,” she assured him.

“See that you are.”

If he knew that she was really an angelfish priestess, he’d kneel and kiss the hem of her dress. He’d escort her home, because angelfish didn’t walk among commoners. Angelfish weren’t allowed to run around loose. They lived in sedated abbeys, where there was never any trouble, and then they died. All except one—one priestess in the whole history of her people. That priestess didn’t die. She saved herself, and so she had problems—problems with the police, problems with customers, health problems, drug problems, nothing but problems. All because she wouldn’t do a simple thing like die.

The crab and the fallen woman stood and faced each other. She couldn’t go until he dismissed her.

“You can go,” he said finally.

She went. She made her way toward Xcalak’s central monorail station, past apartment hives and lumber mills and cotton factories. The logs were grown from sponges, but people called them logs. The cotton bales were made from white mold, but people called it cotton. None of this seemed strange to her at the time.

She passed a turtle man who pulled a cart of bundled newspapers. An octopus man in a yellow rain slicker and a coolie hat was sitting on top of the papers. The turtle stopped in front of a hive. The octopus climbed down and carried a bundle into the lobby. The turtle waited in harness. As the fallen woman passed him, he turned his head to look at her. It was possible to imagine a bored expression on his face. But people had lost the knack of reading one another’s faces. The mutations had taken them too far in too many directions.

The octopus returned to the cart. He showed no expression and had no head. By having no head he learned humility. By virtue of his having no head, other people learned toleration. These were the reasons given at the time.

The fallen woman passed snail mothers on their way to the shopping ziggurat. She saw snail fathers arriving home on rickshaws and ducking into their holes. Patio furniture on balconies. Bright black eyes on translucent stalks. Four snails to a family, always. Papa, mama, son, and daughter. They were born like that, in batches of four. Each family lived for five years. Then they died, all together, like clockwork.

But not the fallen woman. She was no snail. Genetically she was an angelfish. She wondered what kind of mortality program she was carrying. How had the priests set her clock? She’d been scheduled for dissection. Had they set her clock at all? Or had the priests, perhaps, left death out of her by mistake? So that she would live forever. By mistake.

She came to one of the canals that divided the city into hexagons. She crossed a jade footbridge. Iridescent insects swam on the blue water, whistling their familiar songs.

She crossed a long concourse and passed through a triangular arch into a black glass ziggurat. Inside was the waiting room of the monorail station—a vaulted space with rows of wooden benches. Knots of people sat on the benches and waited for trains. Krill men, lamprey men, manatee cows with their calves stuffed into strollers. Then there was the military presence, coming and going, never having to wait long for a train. Sharks mostly, naked and brightly tattooed, with their bony pricks preceding them and swinging from side to side.

They all wore masks, her people. They grew masks on the faces of their babies. But none of this struck her as strange. Perhaps the masks were meant to placate the angry spirits of all the marine life her people had poisoned.

Nereid men sat in corners with their begging bowls. Blind cave fish wandered the aisles, tapping the floor with their long white spines. An ancient jellyfish navigated his tubular walker from his bench to the corn dog stand. Yes, all the pariah clans were represented. Now that she was here.

The fallen woman walked among them. They saw her face, instantly knew her function. They looked but never touched. They knew the rules. In the years to come, their faces would return to her, like noodles swimming to the surface in boiling soup. Social arrangements sculpted in human flesh.

The men in power could explain their reasons for doing things. They did a thing because it was possible to do it. They’d try anything once. They felt that they owed it to Science.

The fallen woman walked down a flight of granite steps and made her way to her favorite bench, which was empty except for a newspaper. Perfect. She picked up the paper and sat down. She thought about skipping town and setting up in a different city. She had shells enough for a ticket. Why not? She took a capsule from her purse and swallowed it dry. She studied the wall-mounted schedule of departures.

She went into the women’s lavatory and stood at a sink. She looked into a spattered metal mirror, leaned over the basin, and splashed her face and her tentacles. She looked in the mirror again, at the second of the various faces she’d wear in her life. Her makeup was indelible. The skate doctor in Chetumal had done a good job.

He was a disreputable reconstructive surgeon in a seedy neighborhood. He had a snout like a serrated trowel and was as sexually bent as any one man could feasibly be. But he knew his trade. The fallen woman could still see the look on his face when she’d told him what she wanted done. He’d never had an angelfish in his office before, and the idea of turning her into an anemone was just too kinky to resist. And think of the challenge! He’d have to saw off half the poor girl’s face! Which is just what he did, after the question of payment had been settled. Sexual favors were the medium of exchange. The favors were granted during the weeks when her face resembled a volcanic crater.

Finally the bandages came off. She admired the surgeon’s work. She couldn’t recognize herself. Her new face’s most convincing detail was a small counterfeit scar. It looked like a healed knife wound. A pimp slash.

The skate doctor begged her to stay on for further surgery. He wanted to implant her with multiple orifices. He swore that such improvements would make her fortune as a hooker. She declined. She didn’t want to attract that much attention. Her face was perfect, but her identity papers were crap.

A pair of well dressed marlin women strolled into the lavatory, conversing in Nahuatl. They saw the fallen woman and stopped dead in their tracks. They turned to one another in significant silence. Then they walked out, speaking just loudly enough to be overheard.

“Are they allowed in here?”

“I had no idea.”

The fallen woman returned to her bench. She took off her shoes and rested her legs on the seat. She read the newspaper. In between paragraphs she studied the men in the waiting room. Watching men was part of her job. Men buying tickets, men standing in line, men from other cities stolidly waiting to go home. Only by long watching could you tell which man to approach, which to avoid. You had to open the conversation. You had to steer the exchange. You had to know what they wanted, even when they didn’t.

She read her newspaper. Construction on a new industrial dolmen. Satellite photos of petroglyphs miles across. Her people were leveling mountains and filling in canyons. They covered the land with a hardening scab of foamcrete, then picked at the scab until it spread and spread and spread. They spiced the foamcrete with the bones of discarded infants to ward off hurricane damage. They were preparing the Glory Day when all Tahuantinsuyu would be paved, from Uxmal to Copan, from sea to crusty sea.

Why did her people want to do this? Reasons were given. Hydroponics had eliminated the need for dirt. Dirt was a known cause of hookworm. Besides, it was a challenge. It was challenging to turn a continent into a slab of dead rock, chiseled with glyphs. To turn the earth into a moon.

The public address system announced a monorail’s readiness for boarding. A line of people formed in front of a locked glass door. They stood there together, jostling each other, burping babies, unsnapping and snapping up their luggage, taking out their tickets, pocketing their tickets, complaining to one another about the wait. . . The fallen woman hated them all.

She might’ve liked them if they’d sung like birds or hooted like monkeys, but they didn’t. They croaked like toads, or else they whined like poodles. Or else they wanted to stick their protrusions up your cracks and squirt their joy juice in you. The fallen woman wanted only to walk off into the desert, to fast and pray. But there was no desert, just vacant lots of foamcrete without a weed or a shadow.

She considered her alternatives. She could buy a ticket and ride a train. She could find a trick and make him rent a room. She could ride a shuttle to the shopping ziggurat and buy a new dress. She could spin a new dress out of cobwebs and old newspapers. She could spin a boat and sail it to the moon.

The fallen woman walked to an entrance vestibule with glass double doors at either end. To her right stood a broken gum ball machine and a metal chair for a shoe shine man. To her left, in a niche, there was a shrine to Our Lady Ixchel the Moon. There was a shelf for offerings and a bamboo mat to kneel on. On the shelf were dried flowers, vanilla wafers, and hexagonal copper coins with holes in them. There were also some notes on blue rice paper, folded very small. Prayers to the White Lady.

The fallen woman faced the shrine and knelt down on the mat. Behind her, people scuffled past, arriving and departing. She searched her purse for an offering. Cosmetics. News clippings. Prick-wraps. Breath mints. Speed. Sleepers. Nothing. She pressed her forehead to the cool bamboo mat. She offered up a prayer to Ixchel Mother of Death. She closed her eyes, and she was far from Xcalak.

Eva floated down a sewage canal, somewhere deep beneath the Quintana Roo medical complex. She had the face of an angelfish novice. She was dressed in the rags of a hospital gown.

The surgeons had wanted to kill Eva, and she’d fled from them. Somehow her path had led her to this buried canal, where she’d constructed a raft from bundles of PVC pipe. She rode the raft down dark slanting channels, rushing with the swift cold water. She was fleeing the scene of an atrocity, and the atrocity was simply that she’d fled.

The canal took a turn and bashed the raft against its wall. Eva slipped through the pipe bundles into water up to her chest. The roar of the current filled her head. The raft broke up around her.

Then she was in yellow light, moving beside a long cavity that was scooped from the canal wall, like the boarding platform of a subway. Half swimming, half climbing, she struggled toward its ledge.

She heard the shouting of men and the pounding of rubber boots on wet stone. Two men were running along beside her! Two sewer men in rubber overalls were reaching for the raft with gaffing hooks. Eva sank into icy muck. A hook snagged the sleeve of her surgical gown. A seam ripped, but the gown dragged her up onto the ledge.

The sewer men spoke hurriedly between them in a language she’d never heard. They spoke Spanish, as she realized later. They pointed their black rubber gloves at her face, her hands. They found her astonishing and disturbing. They fetched scraps of paper and wiped the filth from her eyes and her mouth. They helped her to her feet and made sure that no bones were broken. They were anglerfish men. Each man had a single milky eye and a luminous tassel on a stalk that grew from his head. Their mouths trailed long whiskers. They were pariah caste, the lowest of the lowly.

The sewer men led Eva to the mouth of a gelcrete tunnel, perfectly round. This was the way out. The men went back to their work. She started up the tunnel.

Glowing blue moss lit her way. Blue slime stuck to her feet and made them shine. She walked for hours. Sprinkler heads above her leaked out a mist. The mist settled slowly through the blue air, unfocusing vision. Droplets like sapphires clung to the shaggy walls. Then the air turned brown. Eva could taste the spores long before she arrived at the mushroom farm.

She came to a dim cavern. It was filled with racks of soil, hanging rubber hoses, and steel tanks on rusty scaffolding. Growing in the nubbly petro-soil were clusters of plump white mushrooms. She ate a few of them and drank some water that trickled from a hose. She climbed onto a rack and fell asleep.

When Eva woke, she found her way from the first cavern into another and another, all alike. Then she came to a cavern where no mushrooms grew. There were murmuring pumps and spherical amber-colored tanks on aluminum stilts. The floor was black pumice. A bare bulb cast grids of shadow onto puddles of standing water. She hit her head on a pipe and cursed.

A gentle voice spoke beside her in the dimness. “Be careful,” it said. “You’ll hurt yourself.”

A very fat old woman with long white hair hung upside-down and watched Eva from kindly eyes. The old woman’s arms were crossed beneath her spherical amber belly.

The old woman was a storage tank. Eva looked around. All of the tanks were women. She’d walked right past two of them. They chuckled at her surprise. She sank to the floor, feeling dizzy.

“You seem undernourished,” observed the nearest of the clam women.

“You can’t live forever on mushrooms,” said another.

“Do you like milk?”

The clam women fed her on milk for days. They passed the milk down into their throats with a swallowing sound. Then Eva kissed them, and the milk, sweet as honey, slipped from their hard brown snouts into her mouth.

But the days spent underground passed, and Eva found herself far from the mushroom farm, stumbling up the side of a ditch into the tainted sunlight of Tahuantinsuyu. She walked beside a roadway for a day. She wore a plastic bag. Her lips cracked from the sun. Carts went past her all day—ridden by men, drawn by men. No one stopped for her.

She walked a second day, hoping to find a city. Her fingernails split, and her nose leaked. She stared at the foamcrete at her feet. The earth became a lump of burnt pudding, blackened dough stuck to a bright metal pan. A cartwheel had rolled it flat. It tasted of sawdust and brandy. The sky hung in shreds from rusty thumbtacks. Too much laundering in filthy rivers. Wound on a stick and beaten against a rock. Caminé y caminé.

All the while the fallen woman knelt at the shrine in the monorail station. Rising to her feet, she stole a vanilla wafer from the shelf of offerings.

Out on the street, chewing the wafer, she walked without direction. She walked and walked and watched the wary eyes of the passing men. Encontré el camino a traves de la cuidad.

Some of the men sized her up. But none would meet her gaze. Wasn’t anyone hungry tonight? Wasn’t anyone desperate? Nadie me miró a los ojos. No se escuchó ninguna voz.

And all this was as it should be. Because she was one of the chosen. Because she was born to be forbidden and revered. She could heal the sick or contaminate the healthy with sanctity. Her people built altars to her, and spat on her, and lit candles for her, and burned her at the stake, and died for her, and killed her children. Because that was what they were used to.

She wandered the paths of a plaza where the pavement was set with mosaics of turquoise and mica. She walked across icons of the Whale Emperor and his concubines. She sat on a stone bench beside some yucca plants.

A double file of tunicate children straggled past. A squid nun in a long black habit was leading them home from a field trip. Their heads were smooth and shiny. Their eyes were like little black buttons. They pushed each other and whispered, with pebbles in their shoes. They all looked so achingly well cared for. They all had beds to go home to. They all had legal names. The fallen woman wanted to stroke their heads. Helplessly she watched her hand reaching toward them.

Instantly the nun turned and flew at her, glaring and shaking her fist and screeching at the children.

“Get away from her! Don’t let her touch you! That’s a whore woman! A filthy whore!” The nun swept the children away. They had to run to keep up.

The fallen woman left the plaza. She walked the lamp-lit streets, tracing their hexagons. The curfew sirens sounded. So many problems. And all because she’d run away from the surgeons. It could have been so easy. But no. She’d wanted to live. So she’d flushed her sacred heritage down a toilet, and here she was. In a cesspool. In a labyrinth of stone.

On and on she walked, into the night, over planks and tarpaper and broken slabs of gelcrete. On she walked, dreaming of rivers and pampas and jungle. She walked wishing for a sand dune, a snow bank, a pile of dead leaves, something soft to lie down on. She walked wishing she were asleep. ¿A donde podria yo ir? In a labyrinth of stone. In a coral reef. In a termite mound. In the land of her people who had lost their human faces. En el lugar donde nacir. En un laberinto de peidra.

Recite your plight, recite your plight. In the dying light, recite, recite.

She suddenly heard the legs of a crab cop clattering toward her. She broke and ran, through tilting shadows, toward the dockyard, toward the sea.
