A couple of inspectors pried me off of him. As soon as they let go of me, I fell to my knees and retched. I retched directly into the toilet bowl in the open cabinet. Sometimes a thing is right there when you need it.

I stood up, wiped my mouth, and walked away. I found a dark corner of the loading dock and wept. I was making a fool of myself. I had no Quest. I was just a dumb Polack with delusions of sainthood.

I watched from the shadows while freight handlers prepared a shipment of Autisticons for transport. They wrapped each box in a sheet of foam and fastened the foam with copper straps. They nailed together fiberboard crates that held the Autisticons snugly. They wrangled the crates across metal ramps into the bellies of the waiting trucks.

I walked over to the trucks. Down the narrow corridor between two of them, I could see Wrightwood Avenue baking in the summer sun. A strip of scraggly crabgrass grew between the sidewalk and the curb. A little yellow butterfly flitted past. I waited until no one was around and walked across one of the ramps into the van of a truck. I was going to visit an elementary school. It was part of my job.

The crates were so tall, they nearly reached the van’s silvery inner roof. But there was just enough room for me to crawl in on top of them. I lay on my stomach across two of the crates. I rested my chin on my arms and waited.

A scrape and a clank. Someone had moved the loading ramp. A squeal of hinges. Blackness. The slam of the driver’s door. The vibration of the engine.

The truck lurched, paused, turned left, shifted gears. Braked at the Pulaski Street stoplight, turned north, and rumbled over the potholes. The crates jumped beneath me.

Onto the expressway. Air keening past. I was on my way. Through corn country and cattle land. Across the dusk and into the night. Going west. Whenever I started seeing things in the dark, I’d take out my lighter and strike a flame. There in the steady blue glow would be my hand. There were the crates. There I was.

I turned onto my side and fell asleep. It felt like falling too. Like falling into a deep dark well.

I dreamed that I was a soldier in basic training, running an obstacle course. Bare trees beneath an overcast sky. Frosted brown earth that crackled beneath my boots. I jogged along, huffing out steam. All by myself. Perhaps I was being punished. My fingers and toes were numb. I crawled under some barbed wire and kept moving.

I came to a barricade built of railroad ties. I climbed a rope to the top and threw my legs over. Then I got a surprise. The barricade’s far side was slick aluminum. I lost my grip and fell, feet first, down a steep chute like a playground slide.

The chute leveled out and sent me spinning across the ice of a frozen pond. I slid to a stop on my belly. The ice felt thin. A blackbird flew past, scolding me. I tried to creep toward shore. The ice groaned and broke. I sank into the pond up to my armpits. My hands scrambled uselessly at the ice.

Night fell. A sheet of new ice formed around my chest. The pond held me firmly in its jaws. I was in no further danger of drowning. The stars came out.

An army jeep came toward the pond, blinding me with its headlights. It drove straight across the ice and stopped in front of me. The glare hurt my eyes. I waited to be rescued. But the jeep just sat there, idling its motor. Finally it honked its horn. It wanted me to get out of its way.

“Go around!” I yelled at it. “Go around! You’ve got the whole pond!”

I prayed for release. I woke up, fumbled for my lighter, lit another cigarette, went back to sleep. The air brakes hissed. We’d come to a weigh station at the Missouri border.

Here the aged film of the dream scorches and snaps. I lose the visuals, but the soundtrack proceeds.

I woke up coughing. There was a lot of smoke and a smell like burnt pork chops. Apparently the fiberboard crates were flammable. Apparently I’d barbecued myself. Luckily it happened at a weigh station. As soon as they found me, someone called an ambulance. Amidst loud expressions of anger and disgust.

I wondered what I looked like, as I rode to the hospital. I’ll never know, because the ambulance wouldn’t show me a mirror, and because my eyes were gone. Boiled like two little green onions. The ambulance was a real moron. It wouldn’t shut up. It told me this long involved joke about a pet poodle that gets put in a microwave. What a creep.

I wouldn’t have been so sensitive if I could’ve stopped thinking. Even at times when I wanted to stop—when I was char broiling myself for example, or when mutant cicadas were chewing out my nerves—I’d go right on thinking, as if there were no tomorrow.

At the hospital I was wheeled on a squeaky gurney through hallways that reeked of mint. A radiologist with a stuffy nose fed me stale water from a paper cup. A fluoroscope buzzed. The radiologist turned me over and shot another exposure.

The nurses took off my hands and bandaged me from head to foot. For days I did nothing but lie in a bed and pee into a catheter, filling up little plastic bags. Then my kidneys gave out, so they detoured my renal arteries into a noisy dialysis machine.

Being blind required some adjustments. The first few days I tried desperately to remember what various things looked like. To fix them in my mind before they faded forever. But the more I tried to remember, the more I forgot. Eventually I gave up.

When they took off my bandages, the skin went with them. The dermatologist had planned for this. He’d special-ordered a batch of new skin from a pharmaceuticals concern in Boston. It itched at first, but I was glad to have it.

When my heart failed, the cardiologist had a new heart ready for me. And while they had my chest open, the hepatologist replaced my liver. New kidneys were still on order from a kidney shop in Amarillo. The food was great, and my job insurance was paying all the bills.

When my visceral condition stabilized, the ophthalmologist connected up a new pair of eyes. Everything looked pastel—pearl grays and bleached aquas, the colors of bread mold. But hell, they were eyes. I’d get used to them.

Then they gave me counseling. I needed a lot of therapy, because I was a very disturbed individual. I had a lot to learn about channeling my self-hatred into socially acceptable channels. But my therapists had great faith in me. If I worked hard, if I improved myself, I might someday perform a useful function. I might even become a delivery van or a dump truck. Being helpful to others is so important for rehabilitation.

But the crucial thing in life, I feel, is to become more and more like a machine as you go on, and less and less like a person. I tried to explain that to the two of you the day I met you. I told Naomi when we were shuffling out of that bowling alley. I told Eva when the three of us were hiding in the bushes behind that laundromat. Flesh is too wet, too sticky. The fact that Naomi could absorb us is a perfect demonstration of that. You have to become a machine if you want to escape all that.

When the hospital discharged me, I went back to my rat hole apartment on Humboldt. All of my antisocial behaviors had dropped away like rotten fruit. I could be friendly with people. I could tell them jokes and put them at ease.

The first night I slept in my apartment, I had another dream about Felix and his factory. Shall I tell it to you, before I sink into the alpha waves?

It begins with Felix sitting across a desk from Ivan. Felix clutches his sinister black box to his chest. Ivan rises from his chair, smiling vaguely. A crowd of workers drift into the frame, surrounding Felix, blank-eyed and gaunt. At last Felix understands. The factory is run by zombies, by the walking dead. They would have killed him long ago, but they’ve only just realized that he’s alive.

They chase him through the factory, slavering and braying, past an iron foundry and showers of sparks. There are gas explosions behind him. The factory is falling in on itself. Felix sees an exit door and dashes out into the sunshine. In sunlight this bright, the dead workers can’t touch him.

He’s standing on a windswept desert of fine white sand. A dust devil spins in the distance. A storm blurs the horizon. He turns around to watch the exploding factory, but it’s vanished without a trace. He stands and stares. He feels someone watching him.

He turns again. He’s face to face with great gray brontosaur. Her head hovers a yard from his, regarding him with one calm secretive eye. She speaks.

“You’re a stubborn man, Father. We go around and around, just to please you.”

Title card: “WHO ARE YOU?”

“You tell me,” says the brontosaur. “It’s your movie.”

Title card: FELIX HAS THE SINKING FEELING THAT SHE IS MAKING SENSE.

“How many times are you going to drag Mother and me back to this horrible place?” she asks him.

Then it’s not him anymore. It’s me, Alex. I’m wearing the jeep and standing on the desert under the three suns.

“Am I still dreaming?” I ask Naomi. Slowly and sadly she shakes her head from side to side.

“You shoe is untied,” she tells me.

I look down, but of course there aren’t any shoes on my tires.

When I look up, I’m alone.

CHAPTER EIGHT

THE KIDNAPPING

But he was very stiff and proud:

He said “You needn’t shout so loud!”

And he was very proud and stiff:

He said “I’d go and wake them, if—”

I took a corkscrew from the shelf:

I went to wake them up myself.

—Lewis Carroll

Where was I? Oh yes. At the bowling alley. I swallowed Papa. My nerves got outside of my skin.

It wasn’t a nice thing to do. My nerves crept into Alex like little yellow worms, and they sucked him right up like spaghetti.

When we stumbled out into the drizzle together, we were pretty disorganized. Two hearts, four lungs, extra arms hanging off, Alex’s shirttail folded inside of us... But we made it to his car without falling down and stuffed ourselves inside.

I stripped off his clothes and swallowed what remained of him. We sat there on the dark parking lot, expecting police sirens at any moment. The rain muttered on the asphalt. We were sweaty and uncomfortable. We must have weighed three hundred pounds. My coveralls and bowling shoes could barely hold us. It wasn’t my fault. Most of the weight was Alex.

“Nice car,” I said. “Kind of small.”

Alex wouldn’t talk to me. He was sulking. I pulled us methodically into a human shape. Two ears, one nose, a thatch of rust brown hair. . . Alex’s cleft chin and unhappy mouth. My green eyes and pug nose. And enough blubber to see an Eskimo family through the six-month night.

I fished Alex’s gooey pants from the floor mat. I got his wallet and car keys out of the pockets. I started the car and drove toward the center of town. We passed a fire station and a chain saw store. We passed the fence of a graveyard where willows dripped on moss, and mounds of pine needles sparkled and went dark again.

I turned on the car heater. The slapping of the wiper blades was a cozy sound. The roadbed looked like a gray sponge cake. The hood of the car wobbled like lime gelatin. Gas pumps, mail boxes, and propane tanks slumped past. A used car lot, a drug store, an insurance agency, everything put to sleep for the night. As for me, I was hungry. I’d just eaten a man whole, but it had only made me hungrier. My belly felt bloated and empty at the same time.

I noticed a hairy pink scrap of meat beside the road. I pulled over and walked back along the road shoulder until I found the dead possum.

One beady black eye stared up at nothing. Brown blood was crusted on its snout and whiskers. Guts had spilled from its belly. I pushed it with the toe of my shoe, then hunkered down beside it in the mist. It would’ve made a fine meal, except that it was dead. I scooped it up in my ten fat fingers. I cradled it on one arm and unzipped the top of my coveralls. I held it against my chest, rocking to and fro, urging my nerves to enter it.

They went inside, but it wouldn’t come to life. Something went wrong. The dead thing trembled and kicked. I got scared of its little white teeth. I dropped it to the pavement.

The possum’s skin fell open like a sun-baked trash bag. Strips of it slithered like grass snakes into the tall weeds. A liver and two lungs yanked themselves free from the ribcage and humped away like wet inchworms. Muscles disentangled and scattered. Even its bones crept away. Each separate tissue fled from the others and hid in the weeds, to die distinct and alone. A pretty good trick, I thought. But it didn’t make me any less hungry.

Then I saw a worm that was drowning in a puddle. A big brown night crawler, slowly squirming. I plucked the worm from the rainwater and set it on my tongue. It tried to burrow into my soft palate, so it let it. It pulsed itself inside me. It was alive and delicious. But I needed more.

I squeezed behind the steering wheel again and kept driving, looking for a motel, not finding one. I craved a hot shower. Then I passed a laundromat. I did have some clothes that could use washing. I had Alex’s clothes, wadded up on the floor mat, all slimy and bloody. And there were more loose clothes on the back seat. I circled the block and parked the car at the curb. I got out holding the clothes bundled against my stomach. I kicked the car door shut and pushed through the door of the laundromat into warmth and humidity.

The room was crowded with washers and driers. Against the wall on my right, a row of plastic chairs was bolted to the floor. At the back wall there were vending machines, a bulletin board, and a door into a rest room.

There were four people—a teenage girl about my age, her boyfriend, an older woman, and a little boy. They all seemed to know each other. The girl was pulling wet clothes out of a top loader and slapping them down in a wire cart. She wore a leather jacket over a lacy blouse, and her hair was dyed orange and twisted into pigtails. The boyfriend sat on a chair in torn jeans and a T-shirt. He was sipping a can of soda and watching a coin-op TV that was built into his chair.

The little boy was pushing a cart around and around a row of washers, as fast as he could, bashing into things. His mother of the boy slumped in another chair, in a shapeless purple sweater, reading a magazine. Coats hung on backrests.

The teenagers turned their heads as I came in, and stared at me. Then they looked away. I went to the nearest washer and dumped in my armful of clothes. I thought I’d better get some detergent from a vending machine. That’s the way it’s done after all. I didn’t want to attract attention. I’d ditched the Reserve Corps jeep, and I didn’t fit my description, but still there was no point in taking chances.

I made for the vending machines, stepping over people’s feet as I went. The little boy came around a corner with his cart and stopped. For an awkward moment I blocked his path, and he blocked mine. He ran to the opposite side of the cart and pushed it the other way.

I fed quarters into a slot and pulled a handle. The vender coughed up a packet of laundry soap. I walked back to my laundry, poured the soap over it, shut the washer lid, and fed more quarters. As the washer filled with water, I sat down in a chair. I pretended to be half asleep. But I was very much awake.

The boyfriend threw his soda can into a trash basket. “They changed the movie at the drive-in,” he said.

“That’s too bad,” said the girl. “I wanted to see that one they had. What was it called?”

“The Hodag.”

“Did you see it?”

“I see them all.”

“Any good?”

“I kind of liked it. Splatter movie basically. But the story happens in frontier days. Mountain men of the frozen north, trapping for pelts, panning for gold and all that.”

“Sure.”

“Well, this one winter there’s this blizzard. And this guy who’s a beaver skinner or some shit is living up in the hills with his weird old lady and their kids. But they get snowed in.”

“Uh huh.”

“So they’re starving up there, and then they run out of firewood, and they all freeze to death. Except that the ten-year-old daughter doesn’t die, because she eats some of her mother and her brother, and this brings down an old Indian curse on her.”

“She’s a cannibal? That’s disgusting.”

“Don’t interrupt,” said the older woman, putting down her magazine.

“So this evil spirit possesses her,” the boyfriend went on, “and she turns into the Hodag. This medicine man explains the whole thing after she’s done in a couple of whiskey bums and saloon girls. She sucks their brains out with her—”

“I don’t want to hear this.”

“Sandra,” said the older woman. “Would you let him tell it?”

I felt a draft on one of my ankles. My coveralls were riding up my shin. I reached down and felt a growth sticking out of my leg. I squeezed it through the cloth. It wiggled. It was toes. Three big hairy toes. Alex was trying to sabotage me, the bastard.

Letmeoutyousickbitch! Spitmeoutyoudamnedmonsterbitchfromhell!

Scream all you want, I told him. No one hears you.

The toes went mushy and slipped back inside the leg meat. The little boy was opening and slamming the door of a drier, again and again. Click, slam, click, slam. . .

“So how does it end?” asked Sandra.

“The movie? Oh, a bunch of ghosts show up. The ghost of the girl’s parents and all the people she killed come after her.”

“And do what?” asked Sandra.

“Oh, drag her back to hell or wherever.”

As soon as I got my skin smoothed out, an itch like a peeling sunburn started up between my shoulder blades. It was Alex again. He wouldn’t hold still. I jammed my spine against the back of the chair. What was he poking out now? Something bony. One of his wrist stumps. I squirmed in the chair, pushing on it. He was yelling inside my head. I held my head in my hands. It wasn’t the right shape anymore.

HelphelpI’mtrappedinsideacrazywoman!

There were bulges like blood blisters under my hair. He was leaking out all over. And all the while a newsman on the television was droning on about some local kidnapping. Kidnapping or murder, the police couldn’t decide. It seemed that an unemployed machinist from Illinois, a Mr. Somethingsomething, had been assailed and dragged from a bowling establishment in the quiet rural community of Whatever in central northern Nevermind less than an hour ago. Witnesses were unclear as to—

“Hey, listen to this!” said the boyfriend. “Something here in town made the news.”

I had to get out of sight. I walked carefully to the back of the laundromat, praying that no one would notice my swellings. I ducked into the bathroom and locked the door behind me. I sat on the toilet, wishing I were dead. I needed a quiet motel room and three days of sleep. I had to digest Alex, or he’d split me in two. I listened to the rain on the roof. It was pounding down hard. The bulges moved around under my hair. I leaned against the flush tank and banged the back of my head against the wallboard.

Get in! Get back in! Stop yelling at me! It isn’t my fault!

I couldn’t control him. When I squeezed him in, he just poked out from two other places. His knees were under my arms. His elbow was under my chin. His eyes were bugging out under my tongue!

I stood up and saw myself in the mirror over the sink. I didn’t know whether to laugh or cry. Alex’s fingers were sticking out all over my head. There must’ve been twenty of them. Where was he getting all these fingers?

My coveralls were strangling me. I unzipped them, tugged my arms loose, and rolled them down my flabby white legs. I needed to be out in the rain.

I lurched through the bathroom door. Magically everyone in the room was on their feet, staring at me.

The little boy ran to his mother and hugged her knees. “Mommy?” he whined. “What’s wrong with the fat lady?”

Sandra and her boyfriend were backing very slowly toward the exit. The boyfriend was gagging like he wanted to vomit, but he couldn’t tear his eyes away from me.

I only wanted to run to the woods and hide. But the people were blocking my way. Sandra’s teeth began to chatter, as though she were walking backwards through ice water. Then she started to laugh. Then she went stiff, because the sound of her laughter had startled her. I hobbled toward her like two contestants in a three-legged sack race.

“Y-you sh-shouldn’t laugh at f-fat people,” I told her. “It isn’t n-nice.”

“Leave her alone!” shouted the boyfriend, advancing on me. He grit his teeth and pushed me, hard, in the shoulder.

“Don’t touch it!” Sandra warned him. Too late. My hand was around his wrist.

Thirsty nerves slithered from my fingers. My freak mitochondria scrambled up his arm, but only as far as the elbow. They loosened cartilage and sawed notches in ligaments, like silent saboteurs undermining a railroad bridge. After they did their work, one swift tug would break his arm clean in two. I was hungry, but I mustn’t eat all of him. I had to save room for desert. I put my free hand on his chest and pushed him away from me.

He stumbled back, and my right arm was hanging from his wrist. It came right off my shoulder. I lost my balance and sat down on the linoleum. The boyfriend looked down at my detached arm.

“Aaaaaaaa!” he croaked. “Get it off of me!”

I got up onto my hands and knees, feeling dizzy and sick. I had two hands on the floor, two hands holding my head—No, that can’t be right. I’ll start again. I threw up all over the floor, but not from my mouth, straight from my belly, which fissured and spewed small intestines. I had two hands trying to stuff Alex’s guts back inside me, two hands on the floor, two hands holding my head... I thought I must be dying.

But I also thought that if I ate someone, I’d feel much better. I looked into the eyes of the mother. She was staring at me as though my face were sprouting maggots. They weren’t maggots. They were clean brown earthworms, smelling the air and inching out of me in bundles. Some of the worms were huge. Giant Brazilian night crawlers. I’d only eaten one worm, but one is all you need to run off copies.

“What are you s-staring at?” I screeched.

“Call an ambulance!” pleaded the boyfriend. “It won’t come off!”

“Kill it!” the mother blurted. “Somebody get a gun and kill it!”

“Try it!” said a mouth in one of my shoulders.

“Yeah! Try it!” added a mouth in one of my thighs.

Just like people to kick you when you’re down. I hoped she would kick me. I’d suck her in like quicksand. I tried to stand up, but there were too many limbs to keep track of, and my silky brown hair kept falling in my eyes.

Sandra grabbed her boyfriend’s elbow, and out the front door they ran. Lightning flashed above the parking lot. Two had escaped me, but two remained.

“Wh-what’s y-your name?” I asked the woman.

“Dora,” she told me, and made a dash for the door, dragging her brat behind her.

All at once I was across the room, blocking the door. I think I must have leapt there like a frog.

Dora came at me with a carving knife. She must have had it in her purse, to help her feel safe at night. What a woman! Defending her young with stainless steel! She stuck the bright blade into me and twisted. Some of me did spill out. But just enough to make me even hungrier.

Dora let go of the knife, recoiled from me, and nearly tripped over the boy. “Get back!” she shouted at him.

She was perfect, this Dora. I wanted to gobble her up with cherries on her nipples and mints between her toes. I pulled the knife out of my neck.

She and the brat made a second rush for the door, and this time, she did trip over him. Seeing my opening, I pounced and landed on her back. The door banged shut behind the boy.

I wrestled with her and tried to pin her down. Dora got her foot against my belly and shoved us apart. She wriggled away from me until her back was pressed against a washer.

Her lips moved silently. But her eyes were empty. She seemed to have fled the laundromat and left her body behind.

I tried to think. A worm crawled up my cheek and tried to squeeze in under my eyelid. I hooked it away with one finger, and it buzzed its wings in protest. There were pairs of powdery beige wings all up and down its back. Other worms had budded out gardens of feathery antennae. Moth worms. I’d raised a crop of moth worms. They weren’t my idea. They’d just popped out. I was basically a sensible girl. I just needed a good woman to straighten me out. Yes, a good woman to lay on top of me and straighten me out and hold me together.

My washer shifted into its rinse cycle.

I crept toward Dora. I tried to calm down. I didn’t want to rush her. There’s a good way and a bad way to do these things. I knelt beside her and put some of my arms around her. We’d be happy together, the three of us, Dora and Alex and I. Years from now we’d laugh about today.

I rested my head on her shoulder. I felt so damned fragile. I felt as though I were standing on the edge of a cliff beside the ocean, and any gust of wind might send my flesh tumbling into a cloud of moths and dragonflies, scrambling ants and squirming eels. But no wind reached me, there in that cramped and stale-smelling laundromat. So my flesh stayed hung on my bones.

I needed her. I was starving. Dozens of unheard-of new glands were erupting in my brain stem. I wasn’t consulted.

Dora wouldn’t look at me. She was on some other planet. I pounded her shoulder with my forehead. Tears spilled from my eyes. But Dora was dead to the world. As dead as ravioli. I couldn’t eat her if she wouldn’t pay attention.

An aqueous roar rumbled up my neck, inflating wattles of bright blue skin pimpled with red. White spines erected from my throat. I keened my hunger song and threatened Dora with a clacking of worm jaws and a spitting of ammonia gobs. I stuck my face close to hers. I showed her how the venom dripped from my mandibles. But still she wasn’t afraid of me. Was I being too fussy? I needed things a certain way. I shook my fists at her. My fists rattled like bean pods and glowed like fireflies. She looked straight through me.

My brain crawled with clots of inky frustration. The sutures of my skull strained to contain the rising pressure of my spinal fluid. My cortex clenched itself into an angry lavender shelf fungus, harder than bone. I hissed at the loathsome pink woman, and my quadrate bones hinged open. My jaws were filling up with uninvited teeth—shark teeth, shrew teeth, fangs from a coral snake. . . My skin danced with deformities. A white discharge slashed the sky over the laundromat, stuttering brilliance across the pine boughs. Thunder cracked like a mountain splitting.

A shotgun blast rang out. Pellet shot slammed into a washer and ricocheted all over the place. I jumped away from Dora and twisted my head toward the doorway.

A woman was standing there—a woman with blue-black hair, wearing a red dress and pointing a shotgun at me. Sighting down it, back straight, stock snug to her shoulder, very professional. I recognized her from the bowling alley. That Mexican woman. Eva.

Just then she blew one of my arms off.

“That got your attention,” she said.

I tried to speak as she reloaded, but all I could get from my mouth was a querulous mewling.

“So what’s the idea of ditching me like that?” she demanded. “What’s the matter? You like him better than me?”

Mew mew mew, I told her. She blew a load of shot into my face and chest. The impact knocked me onto my back. She approached me.

“Hold still,” she said. “Hold very still. I know why you came to me. I’m going to give you what you need.”

Then she shot off my head. Lightning flickered behind her back. I gathered my legs under me, all the legs I could locate. I aimed myself at her.

“You just want to die,” Eva was saying as she reloaded again. Her voice was low and soothing. “You’re just looking for a way out. I know all about it, Naomi. I can help you.”

She squeezed off another blast as I leapt at her. The buckshot and I crossed paths in midair. Ragged bits of ribs littered the floor. I slammed into Eva and carried her down.

I didn’t mind her blowing apart my skull. Skulls are easy. I could grow skulls and brains as fast as she could blast them. Already I had new skulls swelling in my back, splitting their sutures like pomegranate peels and writhing with brain worms, all beating the powder from their wings and shaking their feathery little feelers. I held Dora under me while my brains pushed out on stalks. They bristled with spinal nerves like tentacled sunflowers. Worms of me anchored their shafts in her ears and corkscrewed under her tongue, changing all the wiring. I could hear Eva choking, and I was hearing it through her ears. Then I knew that the transplant would succeed.

Somehow we stood up and walked, the three of us. With our spines conjoined at our hips, we sidled, limped, and hopped our way out of the building, into the blowing wet.

We shunned the pavement and lost ourselves in the tall weeds and dwarf birches. We left behind a trail of slime and silver fillings. We spat out shreds of a red silk dress and green coveralls. We crawled under a mound of dead leaves. We curled up and shivered. We hid from the soldiers. It had all turned sour and ugly. I felt soiled. They felt raped. I should have been more gentle with them. Now it was too late.

Our bodies rotted. The gas of putrefaction bubbled in our sagging doughy flesh, inflating our shapelessness. Then the gas found issue, and by stages we collapsed into a lump of fallen angel cake that would never rise again.

What had I ever asked of the world? To be a young woman of no particular talents. To play scrabble and make potato salad. To have a boyfriend to tell me movie plots. That was all I wanted. Not so much. But when had the world ever cooperated? When would it ever let me be?

Eva? said Alex. What are you doing in here?

The same thing you’re doing. Do you think there’s any chance we can be surgically removed?

Stop making fun of me! I begged them. This isn’t funny!

Poor Naomi, said Eva. Do you imagine that we make these jokes because we find this funny? No, Naomi. We are not amused.

Have you heard the one, said Alex, about the cannibal who got rid of three hundred pounds of ugly flab?

He ate his wife? I suggested.

Oh, you’ve heard it. How old are you anyway?

It was the jokes that made me sicken of them. I needed to sleep, and I could hear them all the time. Finally I retched them out. I drove them away and made them find their own piles of leaves. And still I could hear them talking. I’ve heard them ever since.

I pawed myself deeper into the dirt, feeling like a snail with no shell, all raw and pink and sticky. I formed a skull to hold my brain in, but the voices of the others kept scurrying in and out, giggling like silverfish and chasing each other in the dark. Bluebottle flies laid their eggs in my helpless heart. My heart began to buzz and swarm with trilobites. There was no escape.

The soldiers came, with their blue flashers and bullhorns and searchlights and gas grenades. They looked for us noisily for what seemed like days. One by one, they dragged us into the cold cruel sunlight.

They kept asking me what I’d done to the victims. They told me that if I cooperated, I’d get a fair trial. Idiots. Retards. They had no conception of what they were dealing with. And of course, no matter what I said to them, they’d still throw me back in a can at the end of the proceedings. Let them.

Let them stuff me into a can again. Let them freeze me like a tasteless string bean. Let them do their worst. I’d broken one can for them. I wouldn’t mind breaking another.

It wasn’t the can that worried me. It was my skull. My skull wasn’t working. Alex thoughts and Eva feelings crawled in and out of it like moths and worms. They gave me no rest. And what could I do? Can a brain escape its skull? How? Its eyes are in the way. Its nerves are in the way. Can a heart escape its chest? Of course not. The ribs make a cage.

If the heart were a beautiful magician’s assistant, you could stick swords through your chest, and the heart would vanish. It would escape. It would go backstage.

But where is backstage? That’s what I wonder.

And how can I get there?

CHAPTER NINE

SIROCCO

Then someone came to me and said

“The little fishes are in bed.”

I said to him, I said it plain,

“Then you must wake them up again.”

—Lewis Carroll

White sand. Blowing white sand. White sand blowing white sand away.

A scorching wind swept across the desert. Sand spilled from the crests of serpentine ridges. Driven sand in a shadowy sunlight. White sand blurred by scattering white sand. Drifts accumulated and collapsed, and dust devils whirled on their backs.

The hiss of the wind rose and fell. The light sometimes dimmed, sometimes brightened. The blistering hot wind sucked the moisture from all it touched.

And here, lost in the middle of it, were the woman, the jeep, and the brontosaur. They’d arranged themselves in a triangle, each one facing toward the others. The sandstorm washed over them like a scalding fog. At times they lost sight of each other.

The brontosaur crouched with its legs tucked under it. The old woman knelt, sitting on the heels of her shoes, resting her hands on her thighs. The sleeves of her coveralls rattled in the wind. Sand piled up against the dinosaur’s belly, the old woman’s shins, and the tires of the jeep.

White sand caught in the woman’s wild white hair. Packed white sand filled in the wrinkles of the brontosaur’s hide. A mound of white sand had formed on the back of the jeep.

Their eyes kept watch on one another, but no one spoke. A conference was coming to order. Alex glared out from the left eye of the saurian. His neck was looped, his right eye pressed against his left shoulder, out of the wind. The open eye studied the old woman.

Alex was profoundly sorry that he’d bitten off Eva’s head yesterday. He wanted to make it up to her somehow. He loved her so painfully. Surely they could be friends again.

He shifted his gaze to Naomi and searched the sandblasted metal box for some sign of Naomi’s qualities: her youth, her forgiveness, her affection for him. Four optics regarded Alex from the corners of a photovoltaic panel. A net of wire quilted the black glass with a grid of diamonds. Where was Naomi?

Naomi watched poor Alex, feeling his fearful perplexity in her circuits, hearing the echoes of his phantom rages, the whispers of his amputated lusts. She magnified the image of the brontosaur’s muzzle, the rutted folds of skin around one glossy black eye. How much older could they get? Where would it end?

Eva contemplated the backs of her hands. She lifted her dark brown eyes to meet Alex’s baleful glare. He acted guilty. Eva didn’t need his guilt. Yes, he’d killed her yesterday, in an unpleasant way, but she forgave him. He’d been sharing his head with a tumor. She knew what that was like. It wasn’t as though she’d never killed him. What was his problem?

“The meeting,” said Eva, “will now come to order. For once we are going to engage in a rational discussion of our situation here.”

“Good idea,” said Naomi from the jeep.

“I will act as the Chair, and speaking out of turn will not be tolerated.”

“Who made you the Chair?” asked Alex. “I’m the oldest.”

“We will attempt to discuss the feasibility of an escape from this place. The Chair will entertain dispassionate inquiry into such questions as: Where the fuck are we?”

“I’d also like to know how we got here,” said Alex.

“That’s two questions for the agenda. Naomi, would you care to suggest a third?”

“Yes. Why can’t we die?”

“A good question,” said Alex. “It positions us on the firm ground of reality. Obviously this hellhole isn’t real. But I know we’ve been real. Shit, I collected garbage in Tucson. That’s as real as it gets.”

“The Chair has the floor,” pronounced Eva.

“Yes,” said Naomi. “The Chair has the floor.”

“Just as a supposition,” Eva went on, “let us assume that What We Are is one shade more real than Where We Are. Well, what are we? A human, a cyber-jeep, and dinosaur. What does this suggest, in and of itself?”

“We’re crazy?” suggested Naomi.

“Time distortion,” said Alex.

“Explain,” demanded Eva.

“We don’t belong in the same epoch. Dinosaurs were extinct when humans walked the earth. And humans were just dying out when jeeps like me were first built.”

“You have seen my point,” said Eva.

“We’re a time distortion,” said Naomi. “Also we can’t kill each other. Can we add another question?”

“No,” said Eva.

“I’m bored,” said Naomi.

“Take a hike,” said Eva. “It’s nice out.”

“Yeah, get lost,” said Alex.

“I won’t,” said Naomi. “And I’m not a prehistoric dinosaur anyway. Shows how much you know. I’m the product of a more advanced technology than you are. I was woven on a gene loom.”

“But they couldn’t do that in the twentieth century,” said Alex.

“Who said they did?”

“But you remember the twentieth century,” said Alex.

“The Canadian girl,” prompted Eva. “The reservist who turned into a kind of banshee. All that.”

“Oh, my brain,” said Naomi. “Of course my brain remembers all that. But the army kept my brain frozen for centuries. Then they planted it in this apatosaur. So the tumor isn’t my fault. They gave me a bad brain, the brain of an executed kidnapper. They court-martialed her, sent her to the electric chair, and cut her up for spare parts. The brain must have been incorrectly labeled, or they’d never have used it.”

“Surprising that they didn’t destroy it,” said Alex.

“They wanted it for further studies, but they couldn’t think of any,” Naomi told him.

“So you’re technically an apatosaur.”

“Technically,” said Naomi, “I’m an M.A.R., a modified apatosaurus reconstruction, adjusted for human brain and voice. I had a very happy childhood.”

“Where was this?” asked Eva.

“I was hatched aboard an interstellar transport craft on a terraforming mission to a distant Earth-like planet. We called our ship the Space Piercer, because it was shaped like a gigantic knitting needle.”

“So you had a childhood in deep space,” said Alex. “What was happy about it?”

“On the Space Piercer,” Naomi went on, “we never knew fear or confusion. Everything was arranged. We were proud explorers, braving the unknown, traversing the velvet darkness between the stars, which shone upon our ship like distant hearth fires.”

Alex and Eva exchanged blank looks.

“Our silver ship was so beautiful. We hadn’t seen it from the outside of course, me and the other hatchlings of my clutch, but we’d seen video feeds from the outrider satellites. We couldn’t play outside like the humans of the crew. They hadn’t made any pressure suits in our size.”

Eva turned to Alex. “Have you heard this fantasy before?”

“Not that I recall,” he told her.

“The Space Piercer was powered by gravity dissonance, navigated by trance, and protected by prayer. The floor and ramps were all upholstered in foam and white vinyl. The pads of our feet were always fresh and clean. Eva was a trance specialist, and Alex was a radar operator.”

“It sounds nice,” said Eva.

“It was paradise. Each star had its own color and a spherical rainbow around it. I was born there, a red speck on a yellow yolk. The yolk floated in pink jelly in a fat brass vat. I wriggled up out of the jelly, and when I could walk up the ramp, a Teacher gave me a sweet melon to eat.”

“What was the name of the ship?” asked Alex.

“I told you,” said Naomi. “The Space Piercer.”

“Not the Long Spike?” asked Alex.

“No,” said Naomi. “Why would it be called the Long Spike?”

“Never mind.”

“You remember it too, don’t you?”

“No,” said Alex. “Go on with what you were saying.”

“Who was this Teacher?” asked Eva. “Who gave you this melon?”

“The Teachers were modified spider monkeys,” Naomi told her. “They raised us all from wrigglers. They had the biggest heads you ever saw. They taught us how to talk. They taught us where we came from. Whatever we wanted to know, they had answers all ready.”

“Who was in charge of the ship?” asked Alex. “Who were you working for?”

“We were the servants of the Great Spider Mother. We were the seventh generation of apatosaurs to be raised for Her. We were all extensions of Her sacred body. The gene looms had used Her eggs to make every living thing on the ship.”

“Her eggs?” said Eva. “Who was this Spider Mother?”

“Oh, just a little gray wolf spider that they kept in a golden terrarium on the altar of the ship’s cathedral. They’d kept Her alive for centuries and turned Her eggs into farm animals, food crops, humans, even you, Eva.”

“Well, who made the gene looms?” asked Alex.

“The Designers of course,” said Naomi.

“Well, who built the ship?”

“The Builders.”

“I give up,” said Alex.

“We chanted the praises of the Spider Mother in relays. There was always a choir of us singing. The Keepers fed Her damselflies, and we sang praises to the damselflies too. We worshipped the threads of Her web.”

“What did they need brontosaurs for?” Alex wanted to know. “Radar, I can understand. You need radar. But why brontosaurs?”

“We wondered that ourselves at first, because we ate so much hay, and we hadn’t any hands. Then the Teachers told us that we were training to become the ship’s monks.”

“And what are monks good for?” asked Eva.

“We prayed,” said Naomi. “We prayed around the clock, for the safety of the voyage and the success of our mission. We prayed and fasted and purified ourselves with devotions. We prayed to the galaxy and to all the local gods, demons, and angels which the priestesses discovered implicate in the voids that we crossed.”

“But why brontosaurs?” insisted Alex.

“I was getting to that,” Naomi replied. “It’s the length of our spinal cords. The size of a creature’s nervous system sets a limit on its spiritual development. That’s why elephants have larger souls than people do. A brontosaur can attune its spine to the longer etheric wavebands. Whales would’ve been ideal, but they’re very hard to keep in deep space.”

“That makes sense,” said Alex.

“The Teachers had many rules for us and broke our toes for petty infractions. Sadistic monkeys. But the joke was on them. We forgave them and obeyed them to perfect ourselves in obedience.”

“What a kick in the head,” said Alex. “As the blind man said to the elephant. But what can you expect from primates? Filthy bickering scavengers. They invented the wheel, but other than that. . .”

“They’re all so fond of wheels,” said Naomi. “All so wrapped up in things that go in circles. Poems. Songs. Seasons. Centuries. Pinwheels. Prayer wheels. Clockwork. . .”

“Eggs rolling down hills,” said Alex.

“Easter eggs?” said Naomi.

“No no no,” said Alex. “Rolling eggs! Egg races! Can’t you think straight? Don’t you know how to concentrate?”

“Let her be,” said Eva.

“Make me,” Alex told her. “Are you aware of the difference between the eye of a rooster and a windshield wiper? Are you? You can’t be! There’s no comparison. There’s no basis. You want another example?”

“I think we’re off our topic,” said Eva. “Naomi, did this needle ship ever visit any planets?”

“Obviously,” said Naomi. “It dropped us here.”

“Why here?” asked Eva.

“Because we were sick,” said Naomi. “They couldn’t keep sick people on the Space Piercer.”

“So they left us here.”

“Yes. Here at the hospital.”

“There’s a hospital on this planet?” said Eva.

“Not on it,” said Naomi. “Behind it.”

“Fine, but tell me this: Before the ship dumped us here, had the three of us ever been off the Samsara? Had we ever been to Earth?”

“What did you call it?” asked Naomi.

“She called it the Samsara,” said Alex.

“Isn’t that what she called it?” asked Eva.

“No,” Alex told her. “She calls it the Space Piercer. You call it the Samsara.”

“And you called it the Long Spike,” said Naomi.

Eva frowned. “How could they abandon us like that? What kind of hospital is this?”

Naomi laughed. “It isn’t one, really.”

“Then why maroon us here.”

“They didn’t. We escaped. We’re runaway slaves.”

Eva looked from Naomi to Alex and back. “But I thought you said—”

“I lied,” crooned Naomi.

The three of them listened to the wind for a while.

“Let me just sum up,” suggested Alex. “Where the fuck are we? We’re on a planetoid with a hospital behind it. But we aren’t patients, we’re runaway slaves. Fine. How the hell did we get here? I know that one. We perished. We croaked. We just don’t remember how. Why can’t we die? Because we’re dead. Any other questions?”

“Alex,” said Eva, “do you remember leaving Earth?”

“No,” he told her. “I remember Chicago, then a string of jobs as a public utilities vehicle. Then the particle war. Then this. Everything else is a blank.”

“It must have been some party,” said Eva, wiping sand from her eyelashes. “Maybe we died in the war. You’d think a person would remember a thing like that.”

They listened to the wind howl.

“I finally just broke down, I suppose,” said Alex. “Just slid into some ditch and never called for a tow. In the dead of the night, in the dead of winter, in a hole in the bottom of the sea. And then the angels wafted us up to heaven. And here we are.”

“Speaking of angels,” said Naomi. “I had a talk with our guardian angel only last night. And he explained everything.”

“And you believed him,” said Alex.

“Did he tell you his name?” asked Eva.

“Dr. Mazer,” said Naomi.

“And you believed him,” said Alex.

“He’s an angel,” said Naomi. “An angel wouldn’t lie. Dr. Mazer built this whole place—shell craters, grocery stores, Mayan ruins. . . He pulls it out of our heads when we’re asleep. Dr. Mazer told me that I’m the only one of us who’s making a recovery. Although I’m still very sick.”

“Who?” asked Eva. “The bald guy in the white coat?”

“You’ve dreamed of him too!” said Naomi.

“Back up,” said Alex. “Our guardian angel is a head shrinker?”

“I still don’t see why we can’t die,” said Eva. “I acknowledge that we’re dead, but why must that prevent our dying? It looks like prejudice to me.”

“We can’t die,” said Naomi, “because we’re crossing this desert. The only way to die is to get across.”

Eva spoke through clenched teeth. “How can we cross it if it isn’t real? I think that you may be confused.”

“That’s right,” said Alex. “She’s Confused, you’re Wretched, and I’m Demented. We’d make a good vaudeville team. Leggies and germs! Just released from the back wards! It’s those tap-dancing psycho-toxins Confused, Wretched, and Demented!”

“Mother? What is Papa talking about?”

“He’s just talking, Naomi.”

Alex rhythmically pounded his head against the sand. “Torture wheel,” he said. “Torture wheel. Terrible mistake.”

“Don’t fret,” Naomi told him brightly. “We will die eventually. Dr. Mazer promised.”

“The meeting is adjourned,” said Eva.

The old woman started to laugh, and the brontosaur joined her. The wind picked up speed. The sand began to sting their skins.

But as for Naomi, her attention was drifting away. Deep in the jeep’s hardware, she disconnected all her cameras and faded down her microphones. Outside of the jeep, drowned in the raging wind, an old witch and an old dragon were laughing together hysterically.

Naomi wasn’t listening to them. She was deep within herself, curled in a blue ball, floating in saline water. She was opening her true eyes.

Naomi uncoiled herself and swam across the surface of a warm lagoon, a blue plesiosaur, paddling and coasting, paddling and coasting, over the soft gray silt of the shallows. Only her eyes and nostrils broke the surface. She steered by dragging the tip of her tail in the muck. Miniature thunderheads of mud erupted in her wake.

Then she swam through a bright white sky, miles above a barren volcanic plain. Thin icy air whistled past her head. The monotony of the plain was broken by a line of cypress trees. Beyond the trees, the dark rock gave way to green lawns that extended, perfectly level, to the horizon.

The lawns shifted somehow into expanses of green-dyed felt that were stretched over the slate of an infinite pool table. Naomi flew over a triangle of ivory pool balls, each ball as massive as ten mountains. She turned from her course and circled the balls at low altitude. Their scale made her look like a wee blue newt swimming in the boulder crannies of a mountain stream. Naomi saw a castle in the distance. It resembled a giant chess rook carved from white jade.

A mile or so ahead there was a lake of black water in the pool table. The tall white castle rose from the lake’s center. No drawbridge spanned the water, and the castle gate was shut.

Naomi shrank into a newt and swam through a barred window. She swam down a long hallway built entirely from elephant bones. There was a green carpet and potted palms. On both sides of the hall were rows of gray steel doors inset with windows of frosted glass. Naomi noticed a name printed on one of the doors. DR. MAZER.

Naomi wriggled up against the white glass, trying to see through. Finding an area of the glass that offered no resistance, she climbed through it into Dr. Mazer’s office.

Naomi hovered in the dimness, a translucent blue newt, looking around her in puzzlement. She’d seen the office before, but now she hardly recognized it. It was wrecked. A shambles. A file cabinet lay on its side, spilling folders from its drawers. The floor was strewn with trampled books. The surveillance screens were draped with filthy bandages and bloody surgical instruments. All the Chinese porcelain vases were in one corner of the room, kicked into a heap of ceramic shards, damp potting soil, and wilted fronds. Atop Dr. Mazer’s oaken desk, a fort had been constructed from a coffee table, two chairs, and some gray flannel blankets.

Naomi approached the fort and circled it warily until she could see through a gap between two blankets. A man with a bald head and thick spectacles was sitting in lotus position under the coffee table, in his underpants. He also wore the rags of a lab coat smeared with dried crusts of several colors. The doctor muttered to himself, rigid, sweat trickling down his face, his spectacles fogged.

Naomi turned her tiny ghostly head and noticed that the video screens were on, all showing the same picture. It was an overhead view of the woman, the jeep, and the brontosaur, back on the desert of white sand. Why did Mother and Father look all red?

Naomi swam toward the screens for a closer look. The wind out there must’ve been deafening, though you couldn’t hear it in Mazer’s office. The flying sand was flaying the skin from the woman and the saurian. They danced about as they sank into the collapsing drifts of sand. The jeep was simply parked, letting the white tide cover its optical ports one by one.

It’s a storm, thought Naomi as she hung in the stale air of the office. It’s some new kind of storm. And I’m right at the sending station.

A sand slide buried them all. They vanished without a trace.

Spilling heaps of salt. Blowing snow. Shifting clouds. White granulation. White ants swarming on three cathode screens.

Nothing. Naomi turned back to Mazer’s fort. The doctor crept out from under the table and climbed stiffly down from his desk. He dried his face on a blanket then used it to blow his nose.

“Something’s gone wrong,” he told himself.

He walked to the video screens and turned knobs. All the screens would show was static.

“I’ve lost them.”

Dr. Mazer sat heavily in his leather chair and shivered. He rubbed his arms through his coat. He clenched his fists.

“I’m falling apart.”

Someone knocked at the office door. He didn’t seem to hear. The floating newt which was Naomi swam through the door again to see who was there.

In the hall she came face to face with a beautiful coffee-colored Hindu woman in a sarong of white silk. The woman was waiting patiently at Mazer’s door. Her face was ageless and serene. Her lustrous black hair fell behind her to the sash at her waist. Over her sarong she wore a vest of embroidered violet felt decorated with tiny mirrors. She also wore a great many copper bracelets and anklets, silvers rings on her fingers and on the toes of her bare brown feet, and a gold ring on one nostril. On her brow she wore the mark of a Dravidian holy woman.

Reverend Weaver rapped at the glass again. Her posture was formal, but there was mischief in her eyes. She waited. Then she leaned closer to the frosted glass and blew very gently against it.

The door burst from its hinges and flew into the office. It cartwheeled across the floor, breaking its window, and flipped over the desk, scattering the fort. Mazer looked up in confusion. Weaver stood in the doorway and nodded to him, smiling.

“Oh, it’s you,” he said. “Come in.”

Mazer wandered the room, looking for a place where Weaver could sit down. He circled the room completely and would have kept circling except that Weaver put her hand on his shoulder.

As he turned to her, she moved into his arms and kissed him full on the mouth. Mazer backed away from her, wiping his mouth. His back thumped up against a wall. Weaver sat down on a fallen filing cabinet and surveyed the chaos.

“Your office is somewhat disordered,” she remarked.

She folded her hands on her lap, closed her painted eyelids, and sighed. In response, every loose object in the office—maps, pill bottles, stethoscope, even the doctor’s desk—went tumbling through the nearest wall, vanishing from sight. Only the filing cabinet and a red plush sofa with white fringe remained behind. And Dr. Mazer didn’t recognize the sofa.

Weaver raised her arms and stretched her shoulders. The office walls slid back in four directions. She smoothed her sarong over her knees.

“A great improvement,” she said.

She moved to the sofa and patted the cushion beside her. Mazer buttoned the rags of his coat and joined her.

“How have you been, reverend?”

Weaver shrugged. “Depends on where I am. Working hard. I just came from 1891. That’s always restful.”

“Do you still have that tree house in India?”

Weaver nodded. “It suits me when I’m off duty.”

“I prefer to live in the present. On the cutting edge.”

Already they were dueling. It was an old habit and hard to break. Mazer sneezed.

After he wiped his eyes, he found that he and Weaver and the sofa were outdoors, in a field of wild oats under a willow tree, beside a pond. “Do you like macaroni salad?” she asked him. After long acquaintance with Weaver, Mazer expected this sort of thing. He thanked her and received a paper plate and a silver fork.

“Why are we here?”

“I brought us here. Too pastoral?”

“Why have you come?”

Weaver looked up from buttering her toast. Her eyes were wide and innocent. “I missed you,” she told him.

Mazer crossed his arms over his chest. “No. You’ve been listing to hateful rumors.”

“Never.” She touched his arm. “It’s just that I’m worried about you. As are many of the angels in our circle. Wing for example is convinced that you’re insane.”

Mazer gazed across the sparkling water of the pond. “That treacherous little faggot.” He set down his plate and fork. “So you’ve come to see for yourself.”

“Actually I’d like to discuss your three patients.”

“They’re not your concern. When I need a spiritual consultation, I’ll ask for one.”

The field darkened. A shadow fell across the pond. The willow branches knocked together restlessly. “Oh Mazer,” Weaver sighed. “You’re so fucking clinical.”

The reverend got to her knees and took hold of Mazer’s lapels. “Look at yourself. You’ve lost it. You never leave your office anymore. What are the rest of us supposed to think? Listen, Mazer, you’ve got to coordinate yourself with the team. The Black Wall is only five hundred years away.”

“Do you imagine that I’m oblivious to the fact?” Mazer answered. “The Minoan Recurrence is breathing down our necks, and three of the Twelve Implicated are right off their heads. It’s a disaster. And I take full responsibility. But remember one thing, Weaver. You were the one who assigned me custody.”

“That’s perfectly true. But I never expected that you’d make a permanent career out of—”

“Permanent? Nothing is permanent! Time is running out! Yet you dare to stand there and lecture me about the Black Wall?”

“I’m kneeling, doctor. Please take note of it.”

Mazer calmed himself and ate an olive. “I’m sensitive about this case,” he admitted. “The three of them have been a great disappointment to me.”

Weaver peeled a boiled egg and salted it. “The cure is not proceeding as you’d hoped?” she suggested.

Mazer shook his head and snorted bitterly. “That’s an understatement.”

A sparrow lighted on a branch above them. The reverend chewed thoughtfully on her egg. “Would you let me talk with them?”

“Out of the question. They’re my assignment, and I’ll cured them. I just need a little more time.”

The two angels picked at their food in silence. The wind worried the oat stalks. Mazer removed his spectacles and wiped them on the rags of his sleeve. When he put them on again, he and Weaver were seated in a small bare observation gallery which overlooked a surgical theater.

Behind a plate glass window, a team of neurosurgeons and nurses were attempting a brain operation.

“They’re incredibly stubborn,” Mazer went on. “But I’m wearing them down.” He averted his eyes from the exposed encephalon. He was finding it difficult to concentrate. Down there on the operating table, something had gone very wrong. There was blood jetting into the air, and all the nurses were moving rapidly. Mazer closed his eyes and tried to clear his head.

When he opened his eyes, he and Weaver were standing on beaten red earth under a tropical sun. Around them were a throng of Incan warriors in full battle regalia. Everyone was facing a steep granite ziggurat. Chanting priests were herding a rabble of war prisoners up a ramp to the ziggurat’s summit. Above the head of the warriors in their ranks, four prisoners were bound to the four shelves of an altar of obsidian. Four priests held four daggers poised over the prisoners’ chests.

“Where was I?” said Mazer.

Weaver stood with her chin up and watched the disemboweling without flinching, without even blinking.

“Oh yes,” said Mazer. “Much of their delusional material relates to their origin as a family. They feel that if they could account for this, they could undo it. Thus they rationalize their regression and avoid a confrontation with their present situation in the afterlife.”

“But Mazer! They all died psychotic! And your treatments have only driven them deeper in. Don’t you think that the simplest thing, at this point, would be to let the three of them incarnate again? Then they can sort it all out for themselves.”

Mazer grunted impatiently. “No no no. That’s the old thinking. The padded cell approach. Completely out of date. My storm system is much better.”

Weaver took Mazer’s chin in her smooth brown hand and gazed into his watery eyes. “You make me want to scream,” she told him.

They were standing at the end of a bamboo pier, facing a broad green river on a humid night in Malaysia. The stars were very bright above the water. Tree frogs shrilled from the lianas, and all around them the river lapped at mangrove roots.

Weaver took his hand and led him inland along a path through the jungle. Up ahead a bonfire was burning, tossing sparks at the stars. They came to a clearing where a circle of women danced naked around a fire, slowly swaying and revolving to a music that Mazer couldn’t hear.

“Do you have to keep changing things around?” he asked her.

They sat down on bamboo mats and watched the dance.

“And what about you?” asked Weaver. “Do you have to keep switching your patient’s maya bodies on them? What’s the function of that little shell game? How’s that supposed to help your three little peas to adjust?”

“If you’re going to be abusive,” Mazer told her officiously, “I will conclude this discussion.”

The firelight danced in Weaver’s eyes. “You could at least introduce yourself to them.”

“I don’t like to get that involved with my patients. I work best at a distance.”

Weaver hugged herself and rocked herself, grimacing. “Sometimes,” she told him, “I want nothing more in the world than to open your cranium with a large rock. How could I ever have married you?”

“That was long ago. You and I just have different ideas about healing the sick.”

“Healing,” Weaver whispered, growing suddenly to twice her size. “Healing?” she repeated, standing up and towering over the clearing, against the stars, naked in the firelight. “You call yourself a healer?”

Mazer whipped off his lab coat and threw it into the ferns as if preparing to wrestle. The circle of dancers whirled around the blaze.

“They’re not ready!” he shouted hoarsely. “I won’t allow it! I’m not done with them yet! I’m on the verge of a breakthough.”

“You’re on the verge of a serious head injury,” the giantess warned him.

“Then kill me,” he suggested petulantly. “Or if you haven’t the stomach for it, send Spiker after me. Spiker lives for assassinations. She’d love to have an excuse for doing me in.”

The bonfire flared and blinded Mazer momentarily. When his eyes would focus again, he was standing on a tightrope in the glare of spotlights, high above the sawdust in a circus tent. Clowns and bareback riders on elephants watched from the corners of the tent. The crowd on the bleachers held its breath.

Weaver walked up behind Mazer and put her arms around his waist, pressing her breasts against his back and whispering in his ear.

“You are an old old angel,” she told him. “You have fallen into overwork and exhaustion. You have dwelt here too long amongst your dead souls in cages and your snake pits. You have lapsed into mere madness, Mazer. And you are too proud to ask for help. You call yourself a healer? Do you think that you can keep your distance from sick souls, Mazer? From those three you’ve bottled in that hell of white sand? Dream on. You’ve caught their disease. You’ve become it. You’ve turned into a monster. But I’ll always love you, because I love all monsters. And that’s why you’re going to let me have them, the last three inmates in your asylum. Because you love me too, because all men love me. And also because you know that it isn’t right to use people’s souls for plow horses this way. Time to let the troika rest. Give them to me. I’ll put them out to pasture.”

“It’s possible,” Mazer allowed, “that I may have gone wrong. But I can’t just. . . I can’t just. . .”

All at once they were standing face to face again, on the surface of the moon. Sharp shadows, feeble gravity, and vast stillness. Dr. Mazer was an angel, but still he was used to breathing and to a certain amount of air pressure. Weaver was purposely causing him discomfort. No matter. He had given his answer, and his decision was final.

Weaver spoke to him again. Though her voice made no sound, Mazer heard it clearly.

“If you can’t, then we’ll make you. You have exceeded your authority. Your time is up. You may be Alex’s guardian angel, but I’m Eva’s. You will surrender your prisoners. I’ll give you one night to think about it. I’ll be here again tomorrow. Take a bath.”

A crooked smile crossed Mazer’s face. For just one moment he and Weaver were lying side by side under a patchwork quilt in a bed with a fringed white canopy, their heads on two eiderdown pillows, gazing into one another’s eyes.

Then she was gone. Mazer was alone in his trashed office, standing on his filing cabinet.

The three dead monitor screens squatted on their shelf beside the hall door. On the center screen, an image flickered into being. A brontosaur, a woman, and a jeep were standing on a sandy waste. The brontosaur’s head was cocked, one eye staring straight into the video camera. The woman was bouncing excitedly around the jeep. She seemed to be explaining something to it.

They were standing inside the picture tube, marveling at Mazer’s office, which was just as Naomi had described it. The brontosaur craned its neck toward the woman. The woman nodded her head. The only sound from the monitor was a low steady hum.

Reverend Weaver was crossing the drawbridge over the moat that encircled Mazer’s castle. A premonition rose from the black water like a dank fog. It froze Weaver in mid-step.

She realized that if Mazer was forced to give up his patients, he would try to erase his failure by killing them. Yes. He would kill them. Not today. Perhaps not tomorrow. But soon. Weaver had pushed him to far.

A terrible situation. Weaver was going to need help.

Perhaps it was time to call Spiker in.

PART THREE

SIGNS OF RECOVERY

CHAPTER TEN

SPRAYING FOR BUGS

Their legs were old burnt matches.

Their legs were old burnt matches.

Their legs were old burnt matches.

Their arms were just the same.

—Vachel Lindsay

It was 1997, and I was working for the city of Tucson as an exterminator. My life was fairly simple. I’d given up on being a man, and was trying to function as a public works vehicle.

UBLICWORKSVEHICLEPUBLICWORKSVEHICLEPUBLICWORKSVEHICLEPUB

I was south of downtown, driving the side streets around Stone, looking for an address. I watched for the numbers stenciled in yellow paint on the lampposts and checked them against my internal street map. My optics scanned the decaying city, translating walls and alleys, and dumpsters into vehicular simulation space. My tires rumbled over the sun-scorched gutter trash. My fender optics tracked the curbs. Traffic was sparse. There weren’t any pedestrians out, nor any drivers either. The city had grown nearly uninhabitable. The storm drains were choked, so the next winter flood would swamp the place and cripple city services for good. Piped water and wired power would recede into the fabled past. The more stubborn among the citizens were refusing to evacuate to the settlement camps. All the worse for them.

I came to Russell Avenue and braked at a stop sign beside a boarded-up library. I was the only vehicle in Tucson that still stopped for signs, but I’m a creature of habit, what can I do? A tow truck limped past me along Russell, dragging a sedan with no wheels. The tow truck had a flat. I turned onto Russell and proceeded south.

A year earlier I’d been the guidance brain in a trash truck. That was pleasant work, if you didn’t have a nose. But they ripped me out of that truck and wired me into this van. Well, ours not to reason why.

My six tires rattled over cracked cement. The pressure vent on my methanol tank was jammed, and my shock absorbers were a joke, but I was on the job, and that was what mattered to the City. I had a good spare tire but no jack. I’d lost a wiper blade, and when it rained, I compulsively dragged bare metal across my windshield. Also I talked to myself too much.

I had my service manual etched on my visual cortex—four volumes of theoretical perfection and zero tolerance schematics—no rust, no wear, no fraying cables. I used to read it in the back of my mind, for a laugh.

My only friend at this time was a municipal garage door opener who opened the door for me when I went out or came back. She told me that my problem was that I’d been a man, and now I expected too much. I wonder how she could tell.

I was driving through a city of people who stayed at home. They stayed indoors and watched television and gradually melted. That was the new virus, the melting. After they melted, ambulances with loud sirens would take their goo to the University Hospital to make sure they were dead. After they got certified, pickup trucks would haul barrels of them out to the cemetery on Oracle Drive. Then bulldozers covered them over, and then there were forms to fill out.

The city had been built by earthmovers and cement mixers and cranes. Humans could never have built it. Human could hardly live there. Humans grew buboes, sarcomas, and cysts there. Humans broke out in plastic sores, plague cankers, and growths without names there. Their wheat was full of wheat rust, no matter how their chemists poisoned it. Their water was full of cleaning supplies. If the poisons didn’t get them, they still had household pests to contend with. New improved mutant pests! Paper wasps from Chile. Amazonian jumping scorpions. Norwegian wheel bugs and killer isopods from the Malay. The humans had it tough. The ones with no houses to hide in would lock themselves in cars and starve there, or seal themselves into dumpsters with duct tape and suffocate. At least they didn’t have to worry about rats. The bugs had eaten all the rats.

But humans weren’t my job. My job was killing pests. You weren’t going to catch me catching any diseases. You’d never see me melting. Me and the digital clocks and VCRs were immune. God grants small favors for his chosen. Sometimes so small you need a microscope.

I was told what to do, and I did what I was told. If I forgot where I was going, my Comptroller would remind me. If I ignored him, he repeated himself. People’s houses filled up with bugs, so they phoned my Comptroller. Then he would send me out to spray. People didn’t want to be bothered with bugs. People wanted to melt in peace. I could relate to that. I would have liked to be left in peace. But someone had to spray.

I knew a lot about bugs. My ROM included A Bestiary of the Urban Insects of North America, updated to 1996. The carapace of the Pea Scaler ranges from drab brown in winter to brilliant yellow—the sting of the Giant House Centipede—Sometimes I’d run it through my voice coder at high speed. Bug jabber.

ORMALCOMPLAINTFORMALCOMPLAINTFORMALCOMPLAINTFORMALCOM

I was in a disgraceful condition. I should not have been working. My transmission fluid was seeping, there were bubbles in my tires, and my solenoid was out of adjustment. I should have been up on a lift, with grease monkeys packing my wheel bearings.

All it took out there was the irrevocable snap of one brittle fan belt, and you’d be dead on the shoulder of some desolate industrial drag, and the car strippers would come down like flies. But the City didn’t consider that. The City needed every vehicle it could muster in those desperate days. If it moved, put it to work! That was their philosophy. Who was I to complain? I couldn’t feel pain. Neither can bugs, but bugs at least can die. Bugs do have that advantage over machines.

OBILEUNITMOBILEUNITMOBILEUNITMOBILEUNITMOBILEUNITMOBILEUNIT

A dummy sat in my driver’s seat. He didn’t drive me. He was just part of my equipment. There wasn’t any steering wheel, so he kept his gloves in his lap. He had black leather gloves, black leather boots, and no hands or feet in them. The gloves and the boots were his hands and feet.

He was called a Mobile Unit because he could get out of me and walk around. His coat was black vinyl, and his pants, and his cap, and his face. Dual cameras were mounted in his head. Their lens covers were gridded red glass, like a stoplight. I could see through his eyes or talk from the speaker in his neck. I could swivel his head or tap his feet. I could send him into houses and use him to spray for bugs.

When I was a garage truck, I’d had a similar Mobile Unit to empty the trash cans. Sometimes he’d even scrape up Melters. Certain Melters didn’t rate the ambulance treatment—dumpster people, people who melted in public toilets, people like that.

Garbage collection had ended a year earlier, but the City still sent teams around to spray the fresh garbage with a foam that hardened around it. Phenomenal, the things they were doing with plastics. People were dropping like flies, but the new plastics were breeding like rabbits. It gave one hope for the future.

TATUSREPORTSTATUSREPORTSTATUSREPORTSTATUSREPORTSTATUSREP

I turned north from East Sixteenth into a graveled alley between Stone and Russell. To the west was a junk-strewn vacant lot. North of the lot stood a row of two-yard-wide mini-apartments, with aerials and swamp coolers on their roofs and bulbous mounds of petrified foamed-over garbage leaning against them in the alley. To the east, the back yards of houses, behind storm fence. I parked beside a house reputed to be the residence of a Mrs. Everson. My turbines wound down. I filed a status report by tightbeam. It was a punishing day in August, 1:27 p.m.

ISEMBARKATIONDISEMBARKATIONDISEMBARKATIONDISEMBARKATIOND

I raised the dummy’s hand and unbuckled his harness. I unlatched the driver’s door and pushed it open. Swiveled the dummy’s legs out of my cab. Somehow got both of his boots planted on the gravel. Slammed the door behind him and turned him toward the back end of me. All very complicated, if you thought about it too much.

I opened the rear doors and dragged out my canvas pouch and my tool belt. I hung the pouch on my shoulder and stuffed it with spray guns and a cross-section of aerosol toxins. I had pesticides for ants, spiders, roaches, fleas, silverfish, or armored slugs.

A mangy dog trotted up the alley with a scrap of greasy butcher paper in its mouth. It didn’t even look at me. I had no scent. I walked up to the storm fence and followed it south until I came to a gate, chained and padlocked. I hung my gloves on the wire and thought for a while. Luckily I had a bolt cutter on my tool belt.

The side yard was paved with red bricks. A barrel cactus stood in a little well. A swing set with no swings stood rusting. I turned east at the corner of the house, found the front step, and knocked at the door. Just then I was attacked by a vicious carnivore. Well, it was only a small poodle, but it bit my leg.

Slowly I bent at the waist and leaned down toward the dog. “Good dog,” I said soothingly, while it growled and worried my ankle. “Nice doggy.” I drew a cylinder of pressurized methyl cyanase from my pouch and lifted it over my head. Then I clubbed the fucker until it let go of me. I kicked it against the door, and it landed in a broken heap.

“I’m coming! I’m coming!” said a voice from the house. “Don’t kick the door down!” Security chains rattled. I kicked the poodle into a space between the aluminum siding and a potted yucca.

“It’s the exterminator,” I called out. “The exterminator is here.”

“Keep your shirt on,” said Mrs. Everson.

RS.EVERSONMRS.EVERSONMRS.EVERSONMRS.EVERSONMRS.EVERSONMR

The door swung open. An old woman squinted at me, shielding her eyes from the sun with one hand. Her arms were like plucked chicken wings, and her neck was like a powdered eclair. She wore orthopedic shoes, nylons rolled to the ankles, bifocals on a cord, and a hearing aid. Hanging by a thread.

“Are you the exterminator?” she asked me. “Well, come in out of the sun. I’ll talk to you in the den. I’m watching one of my programs.” Then she vanished and left me in the entryway. I would have to locate the den by the sound from the television. Not as simple as it sounds.

I was standing, according to my floor plan, in the entryway. But entryway is a deceptively innocuous term for that sense-numbing welter of porcelain mementos, stacked magazines, ceramic lamps, Christmas cards, et cetera. The ceiling was low, and there was hardly room to move.

I took my bearings from the walls and door frames and set forth. I knocked over an end table. I let it lie. I could understand a table wanting to lie down. I wished that someone would let me lie down.

I found Mrs. Everson sitting in an armchair. She was watching her TV set and eating crackers and cheese. Square orange cheese slices on round orange crackers filled a plate that rested on a tray with legs. Two boxes stood beside the plate, a box of cheese and a box of crackers. Every minute or two, Mrs. Everson would eat a cracker, and there’d be one less cracker on the plate.

Wet laundry was dripping on the rug. Yes, there were clotheslines strung across the den, with dripping clothes on them. It made sense. It saved her going outdoors.

Mrs. Everson phoned her sister and packed an overnight bag. Then she called a cab. The cab came two hours later. Her parting instructions to me, as I bundled her out the door, were that I should help myself to whatever I found in the icebox, but that I should under no circumstances move her furniture, because she had it all where she wanted it.

She shuffled toward her cab in the pitiless afternoon sun. She turned and spoke to me again. “You should be careful not to breathe that stuff,” she told me. “You could grow a cancer like a grapefruit and never be properly compensated.”

I could relate to that. Everyone should be properly compensated.
