When I shut the door behind her, the house was mine. It was just me and the bugs now. But first I had to prep the floors.

I took my claw hammer into the den and got down on my knees in all the fucking magazines and crackers and tried to pull up the fucking carpet tacks. But there was all this fucking furniture in the way! The hell with the carpet tacks, I said to myself. I’ll rip up the fucking carpet with my fucking bare hands.

So I did. The television pitched off its stand and smashed. A bureau full of knickknacks fell on its side. But fuck all that shit! I had work to do! I tore the rug up, wrapped the armchair and the birdcage in it, and dragged the whole mess into the yard.

Then I unloaded my pouch onto the kitchen table. I arranged all my pumps and canisters, my thinners and spreaders, all my sticky syrups and virulent powders. I picked out a tank of ant-and-roach mix and screwed on a red rubber hose with a brass nozzle. (Tri-iso-necrolaine in a base of sodium chromate. If swallowed, induce vomiting.)

I hooked the tank onto a shoulder strap, carried it to the kitchen sink, and pointed the nozzle at the dishes piled there. I turned a valve. Half eaten toast on a flowery plate turned black. Cups and spoons and scrambled egg turned black. The sink and the sudsy water in it turned black. A gleaming black film crept up the wall tiles and blackened the doors of the cupboards.

Wallpaper blistered, peeled, and smoked. The inky stain covered the kitchen ceiling and spread into the living room. Shreds of curling paint fell from the plaster, leaving jagged white holes in the black.

I stood beside a window, spraying a baseboard and watching the guck soak in. The streetlights were on now. The stars were out. A breeze stirred the dead grass at the curb. I lifted my nozzle to the window panes. Oily goo slid down the glass and dripped from the sill. I moved into the bedroom.

Here, bugs! Come to Alex! Heeere, bug bug bug!

I closed off the valve on the tank. I surveyed the black drapes, the black bedspread and bolster, the closet full of black clothes, and the black perfume bottles on the smoking black lace doily. The toxic scum was a great improvement. It lent that cluttered wooden cave a sterile lunar beauty.

You started with the crude stuff to get the bugs’ attention and to flush a few into the open. Once you knew what you were dealing with, you could poison them more selectively. You could fuck up their spiracles with polymers, or mess up their sex lives with pheromones, or even feed them enzymes that killed their alimentary flora and made them starve. The possibilities were endless, a chemist’s holiday. But until you could stink a bug out of the woodwork and tag a specimen, you were just pissing in the wind. And I still hadn’t seen one bug.

All right then, I said to myself. Let’s experiment.

Back in the kitchen, I filled a bucket at the sink and screwed the lid off a jar of potassium tartrate. I poured three cups of the dangerous yellow granules into the water. They sputtered and fizzed. I found a mop and wet down all the floors. When a cabinet or a dresser got in my way, I’d rip the legs off it, drag it to the yard, and throw it on the burn pile. But the mop fell apart before I could finish. And still no bugs.

It never failed. Just when you got a good blood lust rolling, the little cunts crept deeper into the crawl spaces, where you couldn’t get at them, like an itch at the center of your back. Crawl spaces. The hallmark of shoddy construction. I walked around the house kicking holes in the fucking walls.

I had to carry stuff out of there in armfuls—samplers, houseplants, cans of tomato paste. . . And there was always more. What a rat’s nest! It made me want to strangle a parakeet. I got busy with my sledge hammer and knocked down some shelves and partitions. Then I turned off all the lights and sat down against a wall to think.

The sprays might be driving the bugs into the foundation. Perhaps I could lure them out with some bait.

Peanut butter? Chicken liver? Dead dog? The dead dog would be perfect. I walked out into the moonlight and fetched it indoors. I put it on the bedspread and hid in the bedroom closet with the bathrobes and tennis rackets.

Still the bugs wouldn’t show themselves. I needed more bait.

I went to the alley and broke through the plastic crust of one of the hillocks of old garbage. Dragged two black plastic bags back to the house and used a garden rake to distribute the trash evenly. I kept going for more until I had it to a depth of six inches on all the floors. Then I hid in the closet again.

All night I lay in wait, watching with my eyes, listening with my gloves, smelling with my boots. The air conditioner turned itself on, turned itself off, a well adjusted appliance. Cars drove past. I turned off my eyes and just listened. My head felt as if it had retracted into my chest.

But I could make out a new rhythm behind the tranquil murmur of the air conditioner. A chirping. A shrilling. Dozens of shrillings. From the walls, the floor, from every side. A strident mantric din like a horde of locusts. I left the closet and turned on a lamp. The bugs went right on singing, safe in their crannies.

The air conditioner was distracting me, so I ripped out its cord. That’s when I saw my first cicada.

NITIALSIGHTINGINITIALSIGHTINGINITIALSIGHTINGINITIALSIGHTINGINITIA

It dropped out of the air conditioner and landed on the floor. A huge fucker, six inches long. With a humped brown shell like a cricket’s. Angular sawtooth legs. Crooked feelers tapping at the floorboards like blind men’s canes. A radically mutated giant cicada. It belonged in a monster movie, knocking down Hoover Dam.

It scuttled nervously toward the wall, then stopped. I froze where I stood and processed optical data. Sampled out a dorsal view and a profile and ran them through my morphological comparitor. This cicada was not the traditional seventeen-year root sucker. This thing was a carnivore.

I wasn’t normally given to emotional reactions to vermin. But the giant cicada filled me with a physical dismay. It made the vinyl creep on my aluminum bones. It made my clothes itch.

Such things were to be expected. This was the decade when all the genetic codes went through a cheap photocopier. This was the generation when none of the babies were quite what they seemed. These were the years when puppies lay squirming on puppy blankets, all sticky and feeble and new and not quite puppies.

Cautiously I sank to my hands and knees and extended one palm over the specimen. The hideous insect tentatively twitched and squeaked. Then it rushed at me, a sickening scrambling concentration of vitality. I crushed it under my glove. The crunch was deafening.

Luckily for purposes of documentation, the cicada’s head escaped mangling. Its beady eyes glared at me while it died. I lifted my hand, and it hung from the underside, glued there by its own ichor, weakly waving its legs. Then it fell to the floor. I stuffed the corpse into a sandwich bag. The cicadas under the floor went on singing.

I had to simplify the floor plan. I went to my van for a crowbar. The sun was up, and the gravel was wet. A street scrubber drove slowly north along Stone, past the Presbyterian Church and Madelaine’s Beauty College.

Mrs. Everson might come home today. But she couldn’t stay here. I intended to make life very unpleasant for those cicadas.

The best thing would’ve been to burn the fucking house down.

That way I could’ve seen the bugs, snug in their cozy dens, warbling their miserable songs. I could’ve mapped their demographics and charted their social organization. Then I could’ve wiped them out—crushed their eggs, burned their larvae, tortured their drones for strategic information.

Long ago in a previous life, I had been a man built of flesh. For a year or so, I lived in a rainy city in a clammy basement. The fleas there became a problem, because they liked me. As food. I scratched my flea bites until they bled. When the blood dried, I scratched my scabs. Finally I went to the pet section of a supermarket and bought some flea powder and a couple of the flea collars for cats. I sprinkled the powder on my bed and my sofa. The collars, I wore around my ankles under my socks. It seemed like a great idea at the time. Unfortunately the collars were designed for an animal with fur.

I slept in the collars and woke the next morning with big black fleas hopping on and off of me as usual, and angry red water blisters that ran all round my ankles. With trembling hands I unbuckled the plastic shackles from my insulted flesh.

For days I lay on the sofa with my feet up on the backrest, while my puffy yellow ankles wept salty tears down my legs. Suffering for my stupidity, I underwent epiphanies of self-disgust. That’s what happens when you don’t read labels.

It was a good thing for me that I wasn’t flesh anymore. Flesh could be stung by bugs or even eaten. Plus, if I were flesh, I’d have asphyxiated myself by now. I’d be dead or delirious. Whereas I was reasoning with perfect clarity.

My van wasn’t faring so well. The car strippers had removed all its tires and pried loose its engine cover. A tire iron had shattered its windows. Pebbles of auto glass littered the driver’s seat. The strippers applied hacksaws from the van’s own tool kit. The van exploded in slow motion, like a carburetor schematic, clusters of parts floating in midair. There went the alternator. There went the batteries.

What if they find the silica wafers of my brain, Alex? What then? Where are my car keys, Alex? For the love of mercy, help me!

OCTURNENOCTURNENOCTURNENOCTURNENOCTURNENOCTURNENOCTU

An old woman was sleeping under a sofa. A robot sat on top of the sofa. Neither of us were breathing. It was dark outside. A siren wailed, across town somewhere.

I climbed down from the sofa and paced the living room. All the faucets in the house were running. Mrs. Everson’s dresses were stuffed down the drains and into the cracks under the doors. The water lay an inch deep on the floor. All escape routes were blocked. I paced the room, waiting for the water to rise, slipping on loose tiles and place mats—half indoors, half out, half crazy, half dead.

I told myself: Don’t panic. Whatever happened, I mustn’t panic. If I could continue to reason with perfect clarity, all would be well.

A deep dark well. With three sisters at the bottom, eating treacle and feeling ill. A deep dark ill.

I stood beside a blacked-out window in the dead of night. Everything seemed to be shrinking. The walls shrank from the floors. The alley gravel shrank from the patio bricks. Tire tread shrank from asphalt, billboards from the sky, and the stars from the earth. Women shrank from men, and men from one another. I couldn’t sleep. I couldn’t make sense of anything. Somewhere in the neighborhood a mariachi band was playing—accordion and trumpets. It sounded like a party.

The earth spun under my boots. The stars circled Mrs. Everson’s roof. I had burned all her magazines. Something was chewing on my leg.

FTERMATHAFTERMATHAFTERMATHAFTERMATHAFTERMATHAFTERMATH

Time passed. The water company shut off the water. The power company cut the power. Batteries emptied, I shared the ruined house with my friends, the bugs. The moon-scorched skeleton of my van stood in the alley, corroding in the winter downpours.

On the day they cut the power, the picture on the TV screen contracted to a white dot and blinked out. Sometimes children threw rocks through the windows, but I ignored them. The cicadas would take care of them. The cicadas had driven the red ants from Tucson, and were eating the last of the citizens.

I lay on my back with my head in a broom closet. A mop handle and some gallon bottles of ginger ale kept me company. I kept forgetting things. I forgot what I was doing. (Flooding the house.) I forgot what I’d done. (Lost my car keys.) I forgot what I’d planned. (Someday the roof of the house would cave in, and the sun would bleach my optical sockets.) All my vital parts had been pried loose and carried off.

I told jokes to the cicadas. I said, Stop me if you’ve heard this. The cicadas brought me food and taught me things. I couldn’t eat the food, but I learned quite a lot. Things about families and about deserts and about soul murder.

I can’t eat this, I kept telling them. They thought I was joking.

During mating season, the male cicadas turned blue and began to glow. They crept around the ceiling like winking constellations. I wondered whether insects were machines, and whether machines were insects. Certain insects can fly, but not every insect, and the same is true of machines. Flying machines can kill people, but so can insects. Everything had converged to a single dot, but it wouldn’t blink out.

In spring, the male cicadas grew wings and buzzed around me. They dove at my face and whizzed away again. They dug tunnels into my chest and sharpened their mandibles on my servos. When I told them to stop, they said, Make us stop! Corpses can’t give orders!

Then I told them that I would make them stop. They squealed with glee and rolled around inside me, kicking their legs.

I stood up and found a bucket and a length of hose. I kicked down the kitchen door and tramped out to the alley. The moon was full. It was bright as day. Pellets of glass crunched beneath my boots. I siphoned some methanol from the tank of the van and poured it over my head. I went indoors again for a match.

Soon I was blazing away like a pudding in brandy. The drapes caught fire as well. Roasted cicadas clambered from my armpits and my crotch and fell, smoking, to the floor. Shrieking elated bug shrieks they fell, dying happy bug deaths, like mad airmen abandoning a burning bomber.

From all directions, from below and above, a delighted stridulation sprang up. The cicadas pranced in circles around my feet, cheering me on. My black vinyl face was dripping. The drops trailed blue flame and made funny noises. The bugs turned cartwheels, laughing wildly.

Sparks flew from my optics. Brown smoke filled the house. I turned into a tottering scarecrow of silver sticks and black rags. But I was glad, because the bugs were entertained.

I fell to the floor. The flames guttered. The cicadas climbed up onto my wreckage in droves, to celebrate. Papa cicadas in blue overalls and mama cicadas in hoopskirts and petticoats waltzed on my chest. Cicada kids rushed to the final fires of my face, with tiny marshmallows on toothpicks. Nursemaid cicadas in starched white bonnets pushed larvae in strollers up and down my legs. Eventually they raised a tent on my torso and performed circus acts on tiny unicycles and tiny trapezes.

Personally I don’t attach a lot of importance to nightmares. Anyone can have a bad dream. Even machines get them. They’re a form of torture testing. You go on to the next thing. And the next. And the next.

The object of this game is to shoot down the Enemy Memory Ships before they can sink your Dream Boat. Are you ready? Are you set? Are you trapped in a toy truck in a toy chest in a hole at the bottom of the sea? Do you think you escape before you suffocate? What do you think, Alex? Can you get out of there? Can you get out?

ANITATIONSANITATIONSANITATIONSANITATIONSANITATIONSANITATIONS

I was sitting in the cab of the van, one afternoon in spring. We were parked behind a Chinese restaurant that was having some trouble with roaches. I opened the glove compartment and reached inside for a tire gauge.

My glove closed around a plastic bag that squished. I pulled it out, and what do you think was in it? A cream cheese and jelly sandwich on white bread. Which was crawling with maggots. I tossed it out the window.

But I could never figure out how it got there. I mean, who would put a sandwich of all things in my glove compartment? And leave it there to rot? Who would do that?

I could never account for it.

CHAPTER ELEVEN

THE FROZEN MAMMOTH

“So young a child,” said the gentleman sitting

opposite to her, (he was dressed in white paper),

“ought to know which way she’s going, even if

she doesn’t know her own name!”

—Lewis Carroll

Dear Alex. Last night I dreamed that I wrote you a letter.

I wasn’t a brontosaur in the dream. I was little, like Eva. A woman. I was a woman writing you a love letter.

The year was 1999 in my dream, and I hadn’t seen you for nine long years. I hoped that you hadn’t forgotten me. The human race was poised on the brink of extinction, but you were all I thought about. Dear Alex. I thought I could distract you from replacing yourself with machines.

I would have gone looking for you, but I was confined to a brig. The brass were keeping hundreds of us in a women’s army prison, half a mile beneath the surface of Antarctica. A low-security prison, since there was nowhere to escape to.

At night, lying on my bunk under scratchy blankets, I’d leave my body and drift into the sky above the ice. The sunsets were gorgeous. They went on for days. Then the southern lights would ripple like banners across the star field. Except for the blowing of the snow, it could’ve been the moon up there. Some nights I’d drift north to the ocean and cross the waves to see what the human race was up to.

There was a war on, Alex. A global plasma war. Particle beams would land on people’s heads and microwave them. The boomerang masers were interesting machines. The technical boys shaped these rays from coherent light and then fed in hydrogen plasma, which is what the sun is made of. You could fire them ground to ground like missiles, because the light bent. Geodetic torque, they called that. No one could really explain it. It broke several laws of physics. But it worked.

It worked, so they used it. There was speculation, at first, that the beams might suck the whole solar system into an artificial black hole. But that was just alarmism. All the beams did was punch holes in the earth’s crust and cover half the land masses with lava. This was inconvenient for civilians, of course. But by then most of the soldiers had gone to the north pole.

That was because the masers would only fire toward magnetic south. The tactical key to wasting your enemy was to dig in your ruby cannons north of him. Which accounts for all the heavy action over the Arctic Ocean. From there you could broil anybody. Antarctica was the least strategic area, which was why they stuck us there.

And the ordinance for the arctic circle actions. You should have seen it, Alex. Amphibious megaskeletons, bigger than houses. Walking fortresses that shrugged off anything short of a nuke. But once they plugged you into one, you were in there for life. Cutting you loose would be like freeing a crab from its shell. Right up your alley, Alex.

When I wasn’t drifting around in the sky, I’d wash dishes at the mess hall, or lift weights at the gym, or just sit in the commons and listen to the radio. I wondered about the masterminds who were directing all the mayhem. Did they really hope to survive? Or was I missing the point? Did they intend, instead, to incinerate themselves and all of us with them? Like mummified pharaohs and their entombed slaves. Why do that? Maybe just to prove that they owned the world. After all, you don’t own something unless you can destroy it. That was one possible rationale for their elaborate suicide.

But I had a different theory. I thought that the men in power were running scared. I thought they were terrified that Something would happen in the year 2000. So terrified, they had to keep a loaded pistol pressed against their heads at all times. Just in case they had to check out in a hurry. In case the Dreaded Something should come to pass. I think they were afraid of the angels returning to earth—afraid that the earth angels would descend on their sandboxes and take away their toys. But I could’ve been wrong.

Or I could be lying. In fact, Alex, I am lying. I wasn’t in any women’s army prison. I didn’t really know where I was.

My superior officers had tied me to a chair in a bare white room. I was being interrogated. The brass were unhappy with me. I’d deserted my post and cannibalized two civilians. I felt sorry about it, but a fat lot of good being sorry did.

I was writing a love letter in my head. My superiors wouldn’t give me any paper or any clothes to wear either. And they wouldn’t turn on any lights. I was good and sick of sitting naked in the dark. They said I could have clothes when I confessed. But I had confessed. I’d confessed back in 1990. I’d confessed a million times. Weren’t they listening? “I give up!” I told them. “I’ll tell you anything! Anything you want to hear!” It did no good.

“I further testify that I make this statement of contrition under no coercion and in full awareness of my rights to legal counsel,” I told them. “I accept that all polygraph tracings can and will be used against me by this duly-convened court-martial of drooling demeaned corpse-Martians, to fry my eyes or to gas my ass, and I’d swear to all that if you bastards would take the gag out of my mouth. Then you could burn my arm with a light bulb. At least I’d see your ugly faces.”

But they wouldn’t let me see where I was. Or what I was. That was probably wise. Given my medical history, I could be almost anything.

“Do something, you chicken shits. Unbuckle the straps. Pull off the goggles. Yank those cotton balls out from between my toes. You’ll be sorry, you four-star decorated turds. I’ll grind your balls between my teeth! Let me go! Where have you put Alex?”

“I’ll confess. Don’t hit me. I’ll confess. Just don’t rush me. Don’t press me. I’m brittle. I’ll crack.”

“That’s correct, Sir. I went over the wall. I was bored, Sir. I needed some recreation.”

“That’s right, Sir. I met them at the bowling alley.”

“Because I had to, Sir. I couldn’t help myself. I wasn’t thinking straight.”

“No, no, it wasn’t like that. I know I did wrong, Sir, but I’m not a well person. I had been in a can for twenty years.”

The accused burst into tears. Counsel for the defense addressed the court.

“Officers of the bench,” she began, “I ask you to imagine the mental state of a sensitive young woman immersed in liquid nitrogen. Visualize a human nervous system frying in a casserole pan of garlic butter. Picture, if you will, the frenzied writhing of the spinal cord, the lashings of the grosser nerves as they clutch for purchase at the sides of the pan and succeed only in basting themselves. Who can doubt that the accused has suffered enough? I request the court’s permission to enter into evidence a series of slides. (Lights please.) These are the accused and her victims at the time of her arrest.”

Sounds of gagging among the spectators. A rush of scuffling shoes toward the exits.

Lies, Alex. I’m telling you lies. I never dream about interrogations. Would you like to know who I really dreamed I was?

I was that little drowned girl who fell through the ice of the skating pond. Remember her? She was still underwater, hammering up at the ice with her little blue fists.

Long green weeds uncoiled from the bottom of the pond. They reached for the little girl’s ankles and dragged her down into the muck. And all the while they shouted.

“Three cheers!” shouted the weeds. “Three cheers for our patriotic soldiers! Three cheers for our girls in the freezer cans!”

And here came the cans! Marching across the muck! A whole platoon of cans, with pipe cleaners for legs and eyes of shining chrome. Marching along and waving to the weeds in a flurry of white confetti. Trampling the weeds beneath their boots. Wind them up, and they’ll march off the edge of the world. Through sleet, through hail, through the chattering of a thousand Geiger counters. . .

But where were they going, Alex? Marching naked through the snow? Where were they bound for? Turning white as they stained the snow red? In circles of course. In circles. Collapsing one by one, like snowflakes on an oven. And no two exactly alike.

But the drowned girl thought of an escape plan. She still had her skates on, so she slashed at the weeds and kicked her way back to the ice. She set her blades against it and skated upside-down through the water.

Faster and faster she skated, like a scissors on a greased cookie sheet. Finally she came to a pretty inverted village built of candy. The houses were gingerbread, with roofs of butterscotch taffy. The streets were cobbled with malt balls. Cotton-candy sheep grazed on hillocks of lime sherbet. In the distance stood rock-candy mountains with whipped-cream snowcaps.

The girl felt dizzy. She approached a chocolate-cupcake cottage and knocked at the door.

A slot opened up in the pink-frosting roof. A licorice machine gun poked its nose out. A hail of jellybean bullets flew through the peaceful streets. Fleecy white sheep crumpled on a hillside, bleating mournfully and bleeding strawberry syrup.

Lies. Lies and more lies. I was never that little drowned girl. Shall I show you my real face?

It was 1908. The Siberian tundra. The wind harried rags of cirrus across a sky as white as bone. The land lay beneath like a wrinkled bedspread, all ravines and moraines. For the first time in decades, the permafrost had thawed. Streams gurgled across porous rust-red soil, carrying scraps of dead moss. To the north loomed a sheer wall of ice.

To the south, four bearded men in parkas walked toward the glacier. Hounds capered around their boots, barking far and faint. One of the men pointed a mittened hand at the glaring ice. What was he pointing at? What made the dogs so excited? Something in the ice was partly melted free from the glacier. Something grayish brown and shaggy, hanging loose. The hounds bounded toward it.

Before the men could reach it, the hungry dogs had chewed up its forelegs. They snapped at one another and ripped at the good red meat. The men ran at them, yelling, and kicked them away. Then the men stood and stared in silence at the yellow tusks, the dangling elephant’s trunk, the matted fur. Only the eyes had decayed. Bright black tears slid down from two small black holes.

In the weeks and months that followed, newspapers and scientific journals would announce the discovery. A woolly mammoth—a creature extinct since the dawn of history. The men would remember it all their lives. And one of the men wondered, as the years passed, whether he’d really seen the beast move, whether it had really turned its head aside, as the dogs were tearing at it.

And on his death bed, the man thought to himself, What a strange fate. To sleep so long. And to awaken finally, blind and helpless, just as some dogs are ripping out your throat.

Lies. Lies like all the rest. Let’s change the subject. While we’re at it, let’s change my name.

My name was Noqi. I was an Inuit woman, centuries before the coming of the white men. My clan were fishers, and we roamed the Aleutian Islands in our kayaks.

As a girl I lived at the karigi of my aunt. My cousins and I stayed warm and dry there. We chewed hides, carved sewing needles from walrus ivory, and played cat’s cradle with loops of sinew. But then I married.

I married an anutquq, the wizard of our clan. When he asked for me, my aunt was delighted. All of us thought him the most admirable of men. He protected our families from sickness. His amulets brought luck to the seal hunters. And every spring he lured a dying whale to our island. But my husband was not what he seemed.

Soon after we married, a dark dream visited my sleep. In the dream I saw a spirit creature with one eye and three legs and flippers. This was the spirit that became my first aua. She offered to be my helper, but only if I would learn the true nature of my husband.

My husband was no anutquq, she told me, but rather an ilisitsoq, an evil wizard and a master of deception. His hands were polluted with the blood of uncounted innocents. At night he turned his soul into a ptarmigan and flew to other islands, other villages. There he made his mischief. He took the form of an invisible bear and slew men in their sleep. He raped women. He entranced children and led them to the empty lands to freeze. But of all this, my village knew nothing.

I tried to conceal my knowledge from my husband. It was useless. He saw. He sent his long-fanged auas against me, as I slept within my furs. I took a fever and couldn’t eat. The shadow of my death fell upon me.

My one-eyed friend returned. She offered to save me, but only on condition that I become an anutquq. When I agreed, she called her brother and sister auas from the sea. Her sisters were spiny blowfish, and her brothers were giant ice beetles, all crimson and yellow. For days and for nights they battled, tooth and claw, with the auas of my wicked husband. Finally my auas were victorious, and I shook off my fever. My husband’s auas deserted him. My ice beetles crept inside his heart. He sickened and died.

My village should have rejoiced. Instead they imagined that I’d poisoned my husband’s food. They knew how I hated him. But none believed my account of his crimes. They thought me a mad woman. The elders condemned me to a murderer’s death.

The men chose an ice floe, down by the sea, and there they dug a pit. The women bound my limbs with leather thongs and cast me into the pit. The men broke the ice raft loose from the shore. It drifted out to sea, with me and my guardian aua and all my bitter memories.

The wind howled above me, and the pack ice tipped and groaned. I wished that I could stand up and watch the churning of the waves as I died. My aua gnawed at my bonds until they broke. I climbed from my grave and stood on my raft, naked in the wind, as the ice floes crashed and spun. I prayed to the ocean mother Sedna that my soul might find its way to the land of my ancestors. The wind sucked my life away and left behind an ice statue.

My ice raft drifted south until it melted. My body sank to the ocean floor and fed the tiny crabs and benthic worms.

My soul walked west across the breakers, through spray and racing shadows. I reached the Gate of Bones. There stood the ghost of my husband, unmoving amid the chaos of air and ocean. He was waiting for me, blocking my path, with his fishing spear in his fist.

“You have slain my flesh,” he told me. “But I will kill your spirit.”

He cast his spear at me. My one-eyed aua caught it through her belly and dove into the sea with it. I wrestled with my husband, rolling across the jagged whitecaps, as he wrung my neck. I yanked out his hair and gouged at his eyes. I pinned him beneath my hips and sewed his lips together with the fishbone needles of my hatred. Then I called down a killing wind. It blew in through his ears, inflated him like a bladder, and burst him apart. It carried off his brains in ribbons of sparkling blood and eels of gray mist.

But all the while I loved him, Alex. Evil and hateful as he was, I loved him. After I destroyed him, I wept stinging tears and raised a lament to Sedna. And Sedna heard me. She created my husband anew. She lifted us to the heavens and set us among the burning stars and snaking rainbows. And there we continue to wrestle, for we are still at war. We can never rest. And the wind blows through us.

But you don’t believe in ghosts, do you, Alex? You’re quite right. I’m lying again. I was never that woman.

I was just a little girl, just ten years old. My name was Naomi.

Year after year, every day of every week, Naomi sat in the rec room of the Disturbed Children’s Ward. She sat on a chair beside a window and looked down through wire mesh at the hospital grounds and the hospital basketball court. At meal times, an orderly would take her wrist and lead her to the dining hall. When she returned, someone had always moved her chair. Naomi moved it back, while the other children watched a television that chattered on a steel shelf.

She spoke to no one. She never played checkers or dice games. After Lights Out, she lay in her bed and dreamed of the grounds and the basketball court, because she liked things simple. Some nights she wet her bed. Someday she would turn into a brontosaur. But Naomi didn’t know that.

Naomi was what the guardians of mental health called a Chronic Autistic. There were institutions for children like Naomi, and that was exactly where she was. If you asked her how she got that way, she didn’t answer. Not even if you grabbed her by the shoulders and shouted in her face. For all she cared, you could be on the moon. Some children, you see, don’t need wars or famines or plagues to convince them that vegetables have the right idea about life. Some children are convinced from the start.

Naomi lay in her bed and pretended that she was inside a jar of ice, where nothing could reach her. The roof of the ward would dissolve, and she would watch the clouds drift across the stars. The clouds would just be shapes at first. Then they’d start to mean things. One cloud looked like a flattened toad. The next looked like the skull of a horse. Another looked like a giant crow, hanging in the wind and casting its shadow over the earth.

Naomi heard a Man In Power reciting a countdown. Five, four, three. . .

Suddenly she was an inmate of a different institution—a troop repository in the Marianas Trench.

She was back inside a can. As though she’d never left. Mute. Numb. Rigid. Naked as a jaybird. Salt in her wounds and mercury in her blood. Electrified and hysterical. Frozen alive in a hole at the bottom of the sea. She could hardly believe it. And for all she knew, they’d frozen Alex and Eva too. That’s how careful they were about infection.

She sensed thousands of cans around her in the bunker, all filled with other soldiers. The bunker was a complete necropolis, a Noah’s ark launched into the afterlife. After the war, the soldiers would have everything they needed. Frozen food, frozen blood plasma, frozen sperm. . . They seemed to be perfectly safe.

But Naomi didn’t trust the Men In Power. She wanted to escape. The other soldiers thought she was nuts. But she knew she could do it. She’d done it before. She monkeyed with her chromatin, groping for a genotype that could bust her out. Her flesh began to flow and shift. She simplified to an oblong bluish lump that rested like a bundle of knots at the bottom of her can. The lump recited nursery rhymes to itself.

Potatoes were the waiters. Potatoes were the band. Potatoes were the dancers, kicking up the sand.

Inside the lump was a curd, and inside the curd, a brain. Inside the brain was a red brick building which housed the Deoxyribonucleic Academy Library. And down in the library basement, among the reference stacks, was Naomi.

She’d cleared out a foxhole under some shelves. She crouched there, ransacking drawers from a card catalog. She was looking for one particular card. It had to be there somewhere. Meandrina, the brain coral. No. Dipnoi, the lungfish. No. Earwig, eyebright, fingerling. . . No no no. . .

She attacked the problem theoretically. If mutation, M, was mild genetic chaos yet fueled the orderly countertrend of evolution, E, and if hard radiation, R, was super-chaos—then the question arose: What countertrend X existed, such that X was to R as E was to M? I ought to know that, thought Naomi.

The lump grew a fur of cilia that beat against the nitrogen in the canister. The lump began to revolve. Intentional metaplasia. Mind over mud. A bolt of lightning crossing the primeval murk. Total war. When the pulse wave hit, the roentgens would be close behind. What happened before can happen again. The divergent speciation of the trilobites. The spirit moving on the face of the waters.

Fingers and toes rose from the yeasty lump. Mouths opened under the fingernails, and steam bubbled up. Muscles detached from their tendons and glided like manta rays to new anchorages.

What happened before. . . Please don’t throw me in the briar patch! Can happen again. Please don’t throw me in the briar patch!

Naomi’s thyroid clambered down her windpipe like a tree frog. Her adrenal glands squirmed and stretched and crept like inchworms up the webs of her mesentery. Her kidneys clung together in ecstatic excretory embrace and plunged stiff nephritic pricks into each other.

Born and raised! Born and raised! Born and raised in the briar patch!

But what was the name of that dinosaur, the big one? What was the name? Trying to remember the name, Naomi dissolved. She entered into solution with the nitrogen, like a lump stirred into pudding. She had reached the acute phase of her hyperorthochordocephalocerebropanchromomitophytomorphogangliplasia.

Just at that moment, her can and all the other cans in the bunker heated to a glowing white and exploded from internal pressure. Naomi watched it happen. Thinking, This is the blast. Thinking, I am the blast shadow.

Thinking, Brontosaur! That’s the word!

Naomi is ten years old. She stands on a narrow steel platform at the top of a tall steel pole, under a circus tent. She wears powder blue leotards with sequins. She rubs the soles of her slippers in a chalk box. Far below, on a runway between two sections of bleachers, three elephants in plumed headdresses pick at the sawdust with their trunks.

In the center ring, a man in a top hat declaims into a megaphone. “And now, Ladies and Gentlemen, a very special performance. For the first time anywhere, a ten-year-old Chronic Autistic will dive, blindfolded and half naked, one hundred yards into a bucket of crushed ice. Silence please.”

A spotlight illuminates the platform. A drum roll sounds.

Naomi curls her toes over the edge.

And leans forward.

CHAPTER TWELVE

THE HUSH

. . .it’s one of the most serious things that can

possibly happen to one in a battle, to get one’s

head cut off.”

—Lewis Carroll

Tonight we seem to be buried under tons of sand. I can hear the two of you talking, but your voices are muffled. Still it’s nice to know that you’re buried nearby.

I just had a dream that left a contented feeling in my belly. I dreamed of a time when I was happy, a time when my life made sense. It was the twenty-first century, after the War, and I was still walking the earth. My sisters and I were climbing the fire escape of a derelict hotel in Phoenix, Arizona. We were hunting for dogs.

What are dreams, I wonder. I drop down exhausted at the end of a day. I blur at the edges. I turn the blurring into stories, and I call that dreaming. Back in the twenty-first century I did it just the way I do it now. But back then I was a woman in the right place. My life was hard, but it was sane. Being sane was easy, before I married Alex.

I was climbing a fire escape in Phoenix. The soles of my feet were like leather, and the steel was cold beneath them. The morning light was warm on my skin, and on the straight black hair that fell down my back to the belt of my jeans. A canteen and a hatchet hung from the belt.

Those were good years for me, the aftermath of the Boomerang War. A timeless tranquility had fallen over the land. A great hush. I hoped it would last forever.

The wall of the hotel was red brick, deeply pitted by the debris that had ridden the final shit storm. The sun was peeking into the canyons of the streets. A crescent moon still hung in the sky. I came to a landing and leaned against a railing. I waited for Jo Ann to catch up with me. We both had to catch up with Ox.

Ox and Jo Ann were sisters of a kind, and they’d adopted me into their family. They were women of a new race, the post-war format.

I leaned over the rail and listened for Jo Ann. Below me at street level, a particle beam had cut a smooth oval pit deep into the earth. The walls of the pit were black glass, perfectly vertical. The building across the street was half vaporized, sliced in two. I could see into its honeycomb of rooms—den above den, bathroom above bathroom, as though a giant had been playing with scissors. Moldering mattresses and recliner chairs were decorated with crisp brown mummies. Hundreds of black glass pits had perforated Phoenix. Like a stop sign that somebody’d used for rifle practice.

I heard Jo Ann sneaking up the flight of steps below me. I pretended not to notice. She pounced on me and tickled my ribs. I spun around and hugged her. She screamed with delight.

She wore a gunny sack and a belt of rope. Her skin was deep gray like modeling clay. Her hair was bleached out and wispy. She was eight feet tall, and if the pains in her ankles got worse, she was going to need crutches.

She wriggled in my arms and pushed me away. Her eyes shone with trust and humor. It was a long way down, but we were underfed and out of breath and just didn’t care. We started to climb again.

I liked the new crop of people. Mentally they were just like a certain kind of people I’d seen before the War. The rest of us had names for people like that. Retards. Congenital idiots. Defectives. But the joke was on us. Those people were throw-forwards. Their only misfortune was being born before their time. Now their time had come, and wherever they went, they were right at home. The inheritors of the earth. Jo Ann and Ox sensed that I wasn’t one of them, but they opened their hearts to me anyway. Retards are like that.

Jo Ann and I arrived at the top floor. Ox was waiting for us, sitting cross-legged on the steel bars. She was three feet tall, with skin black as tar and no hair at all. She wore a loincloth of dog hide.

Ox had seen a pack of dogs in the hotel. She’d seen it in a dream. We’d climbed the fire escape so that they wouldn’t smell us coming.

We went through a window into a hallway. Ox caught the scent of the dogs and went off to explore, very excited. Jo Ann and I had to catch our breath. I leaned against a wall and took off my sunglasses. I stared at them—orange lenses and copper wire.

Ox was another birth defect. Even before the War, their numbers had been on the rise. I read a newspaper article about it. Medical science had detected an alarming increase of Oxes and Jo Anns among the newborns. A few years later the articles took on a tone of subdued panic. All of the recent babies were now falling into one or another of the boxes marked Damaged Goods. For some of the babies, the people in charge were having to build entirely new boxes. Then the War came along and blew all the boxes right out the window, along with sterile bandages, antibiotics, medical science, and newspapers. I was glad to see them go.

I folded my glasses and hooked them on my belt.

Ox came back to us, wiggling her fingers. She’d found a dog. Jo Ann and I hurried after her. I grabbed up a stick. Ox led us to a stairwell, took us three floors down, and made us stop. She poked her head through the banister posts and listened. Her head was lop-sided and smooth, like a black stone from a riverbed.

Claws scuffled across linoleum. We moved silently along a hallway. Ox and I peeked around a corner. A gray poodle, big as a hog, was sniffing at a pile of trash. It worried a stiff white towel between its jaws. Then it lifted its small frizzy head and smelled the air.

Ox charged at it. Fear dawned in its damp pink eyes. It scrambled away up the hall, the blubber jouncing on its flanks. We all ran after it, waving our sticks and yelling. Jo Ann doubled back to block the staircase. Then Ox and I split up, hoping to surround it.

We found it again on the fire escape. One of its legs had slipped through the bars and gotten wedged. It whimpered when it saw us closing in. Finally it just crouched down and trembled, hanging there in the breeze, high above a back alley in that ruined city.

Ox jabbed her stick between two of its ribs. She pushed the stick in deep and worked the pointed end around in its guts. I grabbed the matted fur on top of its skull and hacked at the neck with my hatchet. I yanked off the head and held it up for Jo Ann to see. Watery pink blood dripped down through the steel bars.

I tossed the head to Jo Ann and helped Ox to free the trapped leg. We dragged the carcass into the hallway. It was like two hundred pounds of fuzzy caterpillar. If we smoked the meat, it could last us quite a while.

The poodle growled at us.

Jo Ann nearly took off running. Headless dogs that growled were a little too spooky for her.

I poked with my stick at the wiry fur around the neck stump. Something feeble sunk its teeth in the end of my stick. When I pulled on the stick, we all saw what had growled at us. A second poodle head—buried in fur and even smaller than the first. Its rubbery pink neck was stretched now almost to the snapping point, but it wouldn’t let go.

Ox whacked the neck in two with her stick. I stumbled backwards against a wall and slid down the wallpaper, my stick still in my fist, and the poodle head still clamped to the end of it.

Jo Ann decided that this was the funniest thing she’d ever seen. She laughed and laughed. Ox grabbed the stick from my hand and danced around her, waving the poodle head and singing a nasal song. The three of us went reeling down the corridor, laughing so hard we had to hold each other up. Jo Ann and I stumbled through a doorway and fell onto a mattress. She kissed me, and my tongue was in her mouth, and life was sweet.

Life was ideal.

In the cool of the evening, we spitted dog meat on coat hangers and roasted it over a trash fire in a steel barrel.

We passed the night in a hotel room. We had ways to amuse ourselves. Jo Ann played with her pinworms. She’d prick a pin into the soles of her feet until one of them stuck its head out. Then she’d nip its head between her fingernails and drag out its body. We were all lousy with pinworms. They made squiggly blue tattoos on our skin. When Jo Ann got tired of one, she’d bite its head off. It doesn’t take much to keep some people happy.

Ox kept a pouch of treasures on a thong around her neck. In quiet moments she’d lay them out and inspect them. Ox collected the smooth jewels of colored glass that she found in the gizzards of the dogs we killed. Besides the lumps of green or amber glass, she carried a few tinker toys—little green sticks and round wooden connectors drilled full of holes. She put them together and took them apart again. She could do that for hours.

I went up on the roof and watched the sky going dark over the city. I thought about my years in Tahuantinsuyu, when I’d stand on rooftops and pray to Huehueteotl the father of fire. “Come down, mighty Huehueteotl,” I would pray. “Come and destroy all the works of my people. Rain down your fiery justice.”

Perhaps he’d heard me. Perhaps he’d answered my prayers.

But long before he did, I’d crossed the border into Texas. And there, for the first time, I watched television. And one Saturday morning I saw a Flash Gordon serial. I recognized Mongo instantly. I’d grown up on Mongo. The gene priests must have built Tahuantinsuyu from secondhand movie sets. There were the Clay People. There were the Hawk People. There were the slaves in the furnace room. The gene priests had stuffed Mongo’s corpse. They’d painted it, powdered it, perfumed it, and anointed it with aromatic llama butter, and still it had stunk like a dead thing.

“The earthlings are doomed!” In how many episodes did Ming the Merciless say, “The earthlings are doomed!”? But the earthlings didn’t need any help from outer space. We built our own death rays and trained them on ourselves. We died with our makeup on. Dr. Zarkov couldn’t save us. And a whole new crop of earthlings sprouted like mushrooms from our ashes.

As the stars were coming out, Jo Ann found a nest of termite grubs under a kitchen sink. We feasted on roast poodle and salty white grubs. Ox sat in a window frame, plucking larvae from the palm of her hand and tossing them into her mouth, one by one.

Late that night, warm in my lovers’ arms, I dreamed of a flying saucer.

We were crossing a vacant lot when the saucer descended, ringing like a bell and shining with a blue light. The aliens came down a ramp to meet us. They looked like jellyfish in garment bags.

They invited the three of us to leave the earth forever, to journey with them among the stars. They apologized for not arriving sooner. Humans, they said, had only recently been declared an endangered species. Now, at last, we were eligible for evacuation.

I told them no. I told them that we liked it where we were.

CHAPTER THIRTEEN

LOW PRESSURE AREA

Jerry Hall,

he is so small,

a rat could eat him,

hat and all.

—Mother Goose

Dr. Mazer sat at his desk. His office was bare now, except for his desk and chair and the video monitors in their niche beside the door. He hunched over the desk, writing in a notebook with a fountain pen. He was working up a progress report on his three patients.

In their dreams they’d been dealing with various traumas from the early parts of their lives. But these weren’t the traumas that Mazer wanted them to deal with. What was worse, they refused to form themselves into a functioning family. They refused to adjust. They just kept moving farther apart.

Mazer flipped to the back of his notebook and wrote the first thing that came into his head.

Once upon a time, there were three little bugs who lived on a helium balloon, floating in the sky. Around and around the white rubber surface of the balloon they crawled, until they were bored to distraction. Then one of the bugs, a girl bug named Naomi, had a wonderful idea.

He started over.

There was once an old man who could build the most devious mazes. He lived on a small island in the Mediterranean Sea, where his only companions were his son and the ghost of his murdered wife. He wasn’t a bad man, but he needed a lot of gold to proceed with his architectural researches. The old man’s

Mazer put down his pen and rubbed the back of his neck. He pulled open a desk drawer and took out the remote control for the video monitors. He tapped a button, and one of the screens began to glow. An image formed from the static.

A man in work clothes ran down the aisle of a warehouse between steel racks three stories tall. He was pursued by a mechanical hand, four yards long.

Mazer pushed two more buttons. The image of Alex shrank to a dot, while Naomi’s screen brightened.

Headlights sliced through white fog. Private Naomi drove a stolen jeep through the pine forests of British Columbia. Her teeth chattered, and her rain-soaked coveralls clung to her skin. Then all at once she was flying, high above the trees, her arms spread like fins, as if swimming down a river of mist. She could see the jeep below her, steering itself along the winding black tarmac.

Dr. Mazer switched off the second monitor and turned on the third.

Eva was a doll, a few inches tall. She was piloting a one-woman submarine through the intestines of a behemoth plesiosaur which had swallowed her whole. The radio of the sub bleeped. It had picked up a homing signal. The rescue party had finally arrived.

Mazer jabbed again at the control box. The screen lapsed into darkness.

This is hopeless, he told himself. I’m totally lost.

Ensign Wing stood under a gloomy sky on a level expanse of green felt. Wind flapped the pants legs of his dress whites. He looked around, frowning, trying to get his bearings.

Weaver had sent him back for another talk with Mazer. He was here to present a proposal which Mazer would certainly reject. His real mission was reconnaissance. The three souls were somewhere in the castle, but where exactly? Weaver was anxious to find out.

Wing knew from long experience that prying a secret loose from Mazer was next to impossible. Concealment was second nature to the old angel. But the feeling within the Organization was that something must be done about Mazer. This imperative had risen like an angry storm front, and the first priority was to evacuate the innocents.

Wing turned toward a rumbling on the horizon. A triangular formation of pool balls, big as houses, was rolling toward him and picking up speed.

“Fuck this shit,” said Wing. He vanished in a shower of blue sparks.

Mazer pulled open another desk drawer. A faint chiming music drifted from the drawer. He reached down, took out a music box, and set it on the desk. It was carved from black walnut and inlaid with mother-of-pearl. A digital display was set into the front of it, glowing with two red numerals: 03.

Mazer took a key from the drawer and fit in into the side of the box. He turned the key counterclockwise, unwinding the spring. The box groaned. The music began to lose speed. Within a day the box would run down, and the music would end.

Mazer returned the box and its key to their drawer. In another drawer he found three death certificates. He applied himself to the filling out of the forms. Having disposed of the last three inmates at his asylum, he would go into retirement.

Alex was trying to wake up, but he kept slipping into dreams. In one dream he was sitting in the waiting room of a bus terminal, a man in a wrinkled suit, reading a magazine.

Naomi lay on the floor beside his chair, wearing the brontosaur. She took up much of the terminal’s floor space. Her neck was curled around behind him. She was reading over his shoulder. A policeman approached Alex and tapped him on the other shoulder.

“Sir? Is that your animal?”

Alex looked up. “Her? She doesn’t belong to me.”

“It’s going to have to leave, Sir. Animals aren’t allowed here.”

Alex studied the man. “Officer,” he said, “there’s something you should know about my friend.” He leaned forward confidentially. “She isn’t really a dinosaur. She just has a vivid imagination. You don’t want to evict the poor kid for what she’s thinking, do you?”

When Eva opened her eyes, she knew she was still dreaming. She found herself in an armchair in a tastefully decorated psychologist’s office.

Behind an oaken desk sat a white rat, five feet tall, in a lab coat, who was cleaning his tortoise shell glasses with a handkerchief. Behind him was a blackboard, on which were chalked the words KILL OR CURE. The rat fitted his glasses onto his pale bony snout.

“So what makes you think you’re immortal?” he asked her.

Eva didn’t trust the rat. But his question seemed worth considering.

“Well,” she said, “when I kill the others, or they kill me—we don’t die.”

“Do you ever dream of dying?”

“Dream? It’s all dreams. If we could wake up, we might be able to die properly.”

The rat knit his claws together and smiled. His face gleamed with sweat.

“But those dreams that come to you at night,” he insisted. “Have you ever died in one of those?”

Eva thought back over the centuries.

“No,” she answered slowly. “I think not.”

“So it’s conceivable that if you died in one of those dreams, you’d stay dead.”

“I suppose so.”

The rat sprang to his feet, knocking his chair over.

“You suppose so?” he shouted. “You suppose so? Do you agree or don’t you? That’s the trouble with you people! How do you expect to get better if you won’t think for yourselves?” His face was red as a beet.

Eva turned in her sleep and groaned.

That guy’s not right in the head, she told herself.

Once upon a time there was a little girl named Naomi, who was not very motivated and not very little either. She grew up in an orphanage in Alberta, where increasing signs of autism occasioned her transfer to a mental ward in British Columbia.

One day while she was looking out a window at a basketball court, an orderly told her that she was cured and would have to leave. The hospital had suffered budget cuts, and Naomi had been reclassified. She and a busload of other crazies were driven into downtown Victoria and dropped outside an apartment building. A fat man gave her a key and showed her to her room. Her room was one yard wide, and she couldn’t stand up in it, but Naomi had never had her own room before. She was overwhelmed with the luxury of it. She could be alone here, all day and all night, with no one to bother her. That night she was too excited to sleep.

The next day she walked around Victoria and found a park with a duck pond. Some old people were throwing bits of bread to the ducks. The autumn wind blew through her sweater. Clouds moved across the sky, white against the gray.

As Naomi was walking home, she passed the parking lot of a grocery store. A man backed his car out of a parking space very fast and came within inches of running her down. Pausing to shift gears, the man glanced at Naomi, who stood frozen in shock. He glanced at her, not apologizing, not angry, not anything. His face was as blank as a refrigerator door.

It came to Naomi that she wasn’t like other people and never would be. She decided that she wasn’t really human.

That night, lying in her new bed, she resolved to join the army and see the world. And when she got older, she did enlist. And the army changed her life. It changed her into a biochemical disaster area. Then it put her in quarantine on a deserted asteroid. There she could invent as many imaginary playmates as she liked.

Everything followed from what came before. It was just a question of putting together the pieces.

Ensign Wing walked down a hallway past an infinite series of doors with frosted glass windows. The clacking of his heels was brisk. He was irritated and trying not to show it. The hall tipped, forcing him to walk downhill. His calves began to ache. The hall narrowed until his shoulders were brushing the walls. He shrank to a height of ten inches without breaking his stride. Iron spikes grew from the ceiling, which began to descend. Wing clenched his fists and yelled down the hall.

“Dad! Quit fooling around. I have to talk to you.”

Wing exploded himself into a whirlwind of green sparks. When the sparks settled, he was outside the door of Mazer’s office. He tried his key, but the lock had been changed. He hammered at the glass with his knuckles. If a chair had been handy, he would’ve thrown it.

“Come on, Mazer. This is important.”

A dense black spillage of scrambling mutant pill bugs gushed out from under the door. They all tried to climb up Wing’s legs. He danced on the green carpet, slapping at his ankles.

“Not funny, Mazer.”

The pill bugs began to bite. A cascade of purple sparks showered the swarm. Wing went elsewhere.

The army granted Naomi a medical discharge. Then they sealed her in a chrome canister and dumped her in the ocean. At the bottom of the Pacific she was vaporized by a hostile particle beam. But she survived.

Who’d believe that?

Naomi groped for memories from after the year 2000. But as she tried to push her mind forward, she felt it slipping back through the ages, sliding past the dawn of humankind, all the way to the days of the dinosaurs.

She remembered the rainbow mist over the lagoons. . . the swaying kelp fields in the dancing sunlight. . . the darting clouds of shrimp that added their click songs to the murmur of creatures far and near. She saw trilobites creeping across the silt of tide pools, where ammonites pursued their arcane molluskan meditations.

Then she recalled the cycad forests—the perpetual overhang of pink volcanic ash and the flurries of gnats that tumbled through the fronds. She marveled at her sister archosaurs, resplendent in their quills and crests and horns, displaying their serrated dorsal fins of green and scarlet.

Naomi was a small graceful female, less than thirty tons. The herd’s new hatchlings were even smaller. But if they all stayed together, not even the rutiodons would bother them.

Through the endless afternoons the herd stood in the muck of their favorite inlet and grazed on sweet reeds. Dragonflies stitched among the horsetails, and flocks of hyperornis waded the shallows, puffing and flapping and shaking clumps of moss between their teeth. Naomi’s calves raked their muzzles across her ribs. She filled their mouths with regurgitated cud.

Then Naomi and her kind were blotted out with a swiftness beyond belief. They were devoured by a new species, water creatures so gigantic that they dwarfed even the saurians. The dinosaurs called them the Spoilers.

They evolved from the man-of-war and grew huge on the surface of the oceans. They killed by stinging. They were hollow animals, beasts of living origami with radial skeletons of cuttlebone and pearly white skins. They rolled up onto the beaches like moving cities, folding into and over themselves. Lifting their minarets against the purple skies, they scoured the continents and consumed the fern forests, slow and hideous and inevitable as doom.

An army of the Spoilers pursued Naomi’s herd. Strung across the horizon, they launched barbed ropes ahead of them, paralyzing all they touched. The night Naomi died, alone and out of breath, she stumbled into a tar pit. She knew what she was doing. She preferred to drown rather than vanish into the mindlessly folding membranes of the Spoilers as though she were an aphid in a mouthful of ferns. She sank into the tar.

In a matter of decades the Spoilers chewed the earth down to its rind. They all starved, leaving behind not a single fossil to mark their passing.

Long after Naomi’s flesh had liquefied, the tar held her bones in place. As they mineralized, their microstructures were perfectly rendered in stone. Every last cranial fossa was preserved, awaiting the electron microscope.

Naomi’s ghost clung to her bones, then to the stones that replaced them. She couldn’t bring herself to leave. She couldn’t believe that her life was over forever. Forever is a long time.

In April of 1927, in the late Jurassic stratum of a gypsum quarry in Colorado, the fossils of Naomi’s skull and spine were collected by a team of graduate students from the zoology department of Yale University. The fossils were shipped by rail to New Haven, cataloged, stored in crates in the basement of the Natural Sciences Library, and forgotten.

In the year 2000 the building was reduced to rubble by a particle storm. But down in the cellar, Naomi’s ghost continued to watch over the fossils. Reptilian nervous tissue conducts impulses at a rate of three feet per second. And forever is a long time.

In 2342 the fossils were rediscovered during a dig organized by the Atlantic Paleo-Genetic Reclamation Project of the Intersolar Terraforming Commission. They were just the sort of raw material that the project was looking for. The commission had set up a planet that was perfect for millet farming. But the soil there was very rocky. The commission envisioned the use of customized vocalizing apatosauri as plow animals. Naomi’s remains were singled out for intensive scanning.

Her ghost watched approvingly as new versions of her body were manufactured by the hundreds. She hovered over the glass tubes where cultured tissues were biosonically shaped into organs. When fifty war-surplus human brains were prepared, fifty skulls would be assembled around them. The workers welded the sutures together with jellied enzymes from caulking guns. Then fifty sets of ribs would be socketed into fifty spines, and teeth punched into the skulls, like so many rivets. Finally the hides were sewn on.

They weren’t real brontosaurs. They spoke instead of honking, and they had no gender. But they were close enough. Naomi’s ghost picked one out and sank into it like a long delayed hot bath.

The commission launched her into the depths of space aboard a silver baton that whirled end over end through the silent void. The ship carried thousands of people, food stores, machines, seed stocks, shelters, and farm animals. Yet it was roughly the size of a pool cue.

How could that be?

The terraformers had the use of EMT, Extreme Miniaturization Technology. Any object could be passed through a series of gravity wells which siphoned off its forces of interatomic repulsion, compressed it to a tiny wafer, and embedded it in a lattice of silica. In short, EMT could fit a brontosaur inside a grain of sand.

So the silver ship had no need to be bigger than a pool cue.

When the ship was loaded, Naomi was compressed into a microchip along with a woman named Eva and a solar-powered jeep named Alex. Unfortunately, due to a programming error, all three were squeezed into a single wafer, all mashed together. By rights they should have died. The loading error wasn’t discovered until years later, when they were expanded at the farming colony. But for some reason they came out of their microchip as good as new.

Or seemingly as good as new. They started to hear one another’s voices in their heads. They hadn’t gone mad. The voices did drive them mad eventually, but their illness was nothing so simple as insanity. They’d been fused into a single hologram. And any part of a hologram—

Put the pieces together, Naomi.

—contains the whole.

But nothing lasts forever. The music box for example was rapidly running down.

“I’m going to say a series of words,” said the white rat in the lab coat. “I’d like for you to respond with the first words that enter your mind. All right?”

In an armchair across from the rat’s desk, there sat a humanoid robot with a face of black leather and eyes of red glass. “All right,” said the robot.

“Husband.”

“Wife.”

“True.”

“False.”

“Father.”

“Son.”

“Family.”

“Dead.”

“Moon,” said the rat.

“Blood,” said the robot.

“Wife.”

“Slain.”

“Mad.”

“Scientist.”

“Caged.”

“Rat.”

“Rat,” the rat repeated.

“Maze,” said the robot.

“Maze,” the rat repeated, glaring.

“Murder,” said Alex.

All the lights went out.

In a clearing in a Malaysian jungle, an old woman lay on her back on the bare earth beside a bonfire. Reverend Weaver knelt at her side, sucking poison from her belly.

In a circle around the fire, women in bright silk sarongs swayed together in rhythm with their eyes closed. They sang an ancient healing song.

Weaver’s assistant held out a wooden bowl filled with orchid-scented water. Into the bowl Weaver spat a slimy black blob. She commended the blob to Ganesha the Reformer, performed her final blessings, and helped the old woman to sit up.

The circle of women dispersed, threading various paths through the underbrush. The bonfire burned low. Weaver stood alone under the lianas and the stars, gazing at the embers of the fire. Her assistant returned to the clearing.

“Someone wants to see you,” she told the reverend. “A navy boy. He’s down on the beach.”

Weaver nodded. “I was expecting him.”

She traced a descending trail through the palms. She walked along the wet sand at the edge of the surf. She found Ensign Wing sitting on a mangrove log, barefoot and bare chested, peeling bark from a stick.

“He won’t talk to me,” said Wing. “I blew it.”

Weaver and Wing walked down the shore together. The moonlight shimmered on the ocean. The waves rolled in. The sound of bamboo flutes was in the air.

“What have you been able to find out?” he asked her.

“He keeps them in a drawer of his desk, in a music box.”

“You knew I wouldn’t get through,” Wing complained.

“That’s right. I needed you for a distraction. And it worked.”

“But there’s something else. Something you aren’t telling me.”

“Yes. He’s decided to kill them.”

Wing stopped in his tracks. “Jesus!”

“He unwound the mainspring. Sometime tomorrow the box will implode. And that will be the end of his three patients. Their world will vanish and them with it. Unless we can shut him down.”

They continued their walk. Small crabs hurried into the brine at their approach.

“We’ll get hold of that box over Mazer’s dead body,” said Wing.

Weaver shrugged. “If necessary.” They sat down with their backs against a smooth boulder. Weaver arranged her sarong.

“Damn him,” muttered Wing. “Who does he think he is?”

“He’s senile. He sees them as evidence of his failure. He wants to erase every trace of them.”

Wing pushed the hair out of his eyes. “Erasure is a pretty extreme punishment for the crime of being insane.”

“I don’t think they’re even insane anymore. I suspect that they cured one another centuries ago. Mazer is just too far gone to see it.”

Wing sighed bitterly. “We have to call in Spiker, don’t we?”

“I’m afraid so. If you or I went up against your father, he’d cripple us.”

Warm rain began to patter down around them—on the sand, on the rocks, on the ocean. Somewhere a seagull cried.

Wing tried to think of some other way. Spiker was an angel who specialized in assassinations. When Spiker was involved in a situation, things got nasty. Wing didn’t feel like arguing. He rested his head on Weaver’s lap. The rain trickled down his face.

“What a mess,” he said. Weaver combed his long blonde hair with her fingers.

“It’s not every son,” she said, “who can survive such a father.”

Wing pounded the sand with his fist. “Fucking Mazer! I hate his guts!”

Wing’s anger made Weaver even angrier.

“That’s not enough, Wing. That never has been enough. We need a professional, and no one outside of the Organization will touch us with a ten-foot pole. So that leaves Spiker.”

“But if—”

“No! I’m fed up! I’ll see him dead and in hell before I’ll let him get away with this. Enough is enough. I want you to talk to Spiker.”

The rain fell harder. The waves rumbled and hissed.

“Do I have to?”

“Yes, my dear boy. You must.”

“Can’t you do it?”

“No, I’ll be busy. I’m going in after the three of them.”

Wing began to sit up. Weaver held him down. “Into the music box? He’ll see you.”

“No he won’t,” said Weaver. “I know a secret passage.”

Wing smiled. “I suppose it could work. Shall I go with you?”

“Better not.”

“But I could help.”

“Keep out of it, Wing. You’re angry. You’d only get in my way.”

Wing frowned. “God, I’m depressed.”

Weaver leaned down and kissed her son’s forehead.

And all the while the music box was running down.

Naomi felt sunlight on her eyelids. She tried to wake up. She wanted to see Alex again and Eva and the desert of white sand. But no matter how she tried, she couldn’t open her eyes. Was she paralyzed? Had her tumor finally reached her brain stem? Was she dead?

Then the dreams receded, and her sense of reality returned. She couldn’t raise her eyelids because she hadn’t any. An ant hasn’t got eyelids. She must forget all that nonsense about a dinosaur with a brain tumor.

By getting this straight, Naomi woke up. She drew the bristles of her forelegs across her antennae and her compound eyes. And there were the sand dunes. And there were Alex and Eva. And there was herself, a giant black ant, ten yards long.

Not far away, Eva sat on the crest of a dune, a little Mexican girl humming an old Spanish song. All three of them seemed to be in their own bodies today. So Alex had to be in the helicopter.

Naomi scurried to his side. Facing the east she folded her forelegs over her thorax and basked in the morning suns.

Eva stood up, walked over to Alex’s landing struts, and climbed the aluminum ladder to his cockpit. Seated inside the bubble she took her pack of cigarettes from the instrument panel and pushed in the handle of the lighter. A minute later, dragging on her first smoke of the day, she put on her headset. The helicopter spoke into her ears.

“How was your night?” it asked.

She shrugged.

“I dreamed of bugs,” it went on. “Better than dreaming about factories. Did you dream?”

“I dreamed of an old lover.”

“A man or a woman?”

“Sort of.”

“Well, I hope you enjoyed yourself.”

Eva studied the copter’s instruments. “Are you all right?”

“Why do you ask?”

“You’re not being a pain in the ass for some reason.”

“That is odd.”

Eva tapped the ash from her cigarette. “I wonder whether Naomi had a dream.”

“Forget Naomi! Let’s ditch her! Fly away with me, my luscious flower. Let me shower you with pleasures undreamed of.”

“Be serious.”

“Naomi,” Alex grumbled. “What do you see in her? She’s ugly. How long must you torture me this way? When will you quench the passion that burns in my soul? Dear heart! How many centuries must I wait for your kiss?”

“You? You haven’t got the equipment.”

“You’re so wrong, Eva. I have a heart of gold. But you have no heart at all. You lock me out.”

“I don’t love you, Alex. I love Naomi.”

“Then kill me. Fly me into a mountain. Let me be your slave. Do anything to me. But don’t keep locking me out.”

“I don’t want a slave. I don’t like slaves.”

The voice in the headphones just sighed. Alex rotated on his landing gear, turning his prow optics toward Naomi. “Eva doesn’t love me,” his bullhorn announced mournfully.

“And you’ll never forgive her for it,” observed the ant.

“I forgive her.”

Eva stretched her legs and yawned. “You do? Really?”

“Sure,” said Alex. “I can forgive poor judgment.”

Eva strapped herself into her seat. The copter spun its rotors and took to the air. The ant kept pace with them below, racing in spurts across the dunes.

Eventually they would wear down the day and reach another night. Eventually they would cross this desolate region and be gone. There was nothing to keep them here.

In Dr. Mazer’s office, a sad old man sat on the wooden floor of an empty room. None of the other angels understood what he was going through. And he was far too ashamed of himself to explain it to them.

Apart from his glasses Mazer was naked. He sat beside a pile of framed etchings, looking at one after another. The etchings took their themes from Greek mythology. Poseidon and Athena. Icarus in flight. The bull dancer of Crete. Mazer began to sob.

He took off his glasses and wept into his hands. It wasn’t easy to be a healer of souls. The work hadn’t come naturally to him. But he’d applied himself to it, and he’d gotten to be good at it. He’d been nearly as good as Weaver.

But then he’d fallen into an old habit of his. He’d built a maze that was impossible to escape. And he’d put some people inside it. Just to see what they’d do. Just like in the old days, when he and the others had been humans.

Now it was all going wrong, just like before. And soon his maze would catch fire and start to burn. Well, mistakes happened. No one was infallible.

On the floor around Mazer, a puddle of urine began to spread.

The copter flew low across the desert. The day progressed. One of the suns sank into a cloud mass to the west. Dunes gave way to prairie, and prairie to forest. They came upon the outskirts of a city. Naomi recognized it. It was Victoria, British Columbia.

She dashed through the streets, clambering over cars and buses. She found her favorite park, the one with the duck pond. The copter landed on a neat green lawn among some maple trees. Eva climbed down from the cockpit. The air smelled of wet leaves. Naomi led the other two toward the pond. Alex extended his wheels and taxied.

Naomi tapped the ground with her antennae as she walked. For the millionth time she explained their situation—the illness that ran in their family, where they were, and why they hadn’t been able to get free.

“You mean we’re still in that microchip?” said Alex. “On that space ship?”

“No no no,” Naomi answered. “After we died, Dr. Mazer copied us into his music box.”

“Are there other copies of us?” he asked in horror.

“Not anymore. There were some in the Akashic Record, but Mazer destroyed them. He’s very thorough. We’re pure information now. That’s how we can move around in here.”

“So this Mazer,” said Eva. “He’s an angel?” Naomi put her head between her forelegs and squealed in frustration. Eva covered her ears.

“I tell you this stuff over and over again! Can’t you listen? All you ever do is sleep and dream and remember things.”

“Naomi,” said Eva soothingly. “Please. Be patient with us. We’re older than you, and we get confused. Start again.”

“There’s this little bald guy with glasses. His name is Dr. Mazer.”

Eva rested her chin on her thumb and pondered. “He thinks he’s a mental therapist?”

“You’ve seen him!” cried Naomi. “And do you remember the glass tunnel? We came to a window at the end of it.”

“And there was an office with a desk,” continued Alex. “He looked like a giant.”

“Because we were inside a picture tube,” explained Naomi.

Maple seeds sailed down from the branches of the trees.

And all the while the music box was running down.

It was winter in Boston, 1931, and the streets were covered with snow. Ensign Wing had a ten a.m. appointment at a Greek restaurant. Spiker had agreed to meet him.

Wing walked into the café at ten sharp and hung up his overcoat. He’d adjusted his naval uniform to accord with the time period. He went to the rear of the place and sat down across a table from Spiker.

Spiker wore a gray fedora and a pinstripe suit. She was tall for an oriental and extremely thin. Her steel gray hair was cropped close to her skull. Anyone but Wing would’ve taken her for a man.

“You’re putting out a contract?” she said.

“In a sense,” said Wing. “Weaver needs to have Mazer put under restraint.”

Spiker sipped her cup of tea. “Go on.”

“He has patients that will have to be removed, but Weaver will handle that herself. Mazer is. . . no longer competent.”

Spiker smiled ironically. “He must be somewhat competent, since you’re calling me in. Is he still at his castle?”

“He never leaves.”

“How many patients are there?”

“Three.”

“Where does he keep them?”

“In a music box in his desk. But Weaver should have them out before you arrive.”

Spiker finished her tea. “Leave it to me,” she said. “I’ll take him down. But I make no guarantees about the captives.”

Wing stood up. He felt numb. Later on he’d feel miserable and angry.

“My father is a dangerous man,” he said. Half a warning, half a threat.

“Not as dangerous as me,” answered Spiker.

PART FOUR

THE CURE

CHAPTER FOURTEEN

THE SINGER

But when the sun was low in the West,

The Dong arose and said—

“What little sense I once possessed

Has quite gone out of my head!”

—Edward Lear

I walked in darkness. I walked a straight line, in darkness, unobstructed. My feet were bare. There was concrete beneath them. I didn’t know where I was.

A bare light bulb came into sight. It was screwed into the ceiling of a tunnel. The walls of the tunnel were lined with conduits and cables. There were power meters under hoods of dusty glass and valve handles in jackets of red rubber. The air was hot and dry and smelled of machine oil. My dress was torn and sooty. My name was Eva. Who else would I be?

I passed beneath the bulb. My shadow walked in front of me. The tunnel went on and on. I was under a city. I could sense the city happening overhead. I needed to find my way back to street level. A person could starve in those tunnels. If I was going to die, I wanted to die under a sky, a clear blue sky. And get on to the next thing. There were skies like that once.

I kept looking for a way back to the surface. The tunnels were a maze I was threading. To escape from a maze, you must be patient. You must keep your wits. You must notice and remember.

I’d forgotten why I went down there. Perhaps the city had suffered a gas attack. Perhaps I was the only survivor of a plague. Perhaps I just went looking for a place to sleep and got lost. But a person couldn’t live down there. Not for weeks or months.

Certainly not for years. Not for decades. Impossible to believe. Not for centuries. Not unless she was dead to the world and dreaming. Or dreaming that she was dead. Or unless she was dead.

I sat with my back against a wall. A ridge kept jabbing my spine. I turned around and looked at it. It was the upper edge of a low steel door. The door had a handle, so just out of curiosity I turned the latch and opened it.

Behind the door was a narrow cupboard. At the bottom of the cupboard was a nest of rags. On top of the nest, settled into the rags up to her chin, was a human head in a tangle of long white hair. The hair was crawling with roaches, which scrambled out of sight as yellow light invaded the cupboard.

The head blinked, waking up. She opened her eyes and looked at me.
