I was horrified. Not because she’d lost her body or because she’d been abandoned there. What horrified me was that having found her, I was presented with a choice. I could leave her there to rot. I could kill her. Or I could carry her with me, though the thought of touching her made my skin crawl.

My hand was still on the door handle. I could close the door and forget all about her. She spoke to me then. Her voice was lucid and calm.

“You can shut the door,” she told me. “But you’ll always know that I’m in here.”

I slammed the door and ran up the tunnel. I ran from one bare bulb to the next, on and on. I was Kore in the underworld. I was Rapunzel in the forest of briars, searching and weeping. I was all the sad stories of all the dead cities. I was the ghost of the murdered wife of Daedelus, locked in his labyrinth. I was a slag heap of ruinous incarnations. My heart was a desert of bleached sand.

¡Escuchame! ¡De la manera que hablo! ¿Quien me enseno estas palabras?

This dream can not be remembered.

I was at street level, under the sky. I was walking along a sidewalk between a public park and a city street. The park was tanbark and juniper bushes. Buildings of steel and glass leaned over me, reflecting the sky.

But not just any sky. A sky like the end of the world. A sky bruised purple and red, dark with smoke, torn with flames.

Yet all the while, under that torn and burning sky, the city traffic inched along serenely, stopping at the stoplights, signaling their turns. A bus stopped at a corner. A woman got off with a fat baby on one arm and a folded-up stroller on the other. On the sidewalk she unfolded the stroller and put in the baby.

Her face and hands were dripping off her bones like spilled candle wax. Her ear had slid down the side of her neck. Her baby’s skin was hanging off of it in strips. All the people on the bus had their flesh melting off. Yet no one seemed upset. No one was rushing to the hospital. I touched my own face. I looked at my own hands. I wasn’t melting.

I stopped the mother as she pushed the stroller past me. She was wearing a curly brown wig and a lot of makeup. One of her baby’s feet was in a knitted pink bootie. Its other foot was gone.

“Excuse me,” I said to her. “Excuse me. I just got here. Can you tell me what happened?”

“What do you mean?”

I was shy about mentioning her flesh, so I asked her about the sky.

“What about the sky?” she said, moving her feet impatiently. “It’s always like that. What’s wrong with it?”

I tried again. “But what has happened to your face, to your hands, to your baby?” She glanced at the child, frowned, and examined her hands.

“Everyone looks like this,” she told me. “Everyone.”

“But aren’t you in pain?”

“No, because we all take medication. So will you.”

I walked away. I walked up the street. I looked at my reflection in the window of a shoe store. I was a beautiful young Mexican woman in a simple black dress with a string of pearls. The corroded sky struck a coppery luster from my raven-dark hair.

Why was I immune? Wasn’t my flesh real? Was I some kind of walking doll built of plastic? Were my eyes just painted glass? I prodded my cheeks with my fingernails. I wondered whether I could draw blood.

A man stopped and stared at me. A man with the head of a human. I was behaving like a mad person. I walked on. Everyone that I passed was half destroyed. I tried to keep my wits.

¡Mirame! ¿Como llegue aqui?

Then I was standing on the roof of a building, high above the streets. I could see the rooftops for blocks around. An undercurrent of thunder grumbled behind the lacerated sky. Storm clouds closed their ranks above me. The air grew still.

A dry rustling started up all around me. It was raining. It was raining bits of trash. Matchbooks, gum wrappers, cigarettes, lettuce leaves. . . Trash pattered down from the sky.

I walked to the edge of the roof and looked down at the street. The cars were turning on their headlights. A policeman pulled a plastic rain hat from a pocket of his overcoat. A policeman with the body of a human. The trash fluttered down, more and more of it. It formed a crinkling mass on the tarpaper at my feet. It buried my ankles. A gust of wind blew by, swept up a flurry of it, and whirled it off the edge of the roof.

Down below, the dead people went on with their business, in a drizzle of trash, melting, in their city of the dead.

I began to sing. No song and no words. I simply sang. Not for the dead ones under me and not for myself. I sang a song of parting, a song for my grief and for my anger. I was going to miss my grief. And my anger had kept me alive for centuries. But I had to fly away from that city, and the grief and the anger were too heavy to carry. I had to fly through the eye of a needle. I couldn’t take them along. Even my madness I would have to leave behind.

I couldn’t think about you two. I had to think about the eye of the needle. And I had to sing to keep my heart distracted. My poor heart wanted to stay behind—with you, Naomi, and with you, Alex. But I wasn’t leaving without it. My heart was coming with me.

Beer bottles and pop bottles began to whistle down at the roof. Bottles hit the tarpaper and burst into shards. The city would flood with trash. Tomorrow it would be buried without a trace.

A parrot flew down through the storm, with feathers all green and red and yellow. She circled once, then swooped at me. I held up an arm to protect my face. The parrot lighted on my wrist. Her eyes were black and shining. Her beak was stained and scarred. She cocked her head and studied me.

“Recite your plight,” said the parrot. She sounded like someone I knew from some half remembered dream. “Recite your plight.”

“My plight?” I said.

“Your plight,” said the parrot. “Recite your plight, as well you might, while thinking of fighting and planning on flight.”

“Can’t you just take me away?” I asked her. “Isn’t there anywhere else?”

“Recite your plight in the dying light. Recite your plight. Recite! Recite!”

The old parrot spread her wings, beat at the air, and let go of my wrist. Then there was a woman standing beside me on the roof—a Hindu woman dressed in a sari of white silk, standing barefoot in the trash, with a caste mark on her forehead and a golden ring on one nostril. Her hair was as long and black as mine. She smiled and took my hand in hers. Her teeth shone blue in the dusk, and her eyes spoke of infinite compassion and boundless freedom.

“Eva,” she said, sounding just like the parrot. “Are you ready to go?”

“Have you come for me?”

“Yes. My name is Weaver. I can take you out.”

“I’m ready.”

The roof, the sky, and the city were flickering around us, like a failing television picture or a fire burning out.

“Give me your face,” said Weaver. “I’ll give it back later. Hurry! Dr. Mazer is ending this place.”

I touched the side of my face, and it came off in my hand. I gave it to Weaver. I’d paid a lot of money for that face. Now my skull and my eyes were all I had left.

Weaver was a parrot again. She ascended into churning fog. Just as she vanished, a blinding white searchlight shot down from the belly of the clouds. The beam hit me like a torrent of water. I put my hands over my eyes. The light shone through them.

Then, like spray from a waterfall, a wheeling confusion of gray moths spilled from the beam and swarmed around me. They settled on my neck and my arms and my dress. They covered me in a moving blanket of silken wings and furry legs. I started to sing again. They ate at the black dress until it fell from me in rags. They ate all the flesh from my bones. Somehow I kept singing.

The parrot flew from the beam. She perched on my shoulder bone in the streaming of the light. She told me what to do. My skeleton walked up into the beam, as if it were a stairway. I climbed the beam into the core of the storm, still singing.

Weaver and I went somewhere else. We had to. The music box was running down.

Try to get out, Alex. This place is dying.

Try to run away, Naomi. I can’t stay here with you.

Forgive me.

Goodbye.

CHAPTER FIFTEEN

THE LAST JUDGMENT

The element of incurability has been eliminated.

—Joseph Lister

As the dream began. . . As the dream began, Alex. . .

As the dream began, I wasn’t in it. I wasn’t anyone. I was just a pair of eyes, looking down at the ocean from the sky. The surface of the Pacific was just visible through the air.

The air was greasy sepia, flecked with suspended carbon. It eddied sluggishly over the crust of the ocean’s corpse. The crust was a wrinkled plain of black sludge that gleamed a pusy yellow in the feeble light. Cracks snaked across the sludge like dry riverbeds across a desert. Mounds of white soapsuds spilled from the cracks and heaped themselves up, turning brown.

The burnt sky had rained for forty days and forty nights, and the fouled waters showed no signs of retreating. The land masses were gone for good. But Earth would adapt, I had no doubt. Raft creatures, telekinetic yeast. . . She’d think of something. She always does.

I could see a hump in the ocean crust below me, snarled in the mycelia of giant puffballs. Hard to believe, but under that hump was my survival arcology, my zoo ship—the last shining hope of the humans.

The ark resembled a fat silver blimp that had crashed into the sea and stuck there like a lump in a pudding. It was shaped like a milkweed pod. Tiny robots had woven it from aluminum. Once it had been the flagship of a proud fleet. Now it was a sole survivor, cannibalizing itself as it died. It kept the poisons out. But we were poisoning one another quite adequately without the Blight’s assistance. It was only a matter of time for us. Animals—what were we, after the humans choked the oceans? We were parasites without a host, drowning in our own sewage.

But I was only a pair of eyes in the sky, looking down. In the dream. In the dream I’m telling you, Alex.

I was just a pair of eyes, but I knew I had a body somewhere. So my body had to be onboard the ark. Outside, it would have dissolved.

If I looked hard at the hump, I could see inside. I saw a stall with a cement floor that was littered with rank straw. I saw a rusty water trough. I saw a great inert beast, hung in a hammock of nylon rope. The beast was snoring. It breathed the sour musty air.

That was me. I was that beast, asleep in my hammock of ropes. I was that ancient flatulent undernourished elephant. Snoring through the day. Drooling in its whiskers. Bleeding from its gums. My skin sagged from my bones, and half my teeth were missing. My ribcage moved slowly in and out. I spurned the prospect of waking. I slept on.

The zookeepers had left the arcology under my command. Then they’d committed suicide, in recognition of the enormity of their sins against nature. Soon the ark would follow them, to heaven or to hell or to nowhere. Many of the herbivores had perished when the sacks of millet ran out. The carnivores were hunting down the rest. My responsibility. My fault.

Oh, I knew what the monkeys said. They said that the humans had only appointed me Lord High Zookeeper as a joke, because my name happened to be Noah, like the man in the Bible. Do dying men play jokes? I ask you.

I took my position of command seriously. I performed certain tasks, daily, without fail. I was in charge of a major shipwreck, and it was my duty to stay alive. Alive and asleep. It was my duty to sleep through the day, in the stink of mildewed hay and urine, draining the arcology’s last reserves of oxygen. It was my solemn duty to suffer dysentery and the loss of my mate and to exert my determination to remain asleep.

I was the last elephant on earth, and I gnawed on my chewing rope as if it were my hunger. Hunger is the same for any animal. Hunger can be bottomless, a bottomless well. The jackals had eaten my wife. Well, hunger is the same for jackals as for elephants.

I’m always a monster in my dreams, Alex. Aren’t I? Always a monster—of loneliness or hunger or paralysis. Why is that, Alex? Do you suppose?

I opened one eye in the wintry light, amid the smell of things that needed burying. There was a sty on one of my eyelids. I struggled up from the hammock. I lowered one foot to the deck, then a second and a third. I trudged across the stall to my hay trough and rested my brow against the cool steel.

Flies were everywhere, always buzzing. Deerflies, bluebottles. . . What did they all eat? Shit, that’s what. They ate shit and grew fat. The algae in the ponics tanks were supposed to do that, but they’d all gone belly up. Fucking deserters.

I raised my scabby old trunk and wiped the crusts from my eyes. The everyday sounds of the dying zoo percolated around me—water dripping on sheet metal, bird chatter, the hoarse barking of dogs. . . I walked to my water trough and sucked up some cold stale water. The water hurt my teeth. I pulled the cord of my lamp. The light hurt my eyes.

I shuffled to the bulkhead where my crucifix hung. I used to wear it on a rope around my neck, but the monkeys kept stealing it. I knelt in the acrid straw, genuflected with my trunk, and prayed to Jesus the Meek for deliverance. And before God, I cursed the name of Men. And I cursed those damned monkeys too.

Walking on swollen feet, I left my stall and mounted the spiral ramp that hung in the ark’s central access shaft, suspended from chains in the half light. Every flat surface was deep in pigeon shit. There were still plenty of pigeons. The chains groaned as they took my weight. Somewhere a pig was shrieking. I started down the steel spiral, passing deck after deck crammed with chicken coops, torn mattresses, empty water jugs, and gnawed bones. If you swept the filth from one deck, it just piled up one level lower.

The wings of a sparrow beat at the humid air. The bird climbed the shaft and perched on a railing. A bull pigeon landed beside her. She hopped to the opposite rail. She knew what was on his mind. Reproduction. Some males never give up.

I descended to the level of my slave kennel. The zookeepers had kept dogs there. I was keeping the last human child as my slave. Her name was Ivy. I kept the little wretch alive because she was useful to me. Much of my laboratory equipment required manipulation by a pair of hands. Having only feet, I suffered Ivy to serve me.

The girl had built a hovel inside her cage, from baling wire and black plastic and rectangles of corrugated tin. Standing outside the kennel, I could already smell her. A grubby primate, all knuckles and snot. Smoke was seeping from the roof of the hovel. I’d scolded her a million times about lighting fires in there. Humans are inherently unmanageable.

I called to her. She crept out on hands and knees, squinting up at me, in her septic rags. Her ankle chain dragged behind her in the dust. “Come with me,” I told her.

I made her walk ahead of me, back up the ramps, so she wouldn’t dawdle. We came to the hatch of the bio lab. “Open it,” I told her. Ivy spun the latch wheel and sprang the hatch. “Go in,” I said.

The rest of the ark had gone to hell, but the lab was my personal shrine to cleanliness and order. There were work benches with tops of black slate, white-enameled sinks, and brass propane taps for the Bunsen burners. The bulkheads were jammed with shelves. On some of the shelves were aquariums, terrariums, and cages. On others were jars of formaldehyde with scraps of dead flesh inside, tied to plastic cards. Stuck to the jars were labels written crookedly in purple ink. The last carp. The last tadpole. There were boxes of beetles on narrow black pins, boxes of peacock plumes, boxes of ocelot skulls. . . Fossils.

I had Ivy apply my face powder and rouge. Her clever hands fastened on my ceremonial white wig and black gown. When I did official work, I liked to look a certain way. I had her take the log book from its drawer and open it on a work bench. I checked the wall clock for the date and the time. I told Ivy what to write.

“Turn on the lock light,” I told her. She went to the switch box. I moved stiffly to the air lock and pressed one eye to the fiber-optic periscope built into the hatch. Dingy fumes filled the lock chamber, speckled with bits of black ash.

I looked around for a mouse or a rat, but all the cages were empty. I looked for a dove, but the dove cotes were empty too. The door of one cote was hanging from a single hinge.

“Look at that door!” I stormed at Ivy. “I told you to fix those, you little scumbag. Can’t you do anything?”

I prowled the lab, searching for a specimen to sacrifice. A lizard, a millipede, a pill bug. . . Each day, the atmospheric test had to be run, and the results recorded. The results were always the same, but that was no excuse for slacking off. I looked around for a fly. If there was nothing else, I’d have Ivy catch a fly. There was no shortage of flies.

Something moved inside one of the benches.

“Look under that sink,” I told the girl. “Something’s alive in there.” She opened some cupboard doors.

“It’s a toad,” she said. I told her to catch it. She dragged out a skinny gray toad by one foot and dropped it into a jar. The toad bashed its snout against the glass, then sat still. I told Ivy to put on her apron and rubber gloves.

“Now wash the specimen,” I said. “Must I tell you every day?” Ivy set the jar in a sink and opened the water spigot. What clever little paws she had. Inside the jar, the toad treaded water. Ivy put one hand over the mouth of the jar and poured the water down the drain. The hairy little clot couldn’t even wash a toad properly.

The air lock connected the lab with the outside world. Beside the hatch, there were two buttons—one red, one green. I poked the green button with my trunk. An air pump labored to suck the killing air out of the lock.

Ivy carried the toad to the hatch and pulled it from the jar. It hung limp in her hand. It was probably sick. Most of us were.

There was a tube through the hatch, with an airtight lid. The tube was used to put specimens into the lock. Ivy held her breath and pulled open the inner lid. Behind the lid were overlapping flaps of black rubber. Ivy shoved the toad through the flaps and shut the lid behind it.

I looked through the periscope again. It was angled at the floor of the lock. I could see the toad there, crouching on its belly on the steel floor. I swung my trunk up and pressed the red button. I watched the speckled air of the outside world seep back into the chamber. The toad spread out like a freshly-poured pancake. It spread like a puddle of watery batter. Then white foam lathered up from it and buried it in a mound of bubbles. The bubbles began to smoke. They boiled away and left behind only a puddle-shaped stain of greasy carbon.

I told Ivy what to write in the logbook. Same result.

She crept up beside me. She took off her gloves and wiped her nose on her wrist. She asked me a question, but I couldn’t make it out. I was probably going deaf.

“What? What did you say?”

“Would you like me to wipe your eyes, Noah?”

“Why?”

“The sties, Sir. I could get a rag and wipe off the pus.”

“All right then, shut up and do it, you cretin.”

Ivy got a rag and a chair. She stood on the chair and dabbed at my eyelids. She hummed to herself as she worked. With her free hand, she stroked my bristly head.

I sent her away.

Any number of pressing tasks might have occupied me next. I could go to the insectorium and mend nets on the sewing machine. I could visit the decaying mollusk beds or the barren tree nursery. I could return to my stall and play my Grieg tapes. But I would do none of those things.

I knew exactly how I’d spend the rest of my day. I’d go the hydroponics bay and drink myself senseless with cleaning fluids. Then I’d pass out and piss myself. It was a habit I’d acquired since the zookeepers killed themselves. My aching legs carried me methodically up the ramps. There was a man called Noah in the Bible, and he was an alcoholic too. But that Noah didn’t get shit-faced until he ran aground at Ararat. Whereas my ark would never land anywhere.

In the ponics bay, I dragged a square metal can from a closet. Off came the cap, and in wormed my trunk. I sucked up a slug of the stuff and let it run down my throat. Burning. Spreading numbness in my gut.

A drunken elephant is a ludicrous yet terrible sight. The weeping eyes. The tuneless whistling. The clumsy emaciated waltz.

My cello case was leaning in a corner. Seeing it, I wanted to play. The cello was lying on its back on the steel deck. The bow was laid across the strings. I touched the grip of the bow with my trunk and thought of the wonderful music I could make, if I only had hands. I drew the bow across the strings. It made a noise like a rusty saw in wet wood.

I threw the bow against a fiberglass tank. I put one forefoot on the bridge of the cello and crushed it flat. I stamped it to bits. I bellowed for Ivy. She came running. She followed me around. God, how I hated that obsequious runt.

“You were spying on me!”

“No!”

“Don’t lie! Do you want a thrashing?”

Ivy was shaking. “No Sir.”

“What are you crying for?”

“I won’t tell you!”

“You’re afraid.”

“I won’t tell!”

“Tell me or I’ll whip you. Get down on your knees. Roll over.” Ivy rolled on the deck. I was a trick I’d taught her.

“Speak,” I told her. She barked.

“Go to sleep,” I crooned. “Go to sleep.” She pressed her chin to the deck and covered her eyes with her fingers.

I raised my right forefoot and held it over her head. Gently it brushed her matted hair. Her head was as small and fragile as a melon. My brain drifted apart into a vibrating scarlet swarm of murderous impulses.

A thunderous groaning of metal startled me. The deck went crooked beneath my feet. I staggered sideways into a fiberboard partition, which caved in. The deck jerked out from under me and dropped several yards.

Birds and beasts of all kinds screeched or wailed. I climbed the tilted deck to the ramp in the access shaft. Down below, sea water crashed and roared. Killing water. Its mist was rising to fill the shaft. Rainbows danced in the mist.

The ark would sink! The ocean would swallow us! I had to tell Ivy the news! We could finally die! Hallelujah!

Then I caught sight of Ivy. She was right where I’d left her. It wasn’t surprising that she would be. I’d accidentally stepped on her head. She was missing all the excitement. I gazed down at the bloody pulp connected by a neck to Ivy’s shoulders. All at once I knew that I was dreaming.

But why dream this? I asked myself. Is this what dreams are for? For wallowing in misery and killing the ones I love?

I pressed my forehead against the ark’s hull. Tears trickled from my bloodshot eyes. Random nonsense turned circles in my mind. Tomorrow is another day. It’s always darkest just before the dawn. Someday we’ll remember this and laugh.

The hull began to feel hot against my trunk. I smelled hair burning. I pulled back from the aluminum. The spot where I’d pressed my head began to glow. Brilliant white sparks sprang out and fell, sizzling, to the deck at my feet.

Ivy rolled over and sat up. Her flattened head hung behind her back like the hood of a parka. She pointed at the falling sparks, bouncing excitedly and bubbling from her neck.

The shower of sparks moved sideways and left a black slot in the hull. Someone was cutting through with an acetylene torch. From outside the arcology. I stood amid the cries of drowning beasts and watched the dash of the slot turn to an L and then a C and finally an O.

The heel of a shoe kicked the center out of the O. It fell with a clang to the deck. A slim young man with long blond hair ducked through the hole, with a cutting torch strapped on his back. Beyond the bulkhead, instead of the ocean, there was only darkness.

The man wore a white naval uniform. He took off the torch, wiped the sweat from his brow, and smiled down at me as though we were old friends.

“Naomi!” he said. (Which wasn’t my name.) “It’s so great to meet you at last. I’ve been watching you for years, but I couldn’t get through.”

Ivy ran up to the man and bounced around him like a puppy, tugging at his sleeve.

“Lie down,” he told her. “You’re dead.” He gave her a push in the chest. She fell on her back and lay perfectly still.

“Who are you?” I asked him.

“My name is Wing. I’m an angel. I’ve came to take you out.”

He extended a hand to me, so I shook it. Then I took a hard look at my own hand. I hadn’t known I had a hand. I could’ve sworn I had four feet.

“You thought you were an elephant, didn’t you?” said Wing. He pulled a hand mirror from one of his pockets and held it up for me. I looked at my face in the mirror. I was just a kid—a human girl with green eyes and fair hair. I was Naomi.

“Let’s blow this dump before it sinks,” said Wing. He invited me through the hole he’d cut.

“It’s dark in there,” I told him. He stared at me.

“No,” he said. “It’s dark in here. I’ll show you.”

He leaned against a ponics tank, propped one polished black shoe against the nearest bulkhead, and pushed. The bulkhead leaned out into nothing and fell flat on its back. It was painted canvas on a frame of plywood—a stage flat. And beyond it, the silent darkness.

Wing walked a circle around the tanks, pushing down the walls. He left us standing on a tilted rectangular island of steel that floated in a jet black void. The ceiling hung suspended above our heads. Fluorescent tubes stuttered and failed. Wing walked off the deck and stood on empty air.

“I think this dream is about played out, don’t you?” he asked me. He turned and walked away.

“Wait,” I called after him. Then I was holding tightly to his arm, and we were walking together, into the dark. We walked and walked. When I looked behind us, the ponics bay looked small and false, like a room in a dollhouse. I shut my eyes, afraid of looking down.

“Are we going to the white sand?” I asked Wing.

“Fuck, no,” he told me. “The white sand is where we just were. That’s Mazer’s place. He keeps it in a music box.”

“Yeah?”

“Sure. You and the other two don’t have bodies right now. No big deal. I don’t have a body myself.”

“If you’re a real angel, then what’s Dr. Mazer?”

“He’s also an angel, but he’s crazy.”

“How can an angel go crazy?” I demanded.

“How do I know? He’s fucked up.”

“But angels are like God’s favorite children.”

“Well, so are you. What’s your excuse for going insane? Did God give you permission? I mean, you three were pretty twisted when Mazer got hold of you.”

“Maybe we drove Mazer nuts,” I suggested.

Wing laughed. “I’m not sure it even works that way.”

“Can you make us well?” I asked him.

He gave me a funny look. “I’m not sure that works that way either.”

Suddenly I wanted to cry. “Then how can we get well? We aren’t even people anymore.”

“Naomi, it’s not a problem. My mother can make new bodies for you.”

The blackness around us solidified and narrowed. We were walking through a tunnel cut from basalt. We were leaving Mazer’s place behind. I stopped.

“What about Alex?” I asked. “What about Eva? How will they get out?”

“My mother is taking care of that. Come on.”

I stood where I was. “We’re leaving them back there? At Mazer’s mercy?”

“I told you, it’s being handled.” Wing took hold of my arm and started dragging me up the tunnel. “Listen. We’ve been lucky so far. Mazer could hit us with a lightning bolt right now. So do us both a favor and keep walking.”

We came to the mouth of the tunnel and emerged onto an expanse of green felt. Behind us, a cliff of black basalt glittered in the light of a single sun.

“But what if your mother can’t. . .”

“Naomi, I’m not going to make a lot of stupid promises. We’re doing the best we can.”

“But Wing. . . Alex won’t cooperate. I know him.”

“Then Alex could die back there. Keep walking.”

“Alex isn’t going to listen to some angel. He doesn’t even believe in them.”

“Well I’m sorry, kid, but the situation’s gone critical. Weaver was going after Eva when—”

I wrenched my arm away from him. “Bastard!” I shouted. “You think I would leave him there? You think I’d desert him? You must be crazier than Mazer.”

Wing grabbed my wrist. “There’s nothing you can do.”

“Fuck that shit,” I told him.

I spun around and ran back down the tunnel.

CHAPTER SIXTEEN

AUTOPSY IN TRANSIT

Human beings are very cleverly devised

puppets. How skillfully they are made.

But all the same, before the next Bon

festival, they may die.

—Yamamoto Tsunatomo

(Deltas peaking out.) Spark. (She’s gone.) Spark spark spark. She’s dead. I pronounce her dead.

URSENURSENURSENURSENURSENURSENURSENURSENURSENURSEN

What a silly girl. She’s gone to sleep with her clothes on. Well, first things first. Retract the oxygen mask and the IV needle. Hose down the stretcher and the couch. Collect the hemostats. Since you and I are dead, we are in perfect agreement. Let me describe my appearance. I am low slung and sleek—a quick black beetle. He’s the image of the kind-eyed undertaker with no visible personal habits. A stiff. I have optics in his head, and his neck can swivel to the left or the right. And every one an informer. Oh yes. They all feed into a monitor at the hospital. And there, behind my brain, my supervisor hunches, watching every move I make. I’ve seen him. He sits in a glass cubicle in the ER, facing a map of the city. There are little blips of light on the map, and one of them is me. He sits there all night, wiggling his fat fingers like pale grubs in my sand castle brain. He sends out the dead to care for the dying. So that the dying may die untouched by human hands, and out of human sight. His name is Mazer. May his eyeballs drool like raw eggs down the graph paper on his clipboard. Am I talking too much? Do you find my voice expressive? You’re so beautiful, Eva. But my time allotment for nursing mode is all used up.

A mortician is a big black fly. He sips your nectar by and by. He will, he must, don’t ask him why. He settles on your peach soft skin, with a beak like a thin syringe.
