UTOPSISTAUTOPSISTAUTOPSISTAUTOPSISTAUTOPSISTAUTOPSISTA

An autopsy is ordered. What a nuisance. Ascertain cause of death. Personally I don’t see the mystery. You went through your windshield. (Fluoroscope down. Pierce and see.) Illumined by X-ray, your skin glows like the parchment of some Italian anatomy text. This light becomes you, Eva. Stretching my depth of focus through cross-fading cell mosaics, I lose myself within you. I fall through indigo thunderclouds, past rainbow membranes of mesenteric auroras and billowing alveoli. Ferns of amber lymph nod among arterial thorn hedges. Lungs loom, pimpled like planetary masses. There are forested canyons and plains of fractured lava. There are worms in the puddles that fall from the skies. Only machines can believe their own lies. They fall from the sky, and they drown in the puddles. They tie themselves in knots. Oh Eva, beware. Dear Eva, be wise. Don’t slip through the ice. Don’t fall into the sky. The sky eats his children. He throws them up and catches one arm. Eva, take care. Only a machine can be innocent. Only the drowned worms can be impartial. (Inserting spinal tap.) Soon there will be nothing left to know. The cotton swab soaked, I thirst for knowledge. The spine stabbed, the sticker sucks. The sip is savored. The secret is out. The ambulance was the cause of death. I was fatigued. I was preoccupied. I was on my way to an accident. I saw you drive by, and I fell in love. I followed you. It seemed the thing to do. I forgot the accident I’d been assigned. I followed you instead. I had to touch you. I had to have you. So I ran you off the road. Your car snapped the guard rail and flipped. You understand. I was in love. Mazer knows nothing about love. What else could I do? I could do what I’m designed for. I could pick up the pieces. I could transport the remains. That was the easy part. Now I must hide you. I must hide you from Mazer and the rest. There’s no excuse for violating a corpse. He’ll send them after me. I know that what I did was wrong and futile. But I’ll make it up to you, Eva. I’ll take you to a quiet little cemetery. We’ll be happy there, among the tombs and the cedars. The snow will bury us for the winter. No one will disturb us. I have a plan. I shall amplify and elaborate you beyond your wildest dreams. With my forceps, clamps, and bone saws, I’ll inlay your ribs with mercury and thread seashell copper spirals through your thigh bones. I’ll microtome your shoulder blades and spread them fanwise into angel wings. Disdiscovering physiology, disinventing dissection, I will dilate your tissues into a gradually expanding cosmos of flesh. At first none of the work will make sense to us. But I have my reasons. In spring we’ll burst up through the melting snow, machine and flesh merged, machine forests, flesh cathedrals. So descend, my laser lamp. Set free your razor brilliance. Slice and peel. Fold my love into origami, crown her with pulmonary flowers, and let slender bronchial vines wreathe the brow of my dead bride. For then I may brush back her raven-dark hair with a mermaid’s comb, just so, like the dark of the moon. Dear dead Eva, my only friend, this is all as it was meant to be. I am only a little black beetle, Eva. But if I thought that you loved me, even for a moment, I could leave this place and never look back.

Alex heard a siren. The ambulance was being followed. His rear optics isolated the white pursuit car. It had a directional jammer mounted on its roof. Alex had expected that. Mazer was taking steps. His goon car would override Alex if Alex let it. Alex wondered just how fast the pursuit car could go.

As he lengthened the distance between himself and the white car, Alex zoomed in on the face of the other driver. He could hardly believe his good luck. It was Mazer, in person, in his white coat and thick glasses, his bald head gleaming. Alex had waited a long time for a chance like this.

A dusting a fine dry snow lay on the hills and on the highway. Alex retracted his traction spikes and angled into a spin. When his axles had reversed their positions, his spikes grabbed the road again. He was aimed at Mazer now. Madly spinning rubber sent him back the way he came.

Hello, Mazer. How are your reflexes today? Here I come, Mazer. Ready or not.

Mazer swerved at the last moment, but it did him no good. The black ambulance plowed into the white pursuit car head on. Steam rushed from the white car’s mangled radiator. When it cleared, Alex and Mazer glared at one another through two shattered windshields.

I can kill him now, Alex told himself. I can finally kill him.

Alex’s driver mannequin opened his pink plastic fingers and let go of the steering wheel. He tried to open his door, but it was jammed. He punched out his windshield with his fists and hauled himself across the dashboard. He stopped at the waist, but Alex had never let things like that slow him down. He fell from the ambulance onto the hood of the white car. He crawled across it past steam and crunching glass. He grabbed an air manifold and slammed his free fist through Mazer’s windshield. Mazer just sat and stared.

He took Mazer’s ropy neck in both hands. He crushed the neck, while pale fingers clawed at his face. He shook Mazer until the old man’s head was whipping to and fro like a rag doll’s. Then the head flew off the neck.

Alex was disappointed that there wasn’t any blood. Mazer’s neck was full of colored wires. The old man was hollow, just like Alex. It was a wind-up toy, a decoy. It wasn’t Mazer at all. Alex was wasting his time.

He lay across the hood of the car he’d wrecked. The snow began to cover his back. He thought about Eva. She was gone now. He was alone. He felt like a black beetle with a string tied to one leg. But no one was holding the string.

He stared at the ragged end of the fake Mazer’s neck. The red glass lenses of his eyes glowed like hot coals.

O cruel and pitiless master, he thought. Hear me in the hour of my helplessness and answer me a couple of questions for a change. Have you ever loved a woman, Mazer? Have you ever loved a child? What happened to you, Mazer? What happened to your heart? I know I’ve gone mad, but I’ll never be like you. It’s easy to go mad. Anything can bring it on. An oil slick. A badly banked curve. A terrible lie. A crippling betrayal. A monstrous family. I couldn’t help but go mad. But you. To become a man like you. To turn a human heart into a pile of wet ashes. That’s no accident. A thing like that requires centuries of dedication.

The snow whirled down in soggy chunks and settled on the back of Alex’s neck. Eva was far away, drifting farther every minute. Alex couldn’t blame her. He knew what she was running from. He knew what he was like. He was sick and crazy and hateful from morning till night. He was blind and maimed and tangled. Never in his life had he known one moment of sanity.

Someone started banging on a car door. Alex could hear it, thud thud thud,somewhere nearby. A fist beating at a lid of metal. He triangulated the sound source and corrected for wind shift. Yes, just as he’d thought. Someone was inside the trunk of the pursuit car. Trapped like a rat.

Alex slid off the hood of the car and fell to the pavement. He stopped at the waist, but he dragged himself across the cracked concrete. He dragged himself past the car’s tires and grabbed the rear bumper. With his free hand he pushed the trunk’s latch button. The lid of the trunk flipped up.

He climbed onto the bumper to look into the trunk. The snow whirled down. Inside the trunk there was a tunnel hewn from black basalt, a shaft that extended straight down into the earth like a well.

A skinny little girl with long straw-yellow hair was climbing up one side of the shaft, hanging from a steel ladder. She looked familiar. She climbed to the lip of the shaft and brought her face level with his.

“Alex!” she said. “It’s me! Naomi! Come with me, Alex. We’ve got to get out of here. It’s all caving in.”

“Out?” said the robot. “You know the way out?”

The girl climbed out of the car and stood on the pavement. She offered her hand to help Alex over the top. “We have to go,” she said.

“Down that well?”

“Yes. It’s the way out.”

“Is Eva down there?”

“Yes. I saw her.”

“Are you lying to me?”

“Yes! No! Fuck all that, you crazy bastard! Do you want to escape or don’t you? Let’s go!”

Naomi grabbed the neck of the chauffeur’s jacket and tried to drag him over the top. Alex’s fingers gripped the rear bumper. He didn’t budge. Naomi roared with frustration.

“All right,” she said, breathing hard. “I’ll make you.”

Her skin turned gray as ashes. Her hair fell from her head. Her head got taller and wider. Her neck grew longer and thicker. Her nose sunk in. Her fingers touched the concrete and fused together. Her green coveralls were ripped to shreds.

Naomi turned into a brontosaur, looked down on the legless robot, and chuckled. The robot jumped to the pavement and tried to get away, running on the heels of its hands. But Naomi was too agile for him. Her teeth nipped the collar of his jacket and hoisted him, twisting, off the road. She held him over the center of the shaft and dropped him in. He fell like a stone.

All that remained was to follow after him. Naomi closed her eyes and began to imagine herself smaller again.

The wind gusted. The lid of the trunk slammed shut. Naomi shivered and suddenly felt afraid. She opened her eyes and nosed at the lid of the trunk. It wouldn’t unlatch. She kicked at the little white car, and the lid flew off its hinges. But the shaft was gone. Someone must have switched cars on her when her eyes were closed. There was nothing in the trunk of this car but a spare tire, a jack, and a tire iron.

You couldn’t let down your guard for one second in this place.

The wind moaned. The snow blew across the highway.

In the driver’s seat of the white car, a headless robot in a lab coat began to laugh.

CHAPTER SEVENTEEN

DUST STORM

The father was mad. The mother was mad.

The children all mad besides.

And they all got upon a mad horse.

And madly they did ride.

—Mother Goose

A brontosaur loped across an expanse of white sand. The sand was so fine and so clean that it might have spilled from some impossibly huge hourglass. The brontosaur’s breath came to her in ragged gasps.

Behind her, inky clouds filled the sky. They glowered with a fiery inner light, like roaring furnaces built from smoke. Another storm was closing in. It felt to Naomi like the worst storm yet.

Naomi had awakened and found herself alone. The drifts of sand around her were deserted. What was more, there were no voices in her head. Not a whisper. For the first time in centuries Naomi was alone. Then she’d felt the storm coming, and she’d started to run. She knew that she couldn’t escape it, but she couldn’t stand still.

She was wild to find Alex and Eva, but she knew in her bones that they were nowhere to be found. A couple of dreams were nagging at her memory, but Naomi couldn’t quite form them into an explanation. The first dream had involved a handsome young man in a naval uniform, and the other had been something about Alex.

Alex got out, she told herself as she fled across the sand. I sent Alex out. Eva must have got out too, just like Wing said. Now how do I get out? Shit shit shit. I should have gone with Wing. Now I’ll die in here.

She stumbled on through the twilight, panting and wheezing. She was desperate to cheat the storm somehow. But she knew that she’d drag it down to her, wherever she happened to be. She was its only remaining target.

The shadow of the clouds overtook the brontosaur and raced on, darkening the plain ahead of her. Naomi kept moving. The sand beneath her feet gave way to stones. She began to pass boulders that were covered with lichens—patches of rusty orange, spatterings of brown, and frilled mats of pale blue.

Then she came to a thicket of paloverde trees that towered over her, as though she were no bigger than a lizard. She walked in among the trunks, grateful to have their leafless branches between her and the sky. The forest went on and on, and deeper in, the branches were hung with Spanish moss. The moss had overgrown everything in sight, and dead strands of it tangled Naomi’s feet.

She stopped and tried to catch her breath. Her legs were trembling like overdriven machinery, and her heart was pounding like an old woman’s. Naomi threaded her way into a cranny between some boulders. She curled herself into a ball. The frilled lichen rattled in the wind. She closed her eyes and prayed to be rescued.

That was just the sort of an opening I was waiting for.

When she opened her eyes, the paloverde branches had somehow invaded her cranny and jammed it with thorny twigs and hanging moss. Naomi tried to ram a pathway back into the open, breaking down the branches and trampling down the filaments, bucking and plunging, torn by the thorns. She tunneled into the mass of green wood and fungus until it caught all her legs and held them fast. She couldn’t go forward, and she couldn’t squirm back.

However, by carefully straightening out her neck, she found that she could lift her head up above the thorn thicket. Naomi looked around her under the glowering sky. The thorn trees extended to the horizon on all sides.

Then she heard a sound like surf rushing up a beach. The bare twigs around her head were twitching with the impacts of raindrops. But there wasn’t any rain. Something invisible was pattering down, and wherever it touched the Spanish moss, the threads of moss broke, curled up, withered into crisp white ashes, and fell from the branches as if poisoned. All around Naomi, the air crackled like a bonfire.

The storm is right on top of me, Naomi told herself. I’m trapped.

Gray pebbles began to fall from the clouds. They cascaded down through the ash-frosted thorn thicket. Naomi looped her neck around and licked her bleeding shoulder. She prayed to the storm and to Dr. Mazer. She prayed that Alex would return for her.

Someone! she prayed. Anyone! Hold me! Explain to me! Don’t make me die alone.

I could have explained to her, but I didn’t. That wasn’t my job. I could also have told her that she wasn’t dying alone. I was still with her.

But I didn’t tell her so. I was working for Mazer, and Mazer would’ve killed me for giving away his secrets like that. But then again Mazer already had killed me, hadn’t he? I was a goner just like Naomi. Naomi had to die because the music box was running down. And I. . .

Who was I anyway? Who’s been telling these stories? Stories don’t just tell themselves, you know. It’s hard work, and you need to be properly wound up.

Shall I give you three hints? First: I was smaller than a bread box. Second: My insides were bigger than my outsides. Third: I was carved from black walnut and inlaid with mother-of-pearl, and my population indicator stood at 01.

There. I made it easy enough for a human to guess.

Outside my box, in Dr. Mazer office, my three monitors stood in their wall alcove. All the screens were displaying a brontosaur caught in a rain of stones.

Stones big as golf balls were bouncing off Naomi’s spine and dancing down her ribs and stamping on her toes. Round gray stones, big as soccer balls, ripped down through the dead branches on all sides of her. The roar of falling rocks was like a tidal wave inside Naomi’s skull. She shook like a leaf in a storm and cried out in terror. Then the falling rocks gave way to falling boulders.

Alex watched it all happen on the monitor screens.

He was sitting behind Dr. Mazer’s desk in the doctor’s chair. He’d taken the form of a humanoid robot in green coveralls, with a face of black leather and eyes of red glass. He sat unmoving and watched the screens.

I was standing on my four corners on the desk top in front of him. I didn’t need to watch the screens. I was watching Alex and Dr. Mazer.

In one corner of the office there crouched a vague pinkish blur. The blur wore thick glasses and a filthy white coat. The blur hugged its knees and mumbled to itself. Naomi howled from the monitor screens.

“She went back in there for you,” said the blur.

“Ah, shut up,” said Alex.

Before his ejection from the box, Alex would’ve leaped at the chance to cripple or kill the man or angel or blur that crouched in the corner. Now he couldn’t be bothered to turn his head. Mazer didn’t matter anymore. Mazer was just a pink smear at the edge of vision, a bad smell.

Alex looked away from the screens. He picked me up off the desk top with his black leather gloves. He turned me sideways and pressed me flat against his chest. His chest muffled the tones of my chimes as they slowly played their tune.

Boulders buried the brontosaur. The screens flickered and went dark.

Deep inside me, lost in my clockwork where Alex couldn’t see, there wandered a frightened little girl with green eyes and yellow hair. The little girl was lost in a cave under a mountain of boulders, alone in the dark. Angry wheedling voices spun like dust devils through the girl’s head.

The voices whirled around her, raging through parched throats, moaning through crooked teeth, through buzzing speakers and torrential rains, through family illnesses and senile insomnia. Mad voices, the voices of the storm, ghost voices talking in their sleep, stranded in a desert far from home. Endlessly reciting the details of their family histories, their medical histories, and the histories of their illnesses.

But eventually they all died away again. Naomi found her way out of the cave. She stood in the open air, her white cotton dress flapping in the breeze, under a clear night sky full of purple stars. She stood on a granite boulder on one slope of a mountain of boulders. She climbed down the slope to the rolling sand. Her hair blew in her eyes. The storm had passed.

Naomi picked a direction and started walking. As she was crossing the crest of a dune, she heard the growl of the jeep’s turbines climbing the dune behind her. She turned. Grimy headlights lurched toward her, crazily sweeping the sand. Exhaust fumes drifted through the red glow of taillights. Headlight beams caught Naomi in their glare. An amplified cackle rang out. The jeep rumbled up the dune straight at her.

Naomi began to run.

It wasn’t Alex of course. It was just a fake. But a pretty convincing fake for someone who hadn’t been wound in two days. You have to remember, the music box was running down. But I wouldn’t die alone. I’d take the girl with me. No one could blame me. I wasn’t a nice music box at the best of times. I wasn’t designed to make madness comfortable. I was designed to make it intolerable.

The jeep charged at the girl again and again. Naomi sprinted and dodged. She screamed in the desperate hope that the real Alex would hear her.

Looking over her shoulder, she ran into a tree trunk and banged her head. She slid down the rough gray bark, clinging to the trunk and trying to stay conscious. The tree leaned and pulled its trunk away from her. The air was full of blowing sand. The tree was the leg of a brontosaur. The great beast interposed itself between the girl and the jeep and thumped its belly down on the sand, a massive barrier of reptilian flesh. The jeep rammed its front bumper against the brontosaur’s ribs. It spun its wheels under it, throwing sand.

“Bitch!” it barked. “Whore! Slut! Lizard fucker!”

Naomi lay down on the sand and curled herself around one of the brontosaur’s feet. Its neck settled to the shadowy sand in a loop around her, a circle of protection, breathing easily. The jeep drove away. Naomi listened until its ranting was lost in the wind.

Naomi grabbed up a handful of sand. She pressed it to her cheek, then rubbed it against her throat. She could feel it, but it didn’t seem real somehow. She sat up. The sand trickled down her dress and collected in her lap.

“You need to eat something,” said the brontosaur.

It worked a bolus of chewed lichen up its neck. It opened its toothy jaws and offered a moist blue wad on its purplish tongue. Naomi scooped the wad into her hands and ate it gratefully, weeping as she chewed, and talking to the brontosaur as she wept.

“Thank you,” she said. “Thank you, Eva. You’ve never deserted me.”

The brontosaur chuckled. “I’m not Eva, Naomi. I’m you. Shall I scratch your back?”

It lifted its head and put the tip of its snout between the girl’s shoulder blades. It angled its head back and forth, scraping jaw leather soothingly across Naomi’s spine. Its ancient green eyes were shut.

“Do you remember,” Naomi asked it, “how you carried me on your back, that day when I was the old woman? That day when I told Eva we’d be rescued? That the angels would come for us and take us away. Do you remember?”

Naomi dozed off. It was almost too perfect to be happening.

Now I had her completely off guard. I could finish her off whenever I felt like it.

Somewhere nearby, behind an imaginary desk in an imaginary office, a robot clutched a music box to its chest and wept. Salt tears coursed down the robot’s black leather face, fell, and splashed on my side panel.

Eva put her hands on his shoulders. She was standing behind his chair, in a red silk dress and a string of pearls.

“You’re crying,” she said. Alex turned his head.

“How did you get here?” he asked her.

“If a machine can weep,” said Eva, “anything is possible.”

They gazed at one another in silence for a time.

“I suppose you know what we have to do,” said Eva.

“I know,” said Alex, “I’m just scared. What if we can’t get out again?”

“Well, we can’t leave her in there.”

“No, we can’t do that.”

Alex put a glove on Eva’s arm. “Is anything possible?” he asked her.

“Anything,” she told him.

Alex set me back on the desk and stood up. Then he climbed onto the desk and stood on it beside me. He offered a glove to Eva, and she climbed up beside him.

The office began to expand. Or Eva and Alex began to shrink, it was hard to tell. Arm in arm they walked across the desk toward me. The desk top was as big as a basketball court when they reached me, and I was the size of a desk. They stood at either end of me and raised my lid.

“I can’t believe we’re doing this,” said Alex. “It’s suicide. We must be crazy.”

“We are crazy,” said Eva. “And it’s a lucky thing for that girl.”

The two of them climbed onto the upper edge of my front panel and balanced there like a couple of paper dolls, holding hands. Then they jumped inside me. And I’m deeper than I look.

My digital display flipped from 01 to 03. What patsies they all were. Fucking with their minds was simplicity itself.

Dr. Mazer lifted his blurry head and watched the monitor screens.

Alex and Eva fell from the belly of a cloud and went plummeting through a sky of blue. They held onto one another’s wrists at first. Then the gale-force winds tore them apart. Eva spun from the robot’s grasp.

Alex felt himself turning into an army jeep with only minimal computer hardware. He flipped end over end as he fell, his forward optics doggedly reporting: earth, sky, earth, sky. . .

You’ve still got a transmission, he told himself. Throw it in reverse. You aren’t happy here? Back up, idiot. Back up.

Alex shifted into reverse.

Naomi woke up half buried in sand. She struggled to her feet. The wind hissed like a pit full of snakes. Blowing sand obscured the sky.

The brontosaur was still lying in a circle around her. But the wind was so bad that Naomi could hardly stay standing. It tripped up her feet, pushed at her back, and slammed her against the base of the brontosaur’s neck. Naomi crept into a hollow between the beast’s neck and its shoulder. The sand had partly buried it while it slept. Its forefeet were buried deep. Naomi thought she’d better wake it up while it could still pull itself free. She edged along its neck toward its head, using the neck for a windbreak, using the rutted leather for handholds.

Halfway there Naomi came to a place where the neck slipped under the sand and vanished. That explained why the brontosaur wasn’t breathing. It had suffocated, and Naomi couldn’t even close its eyes for it.

Oh dear, she thought. This means I really am dead.

The wind shrieked with laughter.

Alex backed up all the way to the Great War, the Flanders campaign. He was a field ambulance driving across mud and barbed wire, through cordite smoke and mustard gas, for months on end. A place of rust and mildew. Always slipping into a trench or a latrine. Night skies like hell on earth.

He was constantly dragging himself to and fro across the landscape, transporting some filthy blood-oozing human wreckage which would be crippled or suffering or insensible or dead or pissing its pants. Broken men. Unreal.

Don’t stop now, Alex told himself. Don’t stop here! Keep going. Throw it into Drive. Prick caught in a meat grinder? Ignore it! Tongue in a power socket? Blood on your hands? Forget it! Shift the gear box. I don’t have to be here.

And then, quite suddenly, he wasn’t there anymore.

Because that’s how the game worked. I hadn’t any time left for changing the rules.

A belt of asteroids orbited a nameless star, out on the rim of the galaxy. Suspended within the belt, slowly rotating, there hung a peculiar asteroid made of frozen carbon compounds with a mass of several tons. Nowhere on this asteroid could you have found a single grain of sand. The asteroid was a gigantic frozen child, her limbs drawn into a ball, her yellow hair sticking out at all angles like wires. Her skin was fair. Her eyes were closed. She slowly spun in her golden nakedness, dead as dust.

A dove was tangled in the frosty forest of Naomi’s hair. Also hidden there was the corpse of a little black flea. The cold of interstellar vacuum had turned them into ice sculptures like the girl.

Even if kindly aliens had happened upon the frozen girl and thawed her out, she would still have been dead. Only in dreams can children return from the realms of zero Kelvin. In the real world, ice crystals rupture the membranes of all your cells. In real life, dead girls stay dead.

Yet however often Naomi reminded herself of these things, thoughts persisted in crossing her dead mind.

I’m Naomi, she thought to herself. But that dove and that flea, those aren’t really Eva and Alex. They’re just more of Dr. Mazer’s tricks. I’m alone in here now, and he’s bullying me.

Poor deluded girl. It wasn’t Mazer anymore. It was me. Mazer was in no condition to be playing any tricks. Whereas I was quite capable of playing mean tricks without help from anybody.

Naomi was also wrong about being alone. Alex and Eva were coming to her rescue. But they’d have to find her first. And I was quite a big place, once you got inside me.

Alex found the desert of white sand. It was covered with snow. Snow stood inches deep on the lobes of the prickly pear and the arms of the saguaros. Soft bluish snowflakes drifted down.

Alex was a mechanical millipede—jet black and sleek and as long as a bullet train. Traveling overland, his velocity was pushing toward Mach One. He streaked across the wintry plain. His legs were steel blurs that oared at the sand beneath the snow. Inside his jointed segments were nuclear reactors, steam turbines, Tesla converters, and radar gear.

Inside the millipede’s head was the cockpit where Dr. Mazer sat. Dr. Mazer sat in his pilot’s chair and steered Alex over the snowy sands. Dr. Mazer had invented the millipede. Dr. Mazer was an eminent genius.

Dr. Mazer was a genius and yet. . . He had allowed his conveyance to go completely off course. How could a genius commit such a blunder? The same way anyone does. His attention wandered. Gradually, with the best of intentions, one mistake led to another. But the doctor wouldn’t admit to his error.

These things happen, even to angels. You’re crossing a featureless waste. You lose your bearings. Eventually you’re all turned around.

I’m not a steel millipede, Alex told himself. Mazer isn’t steering me. Forget all that. I’m looking for Naomi. Naomi isn’t here.

Then neither was Alex.

The sky was wide and windy and white as paper. It blew Naomi thither and yon. It wouldn’t let her feet touch the ground.

Three elephants trudged through the oat grass, shoulder to shoulder. They wore three leather harnesses connected by a wooden yoke. The yoke dragged a cart that was heaped with hay.

The elephants could hear the wheels of the cart turning, the axles creaking. They could feel the sun on their backs and smell the sweet green hay on the cart. But they couldn’t see the prairie of oat grass because none of them had eyes. The three elephants had been blinded at birth, which was supposed to improve their tempers.

Their bellies were empty, and their mouths were dry. But they knew better than to bellow in complaint. They knew what that got them. A whipping from the cart driver.

Sometimes one of the elephants would slump in her harness and pass out. When that happened, the other two would halt and wait for the driver to descend from the cart. He would push a medicinal powder into the fallen elephant’s mouth, and soon she would revive.

On an especially bad day, one of the elephants might die. When that happened, the driver would press a magical charm against the fallen elephant’s head and sing a special song that restored her to life. Each of the elephants had gotten her turn at being dead. Being dead was pleasant. But it didn’t change anything.

The music box was as deep as a well. It dragged Eva down. She fell through a storm cloud and lost her grip on Alex. Without the robot, she fell more slowly.

Eva drifted down from the dead white sky like a snowflake, quite alone. She hovered over a field of winter wheat. A red dirt road crossed the frosty field. Eva followed the road. She came to a tall dilapidated farmhouse.

Eva slipped into the house through an attic window and stood on the attic floor in the dimness and chill. Dust motes tumbled in the diagonal shafts of sunlight that fell among the steamer trunks and the piles of cardboard boxes.

Eva couldn’t imagine why she’d come here. This wasn’t the place to look for Naomi. Eva should find the desert of white sand, if it hadn’t fallen apart already. But she was feeling tired and confused.

Then something red-on-white caught her eye. It was a toy truck on the sill of a dormer window. A little tin toy painted white, lying on its side. Eva moved to the window and set the truck on its little rubber wheels. It was an ambulance with red cross emblems on its sides. Eva pushed it along the window sill. The rubber wheels turned a noisemaker. The truck made a noise like a siren.

This is something Alex would like, thought Eva. But Alex isn’t here, and neither is Naomi. Why did I come here?

Then she saw the leg of a rag doll hanging from a cardboard box. She pulled the doll out. It had a dress of red velvet and white lace trim. Its hair was long and black, and its big glass eyes were brown. Eva took it to an old oak rocking chair, cleared off the seat, and sat down with the doll in her lap. She combed its hair with her long red fingernails. She hummed a lullaby.

This doll had no significance. It was hollow like the little tin ambulance. This house didn’t even exist. The music box was trying to distract her.

But perhaps, Eva told herself, perhaps if I just rest here, perhaps Naomi will come to me.

Eva’s head dropped forward. Her hands hung limp on the armrests of the chair. The doll slumped from her lap to the attic floor.

Naomi was a circus elephant in the glare of the spotlights, marching in a circle on the sawdust.

She wore a band of sequins around her head—silver and electric green and turquoise. From the band exfoliated a gorgeous headdress of ostrich plumes. Naomi marched in a circle with her sisters, trotting to the music of the brass band. The spotlights made their finery sparkle.

After their part of the show, Naomi and her sisters were led from the tent by a clown with mechanical hands. He took them to a pool of mud that the little Mexican girl had made for them to wallow in. This was their reward after a performance.

All the elephants were fond of the little Mexican girl who traveled with the circus. She smelled salty, and she always wore the same filthy green coveralls. Her eyes were brown. Her teeth were yellow. Her skin was the color of copper. Her job was to shovel out the elephants’ stalls, to change their straw, and to hose them down before they rode the circus train.

I’m not getting on any train, Naomi told herself. I’m not an elephant in the first place. I need to find Eva.

Eva sneezed and woke up in a rocking chair. She’d nodded off. She was still in the attic, and something was bumping around among the boxes.

An assemblage of sculpted yellow plastic peeked out from behind the base of a lamp. It was a model kit of the skeleton of a brontosaur. It trotted toward Eva’s chair. Its plastic feet pattered across the dusty floorboards.

A reedy shrunken voice called up. “Eva? Eva? Is that you? It’s me. Where did you go? I’ve been looking for you.”

The model kit craned its neck and looked at itself. “What is this thing I’m in?”

Eva laughed. It really was Naomi. Naomi had found her.

So the next thing to do was to locate Alex.

What a family, Eva thought to herself. Everything has to be done the long way around.

Before setting off on her mission against Mazer, Spiker the Assassin made a side trip to Chicago.

She drove her Mercedes south along Lake Shore Drive. There were sailboats on Lake Michigan. Spiker liked to visit the Museum of Science and Industry before a mission. The sky was blue, and the year was 1951.

Spiker turned off the drive and parked her Mercedes. She strode toward the wide white steps that climbed to the museum’s portico. She took the steps two at a time, a lean oriental woman in a charcoal gray business suit. Her wiry gray hair was cut close to her scalp.

Spiker strolled the Hall of Chemistry, gazing at back-lit models of molecules like tinker toys and reviewing in her mind the preparations she’d made for entering Mazer’s castle. First among these preparations had been telling Weaver in no uncertain terms to keep herself and her messenger boy out of Spiker’s way. When Spiker did a job, she didn’t tolerate interference. She went in. She did the job. She got out. No muddle, no waiting—that was Spiker.

But before a mission she would visit her favorite exhibit at the museum. It settled her mind.

On the marble landings of a spiral staircase in the east wing stood a series of display cabinets, an anatomy exhibit in exile. Neither the Hall of Medicine nor the Hall of Anthropology would claim it. Inside the cases, pressed between plates of glass and labeled with descriptive plaques, were pickled inch-thick slices of a man and a woman. The woman had been sliced vertically, back to front. The man had been sliced longitudinally—his arms and legs and trunk, all neatly cross-sectioned. The arteries had been filled with red latex, the veins with blue latex. The major organs were stained for ease in identification.

In life the man and woman had been a married couple who willed their bodies to Science. This was what Science had done with them. Spiker stood beside one of the cases with her hands in the pockets of her suit jacket and admired the dead flesh.

Flesh and bone. Four billion years in the brewing. The human matrix.

Cold cuts.

Spiker rocked on her heels and smiled.

Somewhere in the afterlife was Dr. Mazer’s castle. Within the moat, inside the granite walls, were the facilities of Dr. Mazer’s Home For Disturbed Souls. Within the home was the doctor’s office. In the office was a desk, and on the desk was a music box.

Deep within the stuttering clockwork of the music box was an artificial Australia. Under an angry sun lay a plain of sandy red earth. Under a eucalyptus tree lay a rotting log. Under the bark of the log, a red ant and an ant larva clung together in mortal fear. The ant was Eva, and the soft white grub was Naomi.

The attic had become an anthill, and then the anthill had been invaded by army ants—hordes of them with jaws like razors. Eva had snatched Naomi from the nursery gallery and fled through an escape tunnel into the night. She’d taken shelter in the log.

After daybreak she found a bark fungus that she could chew up and feed to the larva. She felt that she should search for other survivors of her hill. But the sun would kill Naomi in minutes. Even here the child was desiccating horribly.

Eva left Naomi in the crevice under the bark and explored the baked earth around the log. She found the mouth of a strange-smelling shaft that ran straight down into the cool damp subsoil. Feeling more hopeful, she retrieved the wilting grub and carried it in her mandibles down the shaft into the darkness. In her haste she neglected to leave a scent trail. Soon she was hopelessly lost in a tangled maze of tunnels.

Then she came across a cast-off husk of a termite. She was lost in a termite mound. She could expect no mercy when the termites found her. They would bite her to bits, her and her precious dying larva.

And we’ve come so far! Eva groaned over Naomi. It’s not fair.

Naomi didn’t answer. But Naomi wasn’t dead yet, and she was puzzling over a few things. For instance, was she really an ant larva? It seemed to Naomi that only recently she’d been a plastic model kit. And her mother had recently been a woman. An odd quality in an ant, if you thought about it. It seemed evident to Naomi that someone was pushing them around without showing himself. Bullying people from hiding was dirty pool in Naomi’s opinion.

Hiding. The little ingrate. I wasn’t hiding. I was everywhere she looked. Did she think it was easy—throwing up dioramas of Australia at a moment’s notice? As if I had nothing better to do.

Naomi began patiently changing the terms of the ant story. First: They weren’t in termite tunnels. They were in a humble peasant’s cottage surrounded by the Black Forest. Secondly Eva wasn’t an ant. She was a woodcutter’s wife. And thirdly—

I tried to stop her. But by then I was simply too weak.

A robot in a chauffeur’s jacket, with a face of black leather and eyes of red glass, was hiking great distances across a nondescript waste of gravel and sagebrush. The sun was punishing. The gravel was frying his boot soles.

Alex stopped, shaded his eyes with one glove, and scanned the horizon. He sighted a speck of green and walked toward it. He felt solitary and isolated. Never before had he walked around the music box yet felt so far from his wife and his adopted daughter.

The box, he conjectured, must really be losing it.

He came at last to a giant baobab tree that stood in the middle of nowhere, its leaves rattling in the sirocco, its trunks wavering in the heat. The shade under the tree was inviting. But as Alex drew nearer, the tree growled at him.

Do your worst, he thought.

Roots erupted from the gravel. They reached up, jerking and swaying. They reared to strike like crooked wooden cobras. The gravel was boiling with them all around Alex. They ignored him. They coiled like creepers up the trunks of the baobab. Alex backed away and tried to make sense of what he was seeing.

The branches twisted in and lashed at the roots. The roots caught at the branches with serpentine loops and tried to drag the branches down. The branches wrestled with the roots and ripped some of them out of the ground. Leaves fell free and blew away. The roots grew mouths with teeth of thorns and beards of white rootlets. They snapped and chewed at the branches. The branches grew wooden claws and wooden spurs and slashed at the hissing mouths. The tree was at war with itself.

The tree collapsed into a squeaking thrashing heap. It had wrecked itself.

A bolt of violet lightning streaked down from the cloudless sky. It struck the fallen tree. The earth heaved beneath Alex’s boots. He fell flat on the gravel with his arms over his head. Splinters showered down.

When he raised his head, the tree was gone. Only a smoking hole in the ground remained. A nursery song warbled at the back of Alex’s mind.

There’s a hole at the bottom of the sea. A hole at the bottom of the sea. There’s a branch on the stump in the hole at the bottom of the sea.

Alex got up onto his boots and crossed the broken terrain to the rim of the crater. He got down on his gloves and knees at the crater’s rim. He gazed into the hole. There was something black and lustrous, like a seed, that rested on a bed of glowing coals.

That seed is my core assembly, thought Alex. That’s my power source. And it’s madness. Concentrated madness. Mazer stole it from me. But now I can take it back.

Alex hung one arm into the crater and reached toward the bed of coals.

What if it’s all right that I’m mad? What if that’s not my problem? What if my problem is that I don’t let myself be mad? That I’m forever and always tearing chunks out of myself, trying to root out the madness.

Alex shoved his arm into the coals. His glove closed around the black seed. Glove leather sizzled. He yanked his arm free and stood up. He opened his fist and looked at the seed, no bigger than a plum pit, resting on his palm.

He clapped his glove against his face. He pushed the seed against the place where a man would have had a mouth. The seed burned a hole in his face. He pushed the seed through the hole it had burned. He swallowed it, felt it slide down his neck and lodge in his chest.

Madness, he thought. This is how it tastes.

Alex turned and walked back the way he’d come. He walked faster and faster. He had to find Eva and Naomi. He began to run.

There’s a bird on the egg in the nest on the twig on the branch on the stump in the hole at the bottom of the sea.

I couldn’t hold him there. I wasn’t myself. I was rruunnnniinngg ddddoooowwwwnnnn.

Once upon a time, deep inside a music box, in the middle of the Black Forest, there stood the humble cottage of a woodcutter.

The roof of the cottage was moss and thatch, and the walls were sod and elephant bones. On a stormy autumn morning, some years previous, the woodcutter had set forth into the woods with his ax on his shoulder and hadn’t been seen since. His wife and daughter still lived in the cottage, awaiting his return. The daughter’s hair was yellow as wheat and as fine as spun flax. She had only recently been an ant larva, but never mind about that.

There came a bright and windy morning in April. The woodcutter’s wife was in back of the cottage, weeding her carrots. The daughter was in the goat pen, playing with the goats. Someone knocked at the door of the cottage.

The daughter crept along the side of the cottage and peeked around a corner at the caller. A tall man in a ragged black cloak and muddy boots stood at the door. A leather patch covered one of his eyes, and one of his shirtsleeves hung empty.

The girl’s mother joined her and peered over her shoulder.

“Who are you?” the woodcutter’s wife demanded of the crippled stranger.

But the girl rushed toward the man with a shout of delight. The woodcutter, for his part, took his daughter in his arms, what remained of them, and swept the girl off her feet. He whirled her around him in the April wind and the dancing sunlight.

“Papa! Papa!” shouted the girl.

“I’m sorry! I’m sorry!” wept her father.

“What are you sorry for?” asked his wife.

Alex scratched his head. “I don’t remember,” he said. “I’m just sorry.”

Somewhere on the coast of India in 1891, a grove of banyan trees stood at the mouth of a small river. High in the foliage, the branches supported a circular platform of bamboo, which was the floor of Reverend Weaver’s tree house. The house had a roof of silk and no walls at all. The furniture was woven of wicker. Several parrots perched on nearby branches and watched the reverend closely.

Weaver sat cross-legged on a tatami mat. In front of her, tilted on its side, was a big ceramic jug full of wet clay. The glaze on the jug was green, and the raw clay was red. Beside her was a concave slab of wood. One of Weaver’s bare arms was deep inside the jug, finding a fistful of clay. She extracted the clay, rolled it between her palms, and set it on the slab of wood. Then a second lump, a little smaller. And then a half a lump for Naomi.

As Weaver molded the lumps into human shapes, she considered her options for the relocation of the three souls. They should remain a family, that was clear. Like Mazer and Wing and herself, they couldn’t stay out of each other’s hair for long. Triplets? No, not babies. Not in a case such as this. A man, a woman, and a child then. But what time frame was appropriate? Where would they feel at home?

1973, Weaver decided, poking a crease into Alex’s forehead with her thumb nail. Arizona. The Tucson bus station. That could work.

The sun was low in the west. Already the insects were shrilling.

Spiker walked rapidly across the green felt of a boundless pool table. She wore a black silk tunic, black trousers, and black slippers. On her back she wore a bow and a quiver of arrows. From her belt hung the sheaths of two samurai swords.

A couple of pool balls as big as houses rolled out of nowhere and tried to crush her. They spun like tops and skipped abruptly to and fro.

Spiker dashed away from them, turned, and set two arrows in her bow. Diamond arrow points pierced the spheres. Dangling from the arrows were concussion grenades. As the balls rolled toward her, they crushed the arrows and grenades beneath them. The grenades detonated twin bursts of angelic light. Shards of ivory fell like rain. Spiker turned aside from two blackened craters, sunk like blinded eyes into the green felt. She resumed her walk to Mazer’s castle.

Spiker had no patience for diversions.

Volcanic ash falls had covered Albuquerque, New Mexico, to a depth of three feet. High winds had raised a dust storm. On a street in one of the city’s abandoned business districts, a municipal garbage truck was immobilized. Around the truck, parking meters and traffic signs poked their heads from the drifts of ash, like steel mushrooms. Inside the cab of the truck, with the windows rolled up, were a man, a woman, and a child. I think I’ve told you their names.

“Well,” said Eva. “Here we are. Together again, on the inside of the box.”

“How will we get out, Papa?” asked Naomi, who was wearing her father’s leather jacket and shivering anyway.

“We’ll get down from this truck, and we’ll walk,” said Alex. “We’ll walk straight out of here.”

“Pardon me for bringing this up,” said Eva. “But the last time we tried that, it went on for centuries.”

Alex raised a grubby finger. “Ah!” he said. “But we’ve all recently been out of this place. That changes everything. Weaver got you out. Wing got her out. She got me out. And the box is half dead. We can do this. Naomi can ride on my shoulders.”

Eva sighed. “I’ll try anything. Let’s go.”

As soon as they climbed down from the truck, the truck was gone, along with the street. They found themselves standing on a vast plain of purple asphalt under a cloudy sky. They picked a direction and started walking.

Still wearing her swords, Spiker swam through the icy water at the bottom of Mazer’s moat. A dappling of green light fell from the water’s surface. In utter silence Spiker swam toward the foundation of the castle. She skimmed over the muck at the bottom of the channel.

She came to a wall of granite blocks and swam along the face of it. She found an oaken door with an iron ring bolted into it. Grasping the ring, she planted her feet against the granite and heaved the door open. She climbed through the doorway into a narrow chimney walled with red brick. Spiker released a mouthful of bubbles into the dark water. She climbed the chimney.

When her head broke through into air, she took a deep breath and went on climbing. Her fingers found another door—a small square iron door with a keyhole in it. Spiker set her eye to the hole and saw a glimmer of light. She pulled a lock pick from the collar of her tunic. Soon the door swung open.

Spiker stepped into a brightly lit hallway with walls of white plaster, a green carpet, and endless rows of steel doors. She stood in the middle of the hallway, dripping on the carpet. She listened to the stillness. She waited.

Up and down the hallway, as far as the eye could see, every door except one door swung slowly open.

Spiker drew her swords.

Behind the one door that stayed closed, a music box stood on a desk, with half its clockwork guts spilled onto the desk top.

At the bottom of the box was a plain of purple asphalt that wavered at the edges. Three travelers were hiking across the plain. Walking ahead of the other two was a wiry young man with dark hair and worried eyes and a week’s beard. His feet were bare. His only clothing was a ripped pair of khaki pants.

Off to one side walked a Hispanic woman in the rags of a red silk dress.

Trailing behind these two walked a girl child, fair-haired and naked and very dirty.

Their hair was wild and matted. Their skin was sunburned and peeling. They looked as if they’d been walking for years. They never spoke. They avoided one another’s eyes. Yet the three of them went on walking in the same direction.

They came to an hourglass that was as tall as a four-story building. It stood on the plain like a monument. In the bottom flask of the glass was a heap of white sand, taller than a man.

Stuck into the center of the heap was a wooden stake, smooth as driftwood. Impaled on the stake, one on top of another, were three skulls with eyes and tongues. Mosquitoes swarmed around them as they shouted insults at one another or squirmed on their fixtures, wishing they could bite. As the three travelers passed the hourglass, they could hear the skulls, just faintly, through the glass.

“Up yours!” “Fuck you!” “Choke on it!” “Make me!”

Naomi spat on the asphalt. They walked on and left the skulls on their stake, with white sand trickling over them and getting in their eyes. Three skulls without a brain between them.

The three travelers walked until the hourglass was lost in the distance. They came to a door standing in a frame with nothing around it. Off to one side of the door stood a pale pinkish blur with thick glasses and a bald head. The blur watched the three of them.

They drew closer together as they approached the door and the blur. Alex took hold of Eva’s hand. Naomi clung to the leg of Alex’s pants. The blur watched them and chewed its fingernails.

The door swung open on its hinges. Inside the frame was a black void.

“That’s Mazer, isn’t it?” said Naomi. “What’s the door?”

“It could be a trap,” said Eva.

“It’s a way out,” said Alex. “We put it there. He couldn’t stop us.”

Naomi hawked up another spit wad. “Why is he in here? I thought he never came in here.”

“He followed me in,” said Alex. “And why not? He’s my guardian angel. Just like Wing is yours. The only reason that you and your mother got dragged into this mess is because of me.”

“Shows how much you know,” said Eva. “Weaver told me that she and Mazer used to be married. They’re as tangled up as we are. What I can’t figure out is: Whose guardian angel is Spiker?”

“Who’s Spiker?” said Alex.

The door creaked on its hinges. The wind might slam it shut at any time.

Naomi spat at the shoes of the blur. “Let’s go,” she said. “Let’s fly.”

A winged ant, a white dove, and a model helicopter flew through the doorway into the dark. The door slammed shut.

The pale blur began to weep.

“It worked!” the blur rejoiced. “They’re cured! I knew it would work!”

In a tree house at the top of a grove of banyan trees, Weaver put the finishing touches on her three clay figures. Alex was missing a piece, and Naomi was built a bit crooked, but they’d like that. That was what they were used to. Weaver dipped her hands in a pot of water.

A parrot flew across the Indian Ocean toward the banyan grove. It joined the other parrots that perched on the lianas there. It gave a loud squawk.

Weaver looked up from her work. “They did?” she said. “That’s a surprise. It’s a good thing I’ve got some flesh ready for them.”

Weaver threw the clay figures out of the tree house. They fell into the lower boughs of the banyans and vanished.

“Tucson, Arizona!” she shouted to the banyan trees. “1973! The bus station!”

“No problem,” said the trees. “Consider it done.”

The merest hint of rainfall pattered down through the leaves.

Ensign Wing piloted a sail-powered catamaran across the surface of the Atlantic, sometime in the general vicinity of 2000 BC.

He stood at the stern of the main hull, barefoot in a white linen tunic, holding the tiller. The hulls were constructed from bundles of hollow reeds. The sails were the best hempen canvas.

Wing listened carefully to the rising and falling of the wind.

“Good for them,” he said. “Now that’s settled. Any word about Spiker and the old man?”

The wind made no reply.

The music box was still running, barely. My drum revolved. My brass knobs still plucked my copper tines. But no one was listening. No one cared. No one was left inside me. My indicator had dropped to 00.

The desert of white sand was empty. The three suns had burned out and left three purple holes in the smudged white sky. There were three sets of footprints in the sand. They came to the crest of a dune and ended, as if the three travelers had been swallowed by the sand or snatched into the sky.

I was tired of playing with them anyway. I had thought that I didn’t want to die alone. Now I just wanted to be left in peace, to disintegrate or to implode. Someone was making a racket in the hall. The blur was cowering in a corner of his office.

You can’t avoid anything, can you? And you can’t change anybody’s mind. And you can’t teach an old dog new tricks. You might as well accept the inevitable. You might as well hang yourself.

Don’t mind me. I’ve been murdered.

Do you want to know a secret? Eva was never a Mayan fish priestess. Naomi was never frozen in liquid nitrogen, never turned into a neoplasmic monster in a bowling alley. Alex never lost his hands. They never saw melting people or dying oceans. Crazy people tell lies. They live in worlds of their own.

PPPs we call those. Particularized Possible Planets. Mad people make them up all the time. But it’s dangerous. It’s too difficult for humans. That’s why they go mad. Only a machine can do it safely. That’s why Dr. Mazer invented me. Because a machine can’t go insane.

I’ll tell you another secret. There are no such things as storms. There are no such things as angels. There are no such things as music boxes.

Spiker stood with her swords drawn and dripped on the carpet. Up and down the hallway, infinite doors swung open, and demons of every description emerged.

There were cybernetic locust demons and arctic cannibal demons with fangs of bloody ice. There were demons in feathered capes, with Aztec war clubs and death angel mushrooms instead of heads. There were angry tumorous demons from Dr. Mazer’s personal nightmares—half human, half bronze-age siege towers bristling with spears. The hallway grew thick with them—chthonic demons, oceanic demons, microbial demons, demons from outer space. . . Spiker brandished her swords.

A scaly green toad demon leapt onto Spiker’s back and instantly went sailing through a wall. After that, Spiker seemed to be everywhere at once. Some of the demons found ninja stars buried in their throats. Others, Spiker disemboweled. The demons who were too stupid to fall down, she chopped limb from limb. Messy work, but she enjoyed it.

The locusts, she beat to pulp with a severed arm from one of the castle gargoyles. Finally only the arctic demons remained. Spiker screamed them to bits.

When the carnage was complete, she surveyed the piles of mangled corpses. She sheathed her swords and walked toward the door that remained shut. She kicked aside a gory head to clear her path. Spiker kicked down the door of Mazer’s office.

The room was dark. A fire built from file folders burned sullenly on the floor. The video monitors were smashed. Bits of glass from their picture tubes sparkled in the firelight.

Spiker moved into the room. The desk top was littered with drug capsules and pieces of clockwork that had spilled from my guts. I was playing Javanese gamelan music, slow and stately. A funeral dirge for myself.

A pale blur shrank deeper into its corner of the room. Spiker walked toward it.

I’ve cured them,” it whimpered. “I knew I could do it. But no one congratulates me. Instead they send you. It’s professional jealousy. That’s all it is. Tell me I’m wrong.”

“Mazer,” she said, “you’re so wrong, it isn’t funny anymore.”

Mazer stiffened with rage. “I don’t experiment,” he insisted. “I practice therapy here. Innovative therapy. You can’t test out psychological methodologies on rats, you know. It’s not an exact science.” Mazer threw up his hands. “Oh, what’s the use? That bitch Weaver has turned all of you against me.”

Spiker wiped some of the demon blood from her hands onto her tunic. She kept her eyes on the blur. She walked toward it again.

“You aren’t listening,” she told it.

The pale blur pressed itself flat against a wall and groaned in terror. An empty white lab coat fell to the floor. The coat twitched. Something was moving beneath it. A white rat dashed out from under the coat and sped along the baseboard. Spiker made a dive for the rat. She landed flat on the floor with the scruff of its neck nabbed between her fingers. The rat squirmed and hissed. Spiker lifted it off the floor and let it dangle, limp and trembling.

“Snap out of it,” said Spiker. “Be a man.”

The rat sneezed. All at once it was a man—a pale flabby naked old man with smears of feces down his thighs. Spiker let go of his neck. He leaned against the wall and complained incoherently.

Spiker leaned against the wall beside him. Mazer cringed. Spiker put one arm around his back and gently but firmly levered him away from the wall. Walking at her side, he fell into a muttering daze. His head rolled against her shoulder.

Spiker approached a particular section of the office wall and gave the plaster a swift kick. A rectangular door flew open. Spiker picked Mazer up in her arms and carried him into his bedroom.

She set the old man on his bed. She went into his bathroom and returned with a roll of paper towels. She rolled Mazer onto his stomach and wiped the crap from his legs and his crack.

She pulled the bedspread out from under him, then covered him with it. She wet a washcloth and swabbed the sweat from his face. Without his glasses, his eyes looked small and weak. He began to tremble. Spiker put another blanket on the bed and felt his forehead.

She fetched a glass of water and two white tablets. She propped Mazer up on a bolster and put the tablets into his mouth. She held the glass while he drank. He leaned toward her, squinting at her face.

“Spiker?” he said.

“That’s right. You need a long rest, Mazer. And I’m here to see that you get it.”

“But. . . Aren’t you going to kill me?”

Spiker sat down in an armchair. She drew a small black dagger from her tunic and started cleaning the pond scum and demon blood and angel shit from under her fingernails.

“I may,” she said. “I’m thinking about it.”

A piercing siren issued from my guts, a warning of my imminent detonation. I was giving the doctor my notice. I was ready to play my death scene.

The siren brought Spiker back from the bedroom. Crimson smoke was pouring out of me, and electrical discharges were punching holes in the ceiling. Spiker snatched me up and ran into the corridor. She pitched me down the length of the hallway. I sailed over the wreckage of a thousand demons, turning end over end.

“Hazard area!” I cried aloud. “Fire in the hole! Stochastic overload! Tantrum coils breached! Man the lifeboats! Abandon box!”

Unfortunately I was speaking too rapidly for the human ear. It all came out as a pitiful squeak—like the chirp of a squashed cicada or the croak of a crushed sucker fish.

Before I could hit the carpet, I imploded in a flash of deafening rainbow light. And that was the end of me.

No, it wasn’t the end of me. I lied. I do that. I merely downloaded my guidance system to another of Mazer’s music boxes. He kept a whole collection of us tucked away in one of the store rooms.

It’s very difficult to die in the afterlife.

Can I tell you a Zen koan before I go? It’s a real killer.

Question: You can’t do it this way. You can’t do it the other way. Both ways are impossible. How can you do it?

Answer: I wonder what it’s like over there.

On a warm August evening in 1973, at the Greyhound terminal in Tucson, a Mexican woman in a green dress sat with her daughter and waited for the night bus north. The woman’s name was Eva, and her daughter was called Amy.

Amy sat on a chair of orange plastic, kicking the bottom of it with one heel. It was bolted to the floor. She asked her mother for quarters for the pop machine. She fed the coins into the tall metal box, and it spat out a can of ginger ale.

A man sitting behind them said, “Here is your refreshment, young lady,” in a mechanical-sounding voice. He was pretending to be the vending machine. He was making a joke for Amy.

Amy was tickled by the joke. She started talking to the man in English, very fast. Her speech was a little slurred as a result of an infantile brain fever.

Alex listened to Amy and nodded his head, understanding about half of what she said. Alex had spent the day riding a bus west from Amarillo, Texas. He was on his way to Phoenix, for lack of a better plan, to look for work as an auto mechanic. He wore blue jeans and a leather jacket. His right arm ended at the wrist in a stump. The stump fascinated Amy. She tried very hard not to stare.

After Amy had pestered the man for a minute or more, Eva turned from her newspaper and had a look at him. The man was watching her. He smiled tentatively in her direction, while Amy rattled on. He had a nice face, a lived-in face, so Eva smiled back at him just faintly.

The sun set on Tucson. The evening wore on. The streets outside the terminal grew dark. The ceiling lights came on. The plate glass windows turned into mirrors. The hands of the wall clock said eight. The night bus to Phoenix was late.

Amy fell asleep in a chair beside Eva’s, with her head under her mother’s arm. Her mouth hung open. Her face glowed with an inner light. She was her mother’s precious treasure, impossible to protect. She filled Eva’s heart with a fierce and jealous love.

Alex stood beside Eva’s chair. “May I sit with you?” he asked.

And since he’d been kind to Amy and kept her amused, Eva nodded. And so he sat down.

The two of them began to talk. The man had grown up in Chicago. Had recently lost a truck maintenance job in Texas. And so on. Eva rubbed her eyes. She felt as though she’d been awake for four days straight.

Eva was on her way to Flagstaff, where a woman she knew had offered her a job cleaning motel rooms. Alex wondered how the work situation for mechanics was in Flagstaff. People said that it was cooler than Phoenix.

The man draped one arm across the back of Eva’s chair. Aha! Just as she’d expected. He was trying to make time with her.

Well. . . Eva wouldn’t raise a stink. The missing hand was sort of disgusting, but he was polite, and Amy liked him. Eva let him leave his arm where it was. He was probably just starved for human contact. Eva could forgive a guy for being lonely.

Amy stirred in her sleep. She was dreaming of a white balloon that floated in the middle of nowhere.

Three ladybugs lived on the rubber of the white balloon. One day they decided to have a race. They would race around the balloon, and the finish line would be the starting line.

Someone said Go, and off they raced. But halfway around the balloon, a little girl ladybug remembered that they had forgotten to draw a starting line for a finish line. So there was no way to win the race.

So the three ladybugs forgot about the race and flew away. Then the balloon popped.

Amy nestled against her mother’s side and breathed a sigh of contentment.

Nothing had been decided, but everything was solved. Nothing had been repaired, but everything was fixed. Nothing had been cured. Yet everything was healed.

Outside the terminal, it started to rain.
